to be a non-constant renormalization function. It is important to our argument that this is not the case – in fact, later on, we will show that
\gamma^{\sigma}_{T}
agrees with the mass renormalization of the model on the torus (see
[
BG20
]
) up to a finite correction. It can be shown that they diverge at rates

|\gamma_{T}^{\sigma}|\asymp\log T,\,|\delta_{T}^{\sigma}|\asymp T,

(3.18)

where
\asymp
means bounded from above and below as
T\rightarrow\infty
.

Remark 3.5
.

The presence of
C^{M^{\sigma,{\rm per}}}
in the second term of the energy renormalization
\delta^{\sigma}_{T}
is to ensure that, given boundary conditions on the left
\varphi^{0}_{-}=\varphi^{0,-}_{-}
, the middle
\varphi^{0}=\varphi^{0,-}_{+}=\varphi^{0,+}_{-}
, and the right
\varphi^{0}_{+}=\varphi^{0,+}_{+}
,

\delta_{T}(\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0}_{+})=\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\delta^{\sigma}_{T}+\delta^{\sigma,\partial}_{T}(\varphi_{-}^{0,\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{0,\sigma})-3\cdot 4\int_{M^{\sigma}}\int_{M^{\sigma}}C^{B}_{T}(x,y\mid\varphi^{0},\varphi^{0})^{4}dxdy.

(3.19)

An analogous statement holds for the torus.

Let
T\geq 0
. We define the regularized potential, which naturally decomposes into bulk and boundary-bulk contributions. The
bulk regularized potential
is the map
V_{T}^{\sigma}
given by

V_{T}^{\sigma}(\varphi):=\int_{M^{\sigma}}\llbracket\varphi_{T}^{4}\rrbracket(x)-\gamma_{T}^{\sigma}\llbracket\varphi_{T}^{2}\rrbracket(x)dx-\delta_{T}^{\sigma},\qquad\forall\varphi\in S^{\prime}(M^{\sigma}).

(3.20)

We emphasize that the Wick powers of
\varphi_{T}
above are formally defined by the
same
expressions as for the Gaussian case, i.e. with the same covariance
C^{M^{\sigma}}_{T}
appearing in the constants. We will only use them when the argument is
\varphi\sim\mu^{\sigma}
. Given
\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+}\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, the
boundary-bulk regularized potential
is the map
V^{\sigma,\partial}_{T}(\cdot\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})
defined for every
\varphi\in S^{\prime}(M^{\sigma})
by

\displaystyle V_{T}^{\sigma}(\varphi\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})

\displaystyle=\int_{M^{\sigma}}4\llbracket\varphi_{T}^{3}\rrbracket H^{\sigma}(\varphi^{\sigma}_{+},\varphi^{\sigma}_{-})_{T}+6\llbracket\varphi_{T}^{2}\rrbracket\llbracket H^{\sigma}(\varphi_{+}^{\sigma},\varphi^{\sigma}_{-})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket

(3.21)

\displaystyle\qquad+4\varphi_{T}\llbracket H^{\sigma}(\varphi^{\sigma}_{+},\varphi^{\sigma}_{-})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket-2\gamma_{T}^{\sigma}\varphi_{T}H^{\sigma}(\varphi^{\sigma}_{+},\varphi^{\sigma}_{-})

(3.22)

\displaystyle\qquad-\gamma^{\sigma}_{T}\llbracket H^{\sigma}(\varphi_{+}^{\sigma},\varphi_{-}^{\sigma})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket\,dx-\delta_{T}^{\sigma,\partial},

(3.23)

where we recall the definition of Wick powers of harmonic extensions of the boundary fields from the preceding subsection.

Remark 3.6
.

It is natural to ask why the term
\int_{M^{\sigma}}\llbracket H^{\sigma}(\varphi_{+}^{\sigma},\varphi^{\sigma}_{-})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket\,dx
is not included in the boundary-bulk regularized potential above. On the one hand, since these potentials are defined almost surely in the boundary condition, this term is "deterministic" and hence will be cancelled by the normalization constant. However, the more severe issue arises when considering the gluing formula. For boundary fields sampled according to
\mu^{0}
, we will
not
be able to control this term and it cannot be cancelled in a trivial fashion (e.g. by a further constant renormalization) since we will integrate over the boundary field for the gluing property and Markov property. As we shall see, handling it necessitates a changing the boundary measure in a singular way.

With the same notation as above, we define the
regularized potential

V_{T}^{\sigma}(\cdot\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})
to be the sum of the bulk and boundary-bulk potentials, i.e.

V_{T}^{\sigma}(\varphi\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})=V_{T}^{\sigma}(\varphi)+V^{\sigma,\partial}_{T}(\varphi\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+}),\qquad\forall\varphi\in S^{\prime}(M^{\sigma}).

(3.24)

Definition 3.7
.

The approximate
\varphi^{4}_{3}
measure on
M^{\sigma}
with pair of boundary conditions
\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+}\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is the probability measure
\nu_{T}^{\sigma}(\cdot\mid\varphi_{-}^{0,\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{0,\sigma})
with Radon-Nikodym derivative

\nu_{T}^{\sigma}(d\varphi\mid\varphi_{-}^{0,\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{0,\sigma})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{\sigma}(0\mid\varphi_{-}^{0,\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{0,\sigma})}\exp(-V_{T}^{\sigma}(\varphi\mid\varphi_{-}^{0,\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{0,\sigma}))\mu^{\sigma}(d\varphi),

(3.25)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{\sigma}(0\mid\varphi_{-}^{0,\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{0,\sigma})
is a normalization constant. For every
f\in C^{\infty}(M^{\sigma})
, the unnormalized Laplace transform of
f
under this measure is the quantity

\mathcal{Z}^{\sigma}_{T}(f\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-}):=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\sigma}}\left[\exp\left(\langle f,\varphi\rangle_{M^{\sigma}}-V_{T}^{\sigma}(\varphi\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})\right)\right],\qquad\forall f\in C^{\infty}(M^{\sigma}),

(3.26)

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{M^{\sigma}}
denotes the
L^{2}(M^{\sigma})
inner product. Note that taking
f=0
above indeed yields the normalization constant
\mathcal{Z}^{\sigma}_{T}(0\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})
.

We now restate a more precise version of Theorem
1.1
on the construction of
\varphi^{4}_{3}
with admissible boundary conditions.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
\sigma\in\{\emptyset,+,-\}
and let
(\varphi^{0,\sigma_{-}},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})
be independent admissible boundary conditions. Then almost surely in the boundary conditions, the following statements hold.

(i)

For every
f\in C^{\infty}(M^{\sigma})
, there exists a limiting unnormalized Laplace transform
\mathcal{Z}^{\sigma}(f\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})>0
such that

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{\sigma}(f\mid\varphi^{\sigma,0}_{-},\varphi^{\sigma,0}_{+})=\mathcal{Z}^{\sigma}(f\mid\varphi^{\sigma,0}_{-},\varphi^{\sigma,0}_{+}).

(3.27)

(ii)

There exists a probability measure
\nu^{\sigma}(\cdot\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})
on
S^{\prime}(M^{\sigma})
such that
\nu^{\sigma}_{T}(\cdot\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})
converges weakly to
\nu^{\sigma}(\cdot\mid\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{0,\sigma}_{+})
as
T\rightarrow\infty
.

(iii)

For every
f\in C^{\infty}(M^{\sigma})
, we have that
-\log\mathcal{Z}^{\sigma}(f\mid\varphi^{\sigma,0}_{-},\varphi^{\sigma,0}_{+})
is given by an explicit variational problem.

We will call the probability measure
\nu^{\sigma}(\cdot\mid\varphi_{-}^{\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{\sigma})
constructed in Theorem
3.8
the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
measure on
M^{\sigma}
with random boundary conditions
(\varphi_{-}^{0,\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{0,\sigma})
.

Let us remark that Theorem
1.1
follows from Theorem
3.8
. Parts (i) and (iii) of Theorem
3.8
are special cases of Theorem
3.18
below, and so we defer a discussion of the proof to after the latter theorem is stated. Let us comment, however, that (ii) is a standard consequence of (i).

Remark 3.9
.

We stress that the inclusion of the energy renormalization
\delta_{T}^{\sigma}
in the regularized potential is simply to make the unnormalized Laplace transforms converge. It does not play an intrinsic role in constructing the measure thanks to the normalization constant. However, on the other hand there is a degeneracy in the mass renormalization. One could add e.g. a smooth function to
\gamma^{\sigma}_{T}
that is bounded as
T\rightarrow\infty
and this will yield an equivalent measure to the one constructed above.

Remark 3.10
.

The variational characterization of the unnormalized Laplace transform as stated in Theorems
1.1
and part (iii) of Theorem
3.8
is intentionally vague at this point since stating it will require some additional technical definitions. See Theorem
3.18
below. The relevance of the (limiting) variational problem is that we will use it to establish full convergence of the regularized measures, as opposed to just tightness. See also
[
BG20
]
where this characterization was first establish for
\varphi^{4}_{3}
models on the torus.

3.3
Identification of the boundary measure

In order to identify the correct boundary law of
\varphi^{4}_{3}
, we will first attempt to understand where the naive approach to establishing a gluing of amplitudes fails. For simplicity, we will consider the case of gluing when the boundary conditions on
M
are
0
on both ends -- the resulting boundary measure that we will define will not depend on the choice of boundary conditions
7
7

7

We will see below that we put all boundary condition dependence into the amplitude.
. In this case, we will simply omit the boundary conditions from the notation, i.e. write
\mathcal{Z}_{T}(f)
instead of
\mathcal{Z}_{T}(f\mid 0,0)
.

Let
T\geq 0
and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. By the domain Markov property of Proposition
2.3
and the change of measure in Lemma
2.4
, we have that

\mathcal{Z}_{T}(f)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{0}}\left[\mathcal{Q}\exp\left(-\int_{M}\llbracket H(\varphi^{0})^{4}_{T}\rrbracket\,dx+\tilde{\delta}^{0}_{T}\right)\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{-}(f\mid 0,\varphi^{0})\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{+}(f\mid\varphi^{0},0)\right],

(3.28)

where

\mathcal{Q}:=C(\mathcal{N})\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\langle\varphi^{0},(-\mathcal{N}_{\infty})\varphi^{0}\rangle_{B}-\langle f,H(\varphi^{0})\rangle_{M^{-}}-\langle f,H(\varphi^{0})\rangle_{M^{+}}\right),

(3.29)

and

\tilde{\delta}^{0}_{T}=-3\cdot 4\int_{M}\int_{M}C^{B}_{T}(x,y)^{4}dxdy.

(3.30)

Note that in order to obtain the formula for
\delta^{0}_{T}
, we have used the definitions of the Wick powers above together with Lemma
3.2
. The term
\mathcal{E}_{T}(\varphi^{0})
consists of terms which can be bounded using fairly straightforward analytic arguments (using smoothing properties of the operators involved). The quartic term, on the other hand, is much more severe. Under the
\mu^{0}
, one can show that the variance of the term

\llbracket H(\varphi^{0})^{4}_{T}\rrbracket+\tilde{\delta}^{0}_{T}

(3.31)

contains a logarithmic divergence (see the remark below).

Remark 3.11
.

One can decompose this quartic term into homogeneous Wiener chaoses with respect to
\mu^{0}
and compute the second moment using Wick’s theorem (see e.g. the stochastic estimates in
[
BG20
]
). The term
\delta^{0}_{T}
renormalizes the divergent zeroth chaos (constant) term, but the term in the second chaos also contains a divergence. Let us remark that the zeroth chaos term is not exactly cancelled due to the difference in covariances between
\mu^{0}
and
\tilde{\mu}^{0}
.

The strategy we adopt is to instead exponentially tilt the boundary measure
\mu^{0}
by this quartic density. This leads one to consider an
interacting boundary measure
with formal density

\exp\left(-\int_{M}\llbracket H(\varphi^{0})^{4}_{T}\rrbracket\,dx+\tilde{\delta}^{0}_{T}\right)\mu^{0}(d\varphi^{0}).

(3.32)

There are two issues with this. The first issue, as hinted above, is that the variance of the random variable inside the exponential diverges as
T\rightarrow\infty
, so it is not clear how to interpret the potential, let alone the measure. We will use intuition coming from the construction of
\varphi^{4}_{3}
to instead realize this measure as a
singular perturbation
of
\mu^{0}
. This is in accordance with the predicted singularity in
[
AL08
]
. We will discuss what this means on a more technical level shortly. Let us also emphasize that this is a key point of departure from any existing proofs of the Markov property or Segal’s axioms for quantum fields of which we are aware. Furthermore, such a boundary measure would be
non-Gaussian
, which further adds to the difficulties in working with it.

The second issue is that the candidate potential depends on the bulk geometry
M
. Furthermore, if one were to impose boundary conditions, a priori the potential would include dependence on these. Any reasonable candidate for the boundary measure for the gluing property must be independent of the ambient manifold and boundary conditions – ultimately it should rely on the underlying boundary geometry, of which the bulk geometries are "cobordisms". In order to sidestep this issue, we will replace the harmonic extensions on
M
(or
M^{+}
or
M^{-}
) by their
infinite cylinder
counterparts (recall that this is denoted by
\overline{H}
). We will put any residual terms into the amplitudes.

We will now introduce the boundary measure and postpone further discussion of amplitudes to the next subsection. As usual, we will define this measure as a weak limit of approximations and thus we begin by defining the regularized boundary measure. Let
T\geq 0
. The boundary energy renormalization is the constant

\delta^{0}_{T}:=-3\cdot 4\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\overline{C}^{B}_{T}(x,y)^{4}dxdy,

(3.33)

where

\overline{C}^{B}_{T}(x,y):=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{0}}[\overline{H}\varphi_{T}(x)\overline{H}\varphi_{T}(y)],\qquad\forall x,y\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}.

(3.34)

We define the
regularized boundary potential

V_{T}^{0}(\cdot)
for every
\varphi^{0}\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
by

V_{T}^{0}(\varphi^{0})=\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\llbracket(\overline{H}\varphi_{T}^{0})^{4}\rrbracket-\delta_{T}^{0}\,dx,

(3.35)

where here our convention for the Wick powers of the infinite cylinder harmonic extension is subtly
different
– we use the same definitions as above except
C^{B}_{T}(x,y)
is replaced by
\overline{C}^{B}_{T}(x,y)
.

Remark 3.12
.

Let us stress that the replacement of
C^{B}_{T}(x,y)
by
\overline{C}^{B}_{T}(x,y)
is crucial and allows the boundary theory to be
indepedent of the geometry
. We will have to estimate the error terms from changing this covariance in our analytic estimates on the amplitudes below.

Definition 3.13
.

For every
T>0
, the
regularized interacting boundary measure
is the probability measure
\nu^{0}_{T}
on
S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
with Radon-Nikodym derivative

\nu^{0}_{T}(d\varphi^{0})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{0}}\exp(-V_{T}^{0}(\varphi^{0}))\mu^{0}(d\varphi^{0}),

(3.36)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{0}}[\exp(-V_{T}^{0}(\varphi^{0}))]
is a normalization constant. For every
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, the unnormalized Laplace transform of
f
under
\nu^{0}_{T}
is the quantity

\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{0}(f):=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{0}}[\exp(\langle f,\varphi^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}-V_{T}^{0}(\varphi^{0})],

(3.37)

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}
is the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
inner product.

The following theorem constructs the boundary measure and establishes fundamental properties that we will need in the sequel. It will be proved in Section
4
.

Theorem 3.14
.

For every
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, there exists
\mathcal{Z}^{0}(f)>0
such that

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{0}(f)=\mathcal{Z}^{0}(f).

(3.38)

Consequently, there exists a unique probability measure
\nu^{0}
on
S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
such that the measures
\nu^{0}_{T}
converge weakly to
\nu^{0}
as
T\rightarrow\infty
.

Furthermore, there exists random variables
W^{0}_{\infty}
and
Z^{0}_{\infty}
defined on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
such that

(i)

\nu^{0}={\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(W^{0}_{\infty}+Z^{0}_{\infty})
,

(ii)

{\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(W^{0}_{\infty})=\mu^{0}
,

(iii)

Z^{0}_{\infty}\in H^{1/2-\kappa}_{z}
almost surely,

(iv)

\mathbb{E}[\|Z^{0}_{\infty}\|_{H^{1/2-\kappa}_{z}}^{2}]\leq C
.

We will call the probability measure
\nu^{0}
constructed in Theorem
3.14
the interacting boundary measure/theory. As a consequence of properties (i)-(iv) above,
\nu^{0}
describes an admissible law on boundary conditions.

Remark 3.15
.

The choice of integration domain being
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
inside the boundary potential is an arbitrary choice that makes the boundary measure independent of the length of
M
. Replacing
[-1,1]
with another compact interval yields an equivalent measure to
\nu^{0}
. Perhaps a more natural choice would be the double infinite cylinder,
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
, but we opted for a compact set to avoid convergence issues.

3.4
Regularized amplitudes

Let us return to the issue of defining regularized amplitudes. Fix admissible boundary conditions
\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0}_{+}\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Recall that by the domain Markov property Proposition
2.3
and Lemma
2.4
, for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

\mathcal{Z}_{T}(f\mid\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0}_{+})=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{0}}\left[\mathcal{Q}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{T}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{-}(f\mid 0,\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0})\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{\sigma}(f\mid\varphi^{0},\varphi^{0}_{+})\right],

(3.39)

where (as above) we have that

\mathcal{Q}:=C(\mathcal{N})\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\langle\varphi^{0},(-\mathcal{N}_{\infty})\varphi^{0}\rangle_{B}-\langle f,H(\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0})\rangle_{M^{-}}-\langle f,H(\varphi^{0},\varphi^{0}_{+})\rangle_{M^{+}}\right),

(3.40)

and, moreover,

\mathcal{O}_{T}:=\exp\left(-\int_{M^{-}}\llbracket H(\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket\,dx-\int_{M^{+}}\llbracket H(\varphi^{0},\varphi^{0}_{+})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket\,dx+\tilde{\delta}^{0}_{T}\right),

(3.41)

with

\tilde{\delta}^{0}_{T}=-3\cdot 4\int_{M}\int_{M}C^{B}_{T}(x,y\mid\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0}_{+})^{4}dxdy.

(3.42)

We will decompose the term
\mathcal{Q}
into
free amplitudes
. Let
{\mathsf{C}}(\Delta_{\mathbb{T}^{2}})>0
be the constant identified in
[
Lee03
, Corollary 1.3]
such that

\frac{\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\Delta_{M^{-}}+m^{2})^{-1})}\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\Delta_{M^{+}}+m^{2})^{-1})}}{\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\Delta_{M}+m^{2})^{-1})}}=\mathsf{C}(\Delta_{\mathbb{T}^{2}})\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\mathcal{N})^{-1})}.

(3.43)

The above determinants are interpreted as
\zeta
-regularized determinants, see
[
Sim05
, Chapter 9]
. Let us define

\mathcal{A}^{{\rm free},-}_{T}(f\mid\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0})\\
:=\frac{\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\Delta_{M^{-}}+m^{2})^{-1})}}{{\mathsf{C}}(\Delta_{\mathbb{T}}^{2})\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\mathcal{N}_{0})^{-1/2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\langle\varphi^{0},(-\mathcal{N}_{\infty}^{-})\varphi^{0}\rangle_{B}-\langle f,H(\varphi_{-}^{0},\varphi^{0})\rangle_{M^{-}}\right).

(3.44)

The other free amplitudes
\mathcal{A}^{{\rm free},+}(f\mid\varphi^{0},\varphi^{0}_{+})
and
\mathcal{A}^{{\rm free}}(f\mid\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0}_{+})
are defined analogously. With these definitions, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}

\displaystyle=\frac{C(\mathcal{N})}{\det(2\pi(-\mathcal{N}_{0})^{-1/2}){\mathsf{C}}(\Delta_{\mathbb{T}^{2}})^{2}}\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\Delta_{M^{-}}+m^{2})^{-1})}\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\Delta_{M^{+}}+m^{2})^{-1})}

(3.45)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathcal{A}^{{\rm free},-}(f\mid\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0})\mathcal{A}^{{\rm free},+}(f\mid\varphi^{0},\varphi^{0}_{+}).

(3.46)

We will now rearrange the term
\mathcal{O}_{T}
so that the interacting boundary measure appears. Observe that we may write

\int_{M^{-}}\llbracket H(\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket\,dx=\int_{M^{-}}\llbracket H(\varphi^{0}_{T})^{4}_{T}\rrbracket\,dx+E_{T}(a,-),

(3.47)

and similarly for the analogous term on
M^{+}
with corresponding commutator
E_{T}(a,+)
. Furthermore,

\int_{M}\llbracket H(\varphi^{0})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket\,dx=\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\llbracket\overline{H}(\varphi^{0})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket\,dx+E_{T}(b),

(3.48)

where
E_{T}(b)=E_{T}(b,-)+E_{T}(b,+)
and

E_{T}(b,-)=\int_{M^{-}}\llbracket H(\varphi^{0})^{4}_{T}\rrbracket\,dx-\int_{[-1,0]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\llbracket\overline{H}(\varphi^{0})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket\,dx,

(3.49)

and similarly for
E_{T}(b,+)
. Finally, let us also define

\Delta\delta^{0}_{T}:=\tilde{\delta}^{0}_{T}-\delta^{0}_{T}.

(3.50)

Thus, by symmetry, we have that

\mathcal{O}_{T}=\exp(-V_{T}^{0}(\varphi^{0}))\exp(-E_{T}(a,-)-E_{T}(a,+)-E_{T}(b)+\Delta\delta_{T}^{0}).

(3.51)

In order to smuggle in the normalization constant for the interacting boundary measure, it is convenient to introduce the notation

\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(I^{\sigma}):=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{0}}\left[e^{-\int_{I^{\sigma}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\llbracket(\overline{H}\varphi_{T}^{0})^{4}\rrbracket\,dx+\delta^{0}_{T}|I^{\sigma}|}\right],

(3.52)

where
I_{\emptyset}=[-1,1]
,
I_{-}=[-1,0]
, and
I_{+}=[0,1]
. We now define

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{T}(\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0}_{+})&:=\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{0}(I^{\emptyset})^{-1}\exp\left(-E_{T}(a,-)-E_{T}(a,+)-E_{T}(b)+\Delta\delta^{0}_{T}\right),\\
\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-}(\varphi^{0}_{-},\varphi^{0})&:=\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{0}(I^{-})^{-1}\exp\left(-E_{T}(a,-)-E_{T}(b,-)+\frac{1}{2}\Delta\delta^{0}_{T}\right),\\
\mathcal{E}_{T}^{+}(\varphi^{0},\varphi^{0}_{+})^{-1}&:=\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{0}(I^{+})^{-1}\exp\left(-E_{T}(a,+)-E_{T}(b,+)+\frac{1}{2}\Delta\delta^{0}_{T}\right).\end{split}

(3.53)

The bulk amplitudes are defined as follows.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\sigma\in\{\emptyset,-,+\}
and
\varphi_{-}^{0,\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{0,\sigma}\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. The regularized
\varphi^{4}_{3}
amplitude on
M^{\sigma}
is the map

\mathcal{A}_{T}^{\sigma}:C^{\infty}(M^{\sigma})\times S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

(3.54)

defined by

\mathcal{A}_{T}^{\sigma}(f\mid\varphi_{-}^{\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{\sigma}):=\mathcal{A}^{\rm free,\sigma}(f\mid\varphi_{-}^{0,\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{0,\sigma})\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\sigma}(\varphi_{-}^{\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{\sigma})\mathcal{Z}^{\sigma}_{T}(f\mid\varphi^{\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{\sigma}_{+}).

(3.55)

We now state and prove the gluing property for amplitudes of the regularized
\varphi^{4}_{3}
models.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
T>0
. Then for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
\varphi_{-}^{0},\varphi_{+}^{0}\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, the amplitudes satisfy the following gluing property:

\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})=\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{0}_{T}}\left[\mathcal{A}_{T}^{-}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})\mathcal{A}_{T}^{+}(f\mid\varphi^{0},\varphi_{-})\right].

(3.56)

Proof.

From the computations and definitions above, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{C(\mathcal{N})}{{\mathsf{C}}(\Delta_{\mathbb{T}^{2}})}\frac{\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\Delta_{M^{-}}+m^{2})^{-1})}\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\Delta_{M^{+}}+m^{2})^{-1})}}{\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\mathcal{N}_{0})^{-1/2})}\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\Delta_{M}+m^{2})^{-1})}}

(3.57)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(I^{-})\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(I^{+})}{\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(I)}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{0}_{T}}\left[\mathcal{A}_{T}^{-}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})\mathcal{A}_{T}^{+}(f\mid\varphi^{0},\varphi_{-})\right].

(3.58)

First of all, by symmetry we have that

\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(I)=\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(I^{-})\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(I^{+}).

(3.59)

Now, by the definition of
{\mathsf{C}}(\Delta_{\mathbb{T}^{2}})
, the other constant term is equal
8
8

8

Here, we are using that the ratio of zeta regularized determinants can be obtained by the spectral approximations considered in Lemma
2.4
. This follows from standard approximation arguments and we omit this.
to

C(\mathcal{N})\times\frac{\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\mathcal{N})^{-1/2})}}{\sqrt{\det(2\pi(-\mathcal{N}_{0})^{-1/2})}}=1.

(3.60)

∎

3.5
Amplitudes for the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
model

Our goal in the next two sections is to give a conditional proof of Theorem
1.2
. We will begin by isolating the key technical results required in order to establish the existence of the amplitudes for the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
model without cutoffs and take the limit in the gluing property of Proposition
3.17
. The proofs of these key results – formalized in Theorems
3.18
and
3.20
– will then constitute most of the remainder of this article.

Let us begin by discussing the convergence of the regularized
\varphi^{4}_{3}
amplitudes. Although the regularized amplitudes were defined with respect to arbitrary distributional boundary conditions, here we will require the boundary conditions to be sampled from an admissible law. We will only consider boundary conditions sampled from
\nu^{0}
and stress that the analysis carries over to the general case with almost no changes. At the base level, the difficulty of convergence comes from the fact that samples from
\nu^{0}
are distribution-valued, in fact almost surely in
\mathcal{C}^{-\frac{1}{2}-\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. As such, there are boundary divergences that must be renormalized. Our renormalization procedure is based on the framework of
[
BG20
]
. We will write the regularized amplitudes as a
lifted amplitude
, defined on a larger space, and taking as argument an
enhancement
of both boundary fields simultaneously. The lifted amplitude is deterministically defined and is continuous on this larger space – proving this constitutes the "analytic part" of the argument and is formalized (along with some growth bounds) in Theorem
3.18
. The "probabilistic part" concerns the convergence in law of the random argument, the enhanced boundary field
9
9

9

Although the enhancement is of both boundary fields simultaneously, we will nevertheless refer to it as the enhanced boundary field.
, to a limiting enhanced boundary field – this is formalized in Theorem
3.20
.

\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\ \mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{T}\left(f\ ,\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\right)
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}(f\ \mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}\left(f\ ,\Xi_{\infty}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\right)

Figure 2:
The regularized amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
are lifted to
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{T}(f,\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}))
. Theorems
3.18
and
3.20
yield convergence of these lifted amplitudes to
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}(f,\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}))
, that we then define to be the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
amplitude.

The following theorem – which is one of, if not the most important, key contributions of this article – defines the lifted amplitudes, establishes their continuity, and also some exponential growth bounds. An outline of its proof is found in Section
5
, but we stress that the actual proof is spread over Sections
5
-
8
.

Theorem 3.18
.

There exists a Banach space,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
such that the following holds. Let
\sigma\in\{\emptyset,-,+\}
.

(I)

(Lifted amplitude).
Let
T>0
. There exists measurable maps

•

\Xi^{\partial}_{T}:\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}\quad\hfill\textup{(Enhancement)}

•

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\sigma}_{T}:C^{\infty}(M)\times\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\quad\hfill\textup{(Lift)}\hskip 22.0pt

such that for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M_{\sigma})
and almost surely for every
(\varphi^{\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{\sigma}_{+})\sim\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0}
,

\mathcal{A}_{T}^{\sigma}(f\mid\varphi_{-}^{\sigma},\varphi_{+}^{\sigma})=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\sigma}_{T}(f,\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi^{\sigma}_{-},\varphi^{\sigma}_{+})).

(3.61)

(II)

(Limit amplitude and convergence).
There exists a measurable map, called the limiting lifted amplitude,

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\sigma}_{\infty}:C^{\infty}(M_{\sigma})\times\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

(3.62)

such the following convergence statement holds. Let
(\Xi^{\partial}_{T})\subset\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
and
\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
be such that
\Xi^{\partial}_{T}\rightarrow\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
. Then for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M_{\sigma})
,

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\sigma}_{T}(f,\Xi^{\partial}_{T})=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\sigma}_{\infty}(f,\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}).

(3.63)

(III)

(Almost sure bounds)
For every
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, there exists
c,C,p>0
such that for every
T\in(0,\infty]
and every
\Xi^{\partial}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{T}^{\sigma}(f,\Xi^{\partial})\leq C\exp(c\|\Xi^{\partial}\|^{p}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}}),

(3.64)

Remark 3.19
.

Theorem
3.18
contains a few objects whose existence we postulate as part of the theorem, but for ease of reading at this stage we do not define them

(i)

The Banach space
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
is called the
enhanced boundary space
. We leave its definition implicit. It is obtained by taking the space
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}
in Section
4
and products of intersections of Banach spaces appearing in the estimates on boundary terms in Section
6
.

(ii)

The (cutoff) enhancement map
\Xi^{\partial}_{T}
is a measurable mapping of the boundary data into the stochastic products defined in Section
4
and the (boundary-dependent)
\Upsilon_{i}
terms defined in Section
5
. See Section
5.5
.

(iii)

The definitions of the lifted amplitudes and limiting lifted amplitude are in terms of
renormalized variational problems
that we develop across Sections
5
-
8
. See in particular the outline of proof in Section
5
.

Let us emphasize that Theorem
3.18
, parts I and II are
deterministic statements
. They do not rely on the law of the boundary conditions. However, as clear from part I of the theorem, we need to understand the amplitudes evaluated with random arguments in
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
. This is addressed in the theorem below, which yields the convergence in law of the enhanced boundary field to a limiting enhanced boundary field. Its proof can be found in Sections
4
and
6
(corresponding to the purely boundary-dependent terms – see Proposition
4.3
– and the
\Upsilon_{i}
terms mentioned above, respectively).

Theorem 3.20
.

Almost surely for every
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\sim\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0}
, there exist a random variable

\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}

(3.65)

called the
limiting enhanced boundary field
such that the following holds.

(I)

(Almost sure convergence)
Almost surely in
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\sim\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0}
, we have the following convergnce in the Banach space
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
:

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{1},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{2})=\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

(3.66)

(II)

(Distributional convergence)
Almost surely in
\varphi\sim\nu^{0}
, we have the following convergence in law:

\big(\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi,\cdot)\big)_{*}\nu^{0}_{T}\rightharpoonup\big(\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}(\varphi,\cdot)\big)_{*}\nu^{0}.

(3.67)

Remark 3.21
.

Let us mention that Theorem
3.20
could have been stated for arbitrary independent admissible boundary conditions with small modifications (especially to part II, where
\nu^{0}_{T}
is replaced by a suitable approximation), but we will only apply it in the case of
\nu^{0}
.

In light of Theorems
3.18
and
3.20
, we may now rigorously define the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
amplitudes (without cutoffs) and give meaning to the maps introduced in Theorem
1.2
.

Definition 3.22
.

Let
\sigma\in\{\emptyset,-,+\}
. The
\varphi^{4}_{3}
amplitude on
M^{\sigma}
is the map

\mathcal{A}_{\infty}^{\sigma}:C^{\infty}(M_{\sigma})\times\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

(3.68)

defined for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M_{\sigma})
and almost surely for every
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}
independent admissible boundary conditions by

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}_{\infty}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}):=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\sigma}_{\infty}(f,\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})),

(3.69)

where the lifted amplitude
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\sigma}_{\infty}
is constructed in Theorem
3.18
and the limiting enhanced boundary field is constructed in Theorem
3.20
.

3.6
Proof of Segal’s gluing property for
\varphi^{4}_{3}

We will now turn to the proof of Theorem
1.2
. Our starting point is the approximate gluing property established in Proposition
3.17
. In passing from the almost sure and distributional convergence statements of Theorems
3.18
and
3.20
to convergence in expectation, we will need the following upgrade of the bounds in Theorem
3.18
that can be seen as controlling the large field behaviour. Its proof is contained in Section
9
.

Lemma 3.23
.

There exists
\alpha>0
sufficiently small and a Banach space
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}
such that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
is compactly
embedded and the following estimate holds. There exists
c>0
such that for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0}
almost surely for every
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, there exists
C>0
such that for every
T>0
,

E_{\nu^{0}_{T}}\left[\mathcal{A}_{T}^{-}(f\mid\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})\mathcal{A}^{+}_{T}(f\mid\varphi^{0},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})e^{c\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}}^{\alpha}+c\|\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi^{0},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}}^{\alpha}}\right]\leq C.

(3.70)

Remark 3.24
.

The Banach space
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}
is obtained in a trivial fashion from
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
. As shall be clear,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
is comprised of function/distribution spaces (e.g. Sobolev, Hölder, and Besov spaces) of regularity which can be increased by a sufficiently small parameter without changing any of the statements above. We then use classical compact embedding theorems for these spaces by increasing the regularity of each space by such a sufficiently small parameter.

We are now ready to give the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Observe that for
T>0
, by Proposition
3.17
we have

\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})=\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{T}_{0}}\left[\mathcal{A}_{T}^{-}(f\mid\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})\mathcal{A}_{T}^{+}(f\mid\varphi^{0},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})\right].

(3.71)

Furthermore, for the lefthand side of (
3.71
), by part I of Theorem
3.20
and part II of Theorem
3.18
we have that

\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})=\lim_{T\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{T}(f,\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+}))=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}(f,\Xi_{\infty}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})).

(3.72)

Hence it suffices to estimate the expectation term in the righthand side of (
3.71
). We will use Skorokhod’s embedding theorem to realize this distributional convergence as almost sure convergence and then use a truncation argument to deduce convergence of the expectations. Note that a priori this is up to a subsequence, but the limits will be independent of the choice of subsequence and hence one can deduce full convergence.

By part II of Theorem
3.20
and Skorokod’s embedding theorem
[
Jak98
]
, there exists a subsequence
T\rightarrow\infty
(not relabelled) and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}
-valued random variables
(\Xi^{\partial,-}_{T},\Xi^{\partial,+}_{T})
and
(\Xi^{\partial,-}_{\infty},\Xi^{\partial,+}_{\infty})
defined on some common probability space – which we abuse notation and denote as
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with expectation written as
\mathbb{E}
– such that

•

\Xi^{\partial,-}_{T}
is equal in law to the law of
\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})
where
(\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})\sim\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0}_{T}
, and similarly for
\Xi^{\delta,+}_{T}
.

•

\Xi^{\partial,-}_{\infty}
is equal in law to the law of
\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}(\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})
where
(\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})\sim\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0}
, and similarly for
\Xi^{\partial,+}_{\infty}
.

•

\Xi^{\partial,-}_{T}
(resp.
\Xi^{\partial,+}_{T}
) converges
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely in
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}
to
\Xi^{\partial,-}_{\infty}
(resp.
\Xi^{\partial,+}_{\infty}
).

Let
K>0
be arbitrary. Let us denote
\varphi^{0}_{T}\sim\nu^{0}_{T}
for the rest of this proof. By parts I and II of Theorem
3.18
and the bounded convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\max(||\Xi_{T}^{\partial,-}||_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}},\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial,+}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}})\leq K}\mathcal{A}^{-}_{T}(f\mid\rho_{T}\ast\varphi^{T}_{-},\varphi^{T}_{0})\mathcal{A}^{+}_{T}(f\mid\varphi^{T}_{0},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})\right]

(3.73)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\max(||\Xi_{\infty}^{\partial,-}||_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}},\|\Xi^{\partial,+}_{\infty}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}})\leq K}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{-}(f,\Xi_{\infty}(\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0}))\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{\infty}(f,\Xi_{\infty}(\varphi^{0},\varphi_{+}))\right]

(3.74)

On the other hand, by a union bound and exponential Chebyshev’s inequality, followed by Lemma
3.23
, there exists
\alpha,c,C>0
such that for every
T>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\max{(||\Xi_{T}^{\partial,-}||_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}},||\Xi_{T}^{\partial,+}||_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}})}\geq K}\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\varphi^{T}_{0})\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\varphi^{T}_{0},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})\right]

(3.75)

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-cK^{\alpha}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{c||\Xi_{T}^{\partial,-}||^{\alpha}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}}+c||\Xi_{T}^{\partial,+}||^{\alpha}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}}}\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\varphi^{T}_{0})\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\varphi^{T}_{0},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})\right]\leq Ce^{-cK^{\alpha}}.

(3.76)

Since
K
is arbitrary, Theorem
1.2
now follows by the definition of the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
amplitudes.

∎

3.7
Proof of Markov property

We now establish the Markov property, Theorem
1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Recall the definition of
\nu^{\ell}
. Let
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
f
is
M_{\ell}
-measurable and
g
is
M\setminus M_{\ell}
-measurable. In order to prove the Theorem
1.4
, it is sufficient to show that

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\exp(\langle f+g,\varphi\rangle)]=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\ell}(\cdot|\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})}[\exp(\langle f,\varphi\rangle)]\exp(\langle g,\varphi\rangle)\right].

(3.77)

We will denote
\langle f,\varphi\rangle^{N}=\langle f,\varphi\rangle\wedge N
, and
\mathcal{A}_{T}(f^{N}|\cdot,\cdot)
be the Amplitude where
\langle f,\varphi\rangle
is replaced by
\langle f,\varphi\rangle\wedge N
.
Furthermore denote by

V^{[a,b]}(\varphi_{T})=\int_{[a,b]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\llbracket\varphi_{T}^{4}\rrbracket-\gamma_{T}\int_{[a,b]\times{\mathbb{T}^{2}}}\llbracket\varphi_{T}^{2}\rrbracket-\delta_{T}|a-b|.

(3.78)

By dominated convergence and Theorem
3.18
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\exp(\langle f+g,\varphi\rangle)]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{T}}\left[\left(\exp(\langle f,\varphi_{T}\rangle^{N}+\langle g,\varphi\rangle)\right)\right].

(3.79)

Let us now fix
N
and
T
. By using the definition of
\nu^{T}
, splitting the potential, and using the tower property of conditional expectation, we have that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{T}}\left[\left(\exp(\langle f,\varphi_{T}\rangle^{N}+\langle g,\varphi\rangle)\right)\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{T}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\exp(\langle f,\varphi_{T}\rangle^{N})\exp(-V^{[-\ell,\ell]}_{T}(\varphi_{T}))\mid\mathcal{F}_{M_{l}}\right]\exp(-\langle g,\varphi_{T}\rangle)\exp(-V^{[-L,L]\setminus[-\ell,\ell]}_{T}(\varphi_{T}))\right].\end{split}

(3.80)

Let us define
\varphi_{-}
and
\varphi_{+}
as the restriction of
\varphi\sim\mu
to the tori
\{-\ell\}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
and
\{\ell\}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
, respectively – this almost surely exists thanks to the Markov property of the GFF. Then by the definition of the unnormalized Laplace transform,

(
3.80
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{T}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mathcal{Z}^{(-\ell,\ell)}_{T}(f^{N}\mid(\varphi_{-})_{T},(\varphi_{+})_{T})\exp(\langle g,\varphi\rangle)\exp(-V^{[-L,L]\setminus(-\ell,\ell)}_{T}(\varphi))]

(3.81)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{T}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Bigg[\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{(-\ell,\ell)}(0\mid(\varphi_{-})_{T},(\varphi_{+})_{T})}{\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{(-\ell,\ell)}(0\mid(\varphi_{-})_{T},(\varphi_{+})_{T})}\mathcal{Z}^{(-\ell,\ell)}_{T}(f^{N}\mid(\varphi_{-})_{T},(\varphi_{+})_{T})

(3.82)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\exp(\langle g,\varphi\rangle)\exp(-V^{[-L,L]\setminus(-\ell,\ell)}_{T}(\varphi))\Bigg]

(3.83)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{T}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{(-\ell,\ell)}(0\mid(\varphi_{-})_{T},(\varphi_{+})_{T})}\mathcal{Z}^{(-\ell,\ell)}_{T}(f^{N}\mid(\varphi_{-})_{T},(\varphi^{0})_{T})

(3.84)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\exp(\langle g,\varphi\rangle)\exp(-V^{[-L,L]}_{T}(\varphi))\Bigg],

(3.85)

where in the last line we have unpacked the definition of
\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{(-\ell,\ell)}(0\mid(\varphi_{-})_{T},(\varphi_{+})_{T})
. The theorem now follows by using the definition of
\nu^{\ell}_{T}(\cdot\mid\cdot,\cdot)
and taking limits thanks to Theorem
3.18
.
∎

4
Construction of the boundary measure

In this section we will prove Theorem
3.14
. Following the strategy introduced in
[
BG20
]
, our starting point is to represent
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)
using a variational formula originally due to Boué and Dupuis
[
BD98
]
, and we do this in Section
4.1
. In Section
4.2
we establish the regularity of the relevant Gaussian/multilinear functions of Gaussian random variables that occur in the variational problem. In Section
4.3
we use the regularity of these stochastic objects to obtain a
renormalized
variational representation for
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)
. We then use harmonic analysis estimates to establish uniform bounds on this renormalized variational problem. In Section
4.4
, assuming some technical results, we upgrade the uniform bounds into full convergence of
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)
via a
\Gamma
-convergence argument for the variational problems, and thus prove Theorem
3.14
. Finally in Section
4.5
, we prove the remaining technical results to close the argument.

4.1
Variational representation of
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)

Let
\kappa_{0}>0
be sufficiently small (fixed a posteriori). Denote by
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
the standard Wiener space, where
\Omega^{0}=C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa_{0}}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
and
\mathcal{F}^{0}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra. For the remainder of
this
section, we will simply write
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}^{0}
for notational convenience (with expectation
\mathbb{E}^{0}=\mathbb{E})
. The canonical coordinate process
X^{0}
under
\mathbb{P}
is an
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
-Brownian motion. We will abuse notation and assume that
\mathcal{F}
is
\mathbb{P}
-complete and that the filtration generated by
X^{0}
satisfies the usual conditions (on a filtered probability space). For every
t>0
, let
\sigma_{t}^{0}=\rho_{t}\ast(-\Delta_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}+m^{2})^{-1/2}\ast\rho_{t}
and
J_{t}^{0}=\sqrt{\partial_{t}\sigma_{t}^{0}}
, where the square root is defined in Fourier space after viewing
\sigma^{0}_{t}
as a Fourier multiplier. We abuse notation and write
J_{t}^{0}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for the kernel of
J_{t}^{0}
.

Define the stochastic process
(W^{0}_{T})_{T\geq 0}
by

W^{0}_{T}:=\int_{0}^{T}J^{0}_{t}dX^{0}_{t},\qquad\forall T\geq 0.

(4.1)

A covariance computation yields that for every
T>0
we have that
{\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(W^{0}_{T})={\rm Law}_{\mu^{0}}(\varphi_{T})
. Furthermore, again by a covariance computation, we have that the random variable
W^{0}_{\infty}:=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}W_{T}^{0}
exists (the convergence being in e.g.
L^{2}
) and is equal in law to
\mu^{0}
.

Thanks to the equality in law, it is clear we can express
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)
as an expectation of a functional on Wiener space with sufficiently good integrability properties (i.e. tamed in the sense of
[
Ü14
]
). This allows us to apply the Boué-Dupuis formula
[
BD98
]
(we will use the version in
[
Ü14
]
). In order to state this variational formula, we will need some notation.

Let
\mathbb{H}^{0}
denote the space of progressively measurable processes that are
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely in

\mathcal{H}^{0}:=L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{z}.

(4.2)

For every
u\in\mathbb{H}^{0}
and
T\geq 0
, let

Z_{T}^{0}(u)=\int_{0}^{T}J_{t}^{0}u_{t}dt.

(4.3)

The following proposition is a direct application of the Boué-Dupuis formula to our setting.

Proposition 4.1
.

For every
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,

-\log\mathcal{Z}_{T}^{0}(f)=\inf_{u\in\mathbb{H}^{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[\langle f,W_{T}^{0}+Z_{T}^{0}(u)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}+\mathcal{V}_{T}^{0}(W_{T}^{0},Z_{T}^{0}(u))+\frac{1}{2}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\right],

(4.4)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{T}^{0}(W_{T}^{0},Z_{T}^{0}(u))

\displaystyle=\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}4\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)+6\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}\rrbracket(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u))^{2}

(4.5)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+4\overline{H}W_{T}^{0}(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u))^{3}+(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u))^{4}\,dx-\delta_{T}^{0}.

(4.6)

Above, we recall that
\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket
denote the second and third Wick powers of
\overline{H}W^{0}_{T}
, respectively.

Remark 4.2
.

Note that in principle the term
\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{4}\rrbracket dz
should be appear in the integrand. It is classical that this quantity can be written as an iterated stochastic integral and, hence, martingale. See
[
Nua09
]
. However, by the martingale property, it has mean zero and hence can be omitted in light of the expectation in the variational formula (
4.4
).

4.2
Regularity and convergence of the Wick powers

In order to understand better the divergences in the variational problem (
4.4
), we first
10
10

10

We will show later on that
Z_{T}^{0}(u)\in H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for every
u\in L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{z}
with a uniform estimate on its norm in
T
. See Lemma
4.4
. This allows us to deduce some determinstic regularity estimates on
\overline{H}Z^{0}_{T}(u)
by properties of the harmonic extension
\overline{H}
. See Appendix
C.1
.
need to understand the regularity of the Wick powers of
\overline{H}W_{T}^{0}
. The is contained in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.3
.

For every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
,
\alpha\in(\max(0,\frac{i-2}{2}),i/2]
, every
\delta>0
sufficiently small, and for every
p\in[1,\infty)
there exists
C_{p}>0
such that and

\sup_{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sup_{\tau\in[-1,1]}|\tau|^{\alpha}\|\llbracket(\overline{H}W^{0}_{T})^{i}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-i/2+\alpha-\delta}_{z}}\Big)^{p}\Big]\leq C_{p}.

(4.7)

Furthermore, there exists a random variable
\llbracket(\overline{H}W^{0}_{\infty})^{i}\rrbracket\in L^{\infty}(|\tau|^{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{-i/2+\alpha-\delta}_{z})
– where this weighted space is defined with the norm appearing inside the expectation (
4.7
) – such that for every sequence
(T_{n}):T_{n}\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\llbracket(\overline{H}W^{0}_{T_{n}})^{i}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket(\overline{H}W^{0}_{\infty})^{i}\rrbracket\,\text{ in }L^{\infty}(|\tau|^{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{-i/2+\alpha-\delta}_{z}).

(4.8)

The estimates also hold with
\overline{H}
replaced by
H
, and the single harmonic extension replaced by the double harmonic extension.

In order to prove this proposition, we will need to represent the Wick powers in terms of iterated stochastic integrals. Let us write
\overline{P}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
(z,x)\mapsto\overline{P}(z,x)
to denote the Poisson kernel corresponding to the kernel of the harmonic extension operator
\overline{H}
. Observe that

\overline{H}W^{0}_{t}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{t}J_{s}^{0}dB^{n}_{s}\,\int\mathsf{e_{n}}(z)\overline{P}(z,\cdot)dz.

(4.9)

For every
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\widehat{\overline{P}}_{\tau}(n)
denote the Fourier transform of
\widehat{\overline{P}}(\tau,\cdot)
. Then

\widehat{\overline{H}W_{t}^{0}(\tau,\cdot)}(n)=\int_{0}^{t}J_{s}^{0}dB_{s}^{n}\,\widehat{\overline{P}}_{\tau}(n).

(4.10)

Hence by Itô’s formula,

\widehat{\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{k}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)}(n)=\sum_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n}k!\int_{0\leq t_{1}\leq\dots\leq t_{k-1}\leq t}\prod_{j=1}^{k}J_{t_{j}}^{0}dB_{t_{j}}^{n_{j}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{k}\widehat{\overline{P}}_{\tau}(n_{j}).

(4.11)

Computing the second moment (see
[
Nua09
]
for computing second moments of iterated stochastic integrals), we find that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|\widehat{\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{k}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)}(n)\Big|^{2}\Big]=(k!)^{2}\int_{0\leq t_{1}\leq\dots\leq t_{k-1}\leq t}\prod_{k=1}^{i}J_{t}^{0}(n_{k})^{2}dt_{k}\,\prod_{j=1}^{k}\widehat{\overline{P}}_{\tau}(n_{j})^{2}.

(4.12)

We may now undo the simplex integration and compute these explicitly to obtain:

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|\widehat{\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{k}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)}(n)\Big|^{2}\Big]\leq k!\sum_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\langle n_{j}\rangle}\exp(-2|\tau|\sum_{j=1}^{k}\langle n_{j}\rangle),

(4.13)

where we have additionally used the explicit symbol of
\overline{P}_{\tau}
, which follows by straightforward Fourier space calculations thanks to the infinite cylinder. See Appendix
C.1
.

Let us now prove the proposition.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Let us observe that the last statements of the proposition follow since the two harmonic extensions
\overline{H}
and
H
agree up to a smoothing term. It is sufficient by the martingale convergence theorem to show that these are uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(|\tau|^{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{-i/2+\alpha-\delta}_{z})
. Furthermore, by a Besov embedding argument, it is sufficient to take
p\in[1,\infty)
.

Let
\tau>0
and let
\Delta_{j}
be a Littlewood-Paley block (see Appendix
A
). Then by stationarity and hypercontractivity (see
[
Nua09
]
),

2^{jsp}\mathbb{E}[\|\Delta_{j}(\llbracket\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{k}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}_{z}}^{p}]\leq C_{p}\Big(2^{2js}\mathbb{E}[\|\Delta_{j}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{k}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{z}}^{2}]\Big)^{p/2}.

(4.14)

Then by Parseval’s theorem.

2^{2js}\mathbb{E}[\|\Delta_{j}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{k}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{z}}^{2}=2^{2js}\sum_{n}|\Delta_{j}(n)|^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|\widehat{\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{k}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)}(n)\Big|^{2}\Big].

(4.15)

We may therefore bound the above by

(
4.15
)

\displaystyle\leq k!2^{2js}\sum_{n}|\Delta_{j}(n)|^{2}\sum_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\langle n_{j}\rangle}\exp(-2|\tau|\sum_{j=1}^{k}\langle n_{j}\rangle)

(4.16)

\displaystyle=Ck!2^{2js}\sum_{n}\Delta_{j}(n)\mathcal{K}_{\tau}\underbrace{\ast\dots\ast}_{k}\mathcal{K}_{\tau}(n),

(4.17)

where for any
\beta>0
there exists
C_{\beta}>0

\mathcal{K}_{\tau}(n)\leq C_{\beta}\frac{1}{|\tau|^{\beta}\langle n\rangle^{1+\beta}},

(4.18)

for any
k\beta<1
. Applying the bounds on convolutions from
[
TW18
, Appendix C.3]
, provided
\frac{1+\beta}{2}>\frac{k-1}{k}
, we then obtain

2^{2js}\sum_{n}\Delta_{j}(n)\mathcal{K}_{\tau}\underbrace{\ast\dots\ast}_{k}\mathcal{K}_{\tau}(n)\leq C2^{2js}\sum_{n}\Delta_{j}(n)\frac{1}{|\tau|^{k\beta}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{k\beta+1}}\leq C^{\prime}|\tau|^{-k\beta}\frac{2^{2j(1+s)}}{2^{j(k\beta+1)}}.

(4.19)

Thus, for convergence, we need

s<-1/2+k\beta/2,

(4.20)

for every
\beta\in(\min(0,\frac{k-2}{k}),1)
.

The convergence statement is a straightforward modification of the bounds. ∎

4.3
Renormalized variational problem and uniform bounds

Let us now return to the variational problem in (
4.4
). We begin with a deterministic regularity estimate on
Z_{T}^{0}(u)
for
u\in\mathcal{H}^{0}=L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
which will allow us to identify divergences. Its proof, which we omit, follows from a similar computation to
[
BG20
, Lemma 2]
and the regularizing properties of
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}+m^{2})^{-1/2}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For every
u\in\mathcal{H}^{0}
,

\sup_{T}\|Z^{0}_{T}(u)\|_{H^{1/2}_{z}}\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}.

(4.21)

In light of Proposition
4.3
and Lemma
4.4
, together with properties of the harmonic extension (see Appendix
C.1
), it is clear that there is a potential divergence in the integrand involving
\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket
. In order to renormalize it, let us recall that

\delta^{0}_{T}:=-3\cdot 4\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\overline{C}_{T}^{B}(x,x^{\prime})^{4}dxdx^{\prime},

(4.22)

where

\overline{C}_{T}^{B}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{0}}[H\varphi_{T}(x)H\varphi_{T}(x^{\prime})].

(4.23)

For every
T>0
, let us define the map
\ell^{0}_{T}:\mathcal{H}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}
by

\ell^{0}_{T}(u)_{t}:=4J_{t}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq T}+u_{t},\qquad\forall t\geq 0.

(4.24)

Above,
\overline{H}^{*}
is the adjoint of
\overline{H}
in
L^{2}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
with kernel
\overline{P}^{*}:([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We may also extend the definition of
\ell^{0}_{T}
to
T=\infty
, although it is no longer a map acting on
\mathcal{H}^{0}
to itself, i.e. there is a loss of regularity. We will address this issue below in the context of the
boundary remainder map
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For every
T>0
and every
u\in\mathbb{H}^{0}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}4\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)dx+\frac{1}{2}\|u\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{z}}^{2}-\delta^{0}_{T}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\ell^{0}_{T}(u)\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{z}}^{2}\right].

(4.25)

Proof.

By Itô’s formula, the stochastic integral being mean zero, and taking adjoints,

\displaystyle\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}

\displaystyle 4\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)dx

(4.26)

\displaystyle=\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}4\Big(\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket\overline{H}J_{t}^{0}(u_{t})dt+4\overline{H}Z_{t}^{0}(u)d\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket\Big)dtdz

(4.27)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathbb{E}[\cdot]=0}{=}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}4J_{t}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket\,u_{t}dtdz.

(4.28)

Thus, by the change of variables (
4.24
), the lemma will follow if we can establish that

-\frac{4^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[(J_{t}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket)^{2}]dtdz=\delta^{0}_{T}.

(4.29)

Observe that

-\frac{2}{4^{2}}\times\text{LHS of }\eqref{eq: bdry cubic 1}=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathbb{(}\dots)dxdx^{\prime}dzdz^{\prime}dt,

(4.30)

where

(\dots)=(J_{t}^{0})^{2}(z,z^{\prime})\overline{P}^{*}(z^{\prime},x^{\prime})\overline{P}^{*}(z,x)\mathbb{E}\Big[\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket(x)\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket(x^{\prime})]\Big].

(4.31)

We will use that on functions mapping
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that are compactly supported on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
, the (action of the) adjoint of
\overline{H}
is equal to the adjoint of
\overline{H}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
restricted to
L^{2}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Thus, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
,

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(J_{t}^{0})^{2}(z,z^{\prime})\overline{P}^{*}(z^{\prime},x^{\prime})\overline{P}^{*}(z,x)dzdz^{\prime}=\dot{\overline{C}}_{t}^{B}(x,x^{\prime}).

(4.32)

Hence, by standard properties of Wick powers,

-\frac{2}{4^{2}}\times\text{LHS of }\eqref{eq: bdry cubic 1}=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\dot{\overline{C}}_{t}^{B}(x,x^{\prime})\overline{C}_{t}^{B}(x,x^{\prime})^{3}dxdx^{\prime}=-\frac{2}{4^{2}}\delta^{0}_{T},

(4.33)

which upon rearrangement yields (
4.29
).
∎

4.3.1
Definition of the renormalized variational problem

Let us now define a renormalized variational problem. First, let us introduce some notation. For every
T\in(0,\infty]
, define the vector of stochastic processes with distribution-valued coordinates,

\Xi^{0}_{T}(X^{0}):=\left((\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})_{t\leq T},(\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{2}\rrbracket)_{t\leq T},(\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{t}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket)_{t\leq T}\right).

(4.34)

We stress that thanks to Proposition
4.3
, we include the case
T=\infty
. Consider the Banach space

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}:=C_{t}L^{4}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa_{0}^{\prime}}_{z}\times C_{t}L^{8}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa_{0}^{\prime}}_{z}\times C_{t}L^{1}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa_{0}^{\prime}}_{z},

(4.35)

where
\kappa_{0}^{\prime}>0
is sufficiently small and fixed a posteriori. Proposition
4.3
and properties of the harmonic extension implies that
\Xi^{0}_{T}(X^{0})\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}
almost surely. In fact, we have the following convergence result.

Proposition 4.6
.

For every
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\Xi^{0}_{T}(X^{0})-\Xi^{0}_{\infty}(X^{0})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}}^{p}\right]=0.

(4.36)

Remark 4.7
.

We will typically denote an element of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}
by
\Xi^{0}=(\Xi^{0,1},\Xi^{0,2},\Xi^{0,3})
. To distinguish this from the stochastic process, we will always keep the dependecy of
\Xi^{0}_{T}(X^{0})
on the underlying
L^{2}
-Brownian motion
X^{0}
in the notation. Let us also remark that there is flexibility in the exact choice of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}
and our choice is motivated by the analytic estimates below.

Let us now define the
boundary remainder map
, which amounts to modifying the definition of
\ell^{0}_{T}
to allow for the case
T=\infty
. Let us introduce the Banach space

\mathcal{L}^{0}:=L^{2}_{t}W^{1/2-\kappa_{0}^{\prime},2+\kappa_{0}^{\prime\prime}}_{z},

(4.37)

where for every
\kappa_{0}^{\prime}>0
sufficiently small,
\kappa_{0}^{\prime\prime}>0
is chosen so that
H^{1/2}_{z}\hookrightarrow W^{1/2-\kappa_{0}^{\prime},2+\kappa_{0}^{\prime\prime}}_{z}\hookrightarrow L^{4}_{z}
. For every
T\in(0,\infty]
, the boundary remainder map is the map
\ell^{0}_{T}:\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{0}
defined by

\ell^{0}_{T}(\Xi^{0},u)_{t}:=4J_{t}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}\Xi^{0,3}_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq T}+u_{t},\qquad\forall t\geq 0.

(4.38)

In the case
T<\infty
and
\Xi^{0}=\Xi^{0}_{T}(X^{0})
, we recover (
4.24
) and simply write
\ell^{0}_{T}(\Xi^{0}_{T}(X^{0}),u)=\ell^{0}_{T}(u)
.

For every
T\in(0,\infty]
, the
residual boundary potential
is the map
\Phi^{0}_{T}:\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by

\Phi_{T}^{0}(\Xi^{0},u):=\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}6\Xi^{0,2}(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u))^{2}+4\Xi^{0,1}(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u))^{3}dx,\qquad\forall(\Xi,u)\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}.

(4.39)

Additionally, let
F^{0}_{T}:\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote the map defined for every
(\Xi^{0},u)\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}
by

F^{0}_{T}(\Xi^{0},u):=\Phi^{0}_{T}(\Xi^{0},u)+\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell^{0}_{T}(\Xi^{0},u)\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{z}}^{2}.

(4.40)

The map
F^{0}_{T}
consists of the residual boundary potential together with positive terms – corresponding to an
effective potential
and
effective relative entropy
– that will help us analyze the variational problem below.
Given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, let
F^{0,f}_{T}:\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the map defined by

F^{0,f}_{T}(\Xi^{0},u):=\langle f,Z^{0}_{T}(u)\rangle_{L^{2}_{z}}+F^{0}_{T}(\Xi^{0},u),\qquad\forall(\Xi^{0},u)\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}.

(4.41)

Write
\mathbb{L}^{0}
to denote the space of progressively measurable processes that are almost surely in
\mathcal{L}^{0}
.

Definition 4.8
.

For every
T\in(0,\infty]
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, the
renormalized boundary cost function
is the map
\mathbb{F}^{0,f}_{T}:\mathbb{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by

\mathbb{F}^{0,f}_{T}(u)=\mathbb{E}\left[F^{0,f}_{T}(\Xi^{0}_{T}(X^{0}),u)\right],\qquad\forall u\in\mathbb{L}^{0}.

(4.42)

In the case
f=0
,
F^{0,0}_{T}=F^{0}_{T}
and we write
\mathbb{F}^{0,0}_{T}=\mathbb{F}^{0}_{T}
.

As a direct consequence of Lemma
4.5
and the definitions above, we obtain the following renormalized variational problem when
T<\infty
.

Proposition 4.9
.

For every
T>0
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,

-\log\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)=\inf_{u\in\mathbb{L}^{0}}\mathbb{F}^{0,f}_{T}(u).

(4.43)

For the remainder of the section, we will analyze the convergence of the infima in the above renormalized variational problem.

4.3.2
Bounds on the residual boundary potential

As a justification that we now have the "correct" renormalized problem, we will start by showing that the residual boundary potential contains no further (analytic) divergences. In particular, we show that the residual boundary potential admits deterministic bounds in terms of the effective potential and relative entropy terms in
F^{0}_{T}
up to an error that can be controlled by stochastic estimates (c.f. Proposition
4.3
). We will actually show these bounds also hold for the residual boundary potential at
T=\infty
, even though we have not yet defined the variational problem at
T=\infty
. Let us write
\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}
and
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{w}
for the spaces
\mathcal{L}^{0}
and
\mathcal{H}^{0}
endowed with their respective weak topologies.

Proposition 4.10
.

There exists
p_{0}\in[1,\infty)
such that for every
\delta>0
, there exists
C_{\delta}>0
such that for every
T\in(0,\infty]
and
(\Xi^{0},u)\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}
,

|\Phi^{0}_{T}(\Xi^{0},u)|\leq C_{\delta}\|\Xi^{0}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}}^{p_{0}}+\delta\left(\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\|\ell^{0}_{T}(\Xi^{0},u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\right).

(4.44)

Furthermore, assume that
\Xi_{T}^{0}\rightarrow\Xi_{\infty}^{0}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}
,
u^{T}\rightharpoonup u^{\infty}
in
\mathcal{L}_{w}^{0}
, and
\ell^{0}_{T}(\Xi_{T}^{0},u^{T})\rightharpoonup\ell^{0}_{\infty}(\Xi_{\infty}^{0},u^{\infty})
in
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{w}
. Then

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\Phi^{0}_{T}(\Xi_{T}^{0},u^{T})=\Phi^{0}_{\infty}(\Xi_{\infty}^{0},u^{\infty}).

(4.45)

We begin with a preliminary analytical lemma.

Lemma 4.11
.

For every
\delta>0
sufficiently small there exists
C>0
such that, for every
T>0
,

\Big|\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}\rrbracket(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u))^{2}dx\Big|\\
\leq C\||\tau|^{1/2-\delta}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}(\tau,\cdot)\rrbracket\|_{L^{4}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2+\delta}_{z}}\||\tau|^{-1/2+\delta}\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{1/2-\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}},

(4.46)

and

\Big|\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}\overline{H}W_{T}^{0}\cdot(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u))^{3}dx\Big|\leq C\|\overline{H}W_{T}^{0}\|_{L^{8(1-\delta)}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2+\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|^{\frac{1/2-\delta}{1-\delta}}_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{1-\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|^{{2+\frac{1/2}{1-\delta}}}_{L^{4}}.

(4.47)

Proof.

Consider the first inequality. We will split the integral on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
into an integral over
[-1,1]
and an integral on the
\tau+\mathbb{T}^{2}
slices. Fix such a
\tau\neq 0
. Then by duality and the fractional Leibniz rule,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}&\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}(\tau,\cdot)\rrbracket(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot))^{2}dz\Big|\\
&\qquad\leq\|\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}(\tau,\cdot)\rrbracket\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1/2+\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot)\|_{H^{1/2-\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{4}_{z}}.\end{split}

(4.48)

Hence

\eqref{eq: bdry quadratic 1}\leq|\tau|^{1/2-\delta}\|\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}(\tau,\cdot)\rrbracket\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1/2+\delta}_{z}}\frac{1}{|\tau|^{1/2-\delta}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot)\|_{H^{1/2-\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{4}_{z}}

(4.49)

The first inequality now follows from integrating in the
\tau
-variable and using Hölder’s inequality.

Let us now analyze the second inequality. Fix a
\tau
-slice as above. By duality, the fractional Leibniz rule applied twice, and interpolation, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}

\displaystyle\overline{H}W_{T}^{0}(\tau,\cdot)\cdot(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot))^{3}dz\Big|

(4.50)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\|\overline{H}W_{T}^{0}(\tau,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1/2+\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot)\|_{H^{1-\delta}_{z}}^{\frac{1/2-\delta}{1-\delta}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{4}_{z}}^{2+\frac{1/2}{1-\delta}}.

(4.51)

The second inequality follows by integrating in the
\tau
variable and using Hölder’s inequality.
∎

Let us now turn to the proof of Proposition
4.10
.

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

By Lemma
4.5
, we have that (
4.43
) holds with
\ell^{0}_{T}
as above and

\Phi^{0}_{T}=\int_{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}6\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}\rrbracket(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u))^{2}+4\overline{H}W_{T}^{0}\cdot(\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u))^{3}dx.

(4.52)

In addition, by Lemma
4.11
we have that

\displaystyle|\Phi^{0}_{T}|

\displaystyle\leq C\||\tau|^{1/2-\delta}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}(\tau,\cdot)\rrbracket\|_{L^{4}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2+\delta}_{z}}

(4.53)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\||\tau|^{-1/2+\delta}\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{1/2-\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}

(4.54)

\displaystyle\quad+C\|\overline{H}W_{T}^{0}\|_{L^{8(1-\delta)}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2+\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{1-\delta}_{z}}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}.

(4.55)

Let us define

\mathbb{W}^{0,3}_{T}:=Z_{T}^{0}(J_{\bullet}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}\llbracket(HW_{\bullet}^{0})^{3}\rrbracket)

(4.56)

and hence

\overline{H}Z_{T}(u)=-4\overline{H}\mathbb{W}_{T}^{0.3}+\overline{H}Z_{T}(\ell_{T}(u)).

(4.57)

Using the
the properties of
Z_{T}^{0}
, and that
\tau\mapsto\tau^{-1/2+\delta}
is in
L^{2}([-1,1])
, we
obtain

\||\tau|^{-1/2+\delta}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{1/2-\delta}_{z}}\leq C(\|\mathbb{W}^{0,3}_{T}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2-\delta}_{z}}+\|\ell_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{z}}).

(4.58)

Furthermore, by (
C.4
) and the estimate above,

\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{1-\delta}_{z}}\leq C\Big(\|\mathbb{W}^{0,3}_{T}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2-2\delta}_{z}}+\|\ell_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{z}}\Big).

(4.59)

Thus by applying Young’s inequality to the estimates from Lemma
4.11
, we have shown that for any
\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists
c_{\delta}>0

and
p,q\in(1,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle|\Phi^{0}_{T}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\delta}\Big(\|\overline{H}W_{T}^{0}\|^{p}_{L^{8(1-\delta)}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}_{z}^{-1/2+\delta}}+\||\tau|^{1/2-\delta}\llbracket(\overline{H}W_{T}^{0})^{2}\rrbracket\|_{L^{4}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2+\delta}_{z}}^{q}+\|\mathbb{W}_{T}^{0,3}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2-\delta}_{z}}^{2}\Big)

(4.60)

\displaystyle\qquad+\delta\Big(\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{z}}^{2}\Big).

(4.61)

Now using the difinition of
\Xi^{0}_{T}
we have thus established (
4.44
).

The final property (
4.45
) follows from Lemma
4.11
and the observation that
\Phi^{0}_{T}
is a sum of multilinear terms in
\Xi^{0}_{T},Z^{0}_{T}
.
∎

4.3.3
Limiting renormalized variational problem and ultraviolet stability

Let us now define the natural limiting Laplace transform
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{\infty}(f)
in terms of a variational problem.

Definition 4.12
.

For every
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, define
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{\infty}(f)
by the variational formula

\mathcal{-}\log\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{\infty}(f):=\inf_{u\in\mathbb{L}^{0}}\mathbb{F}^{0,f}_{\infty}(u).

(4.62)

Here, we set the infimum to be
\infty
if it does not exist.

Let us now show that the bounds we have already obtained allow us to bound
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)
uniformly in
T
– so called
ultraviolet stability
for the measures
(\nu^{0}_{T})
. From this, one can deduce tightness of the measures
(\nu^{0}_{T})
. Note that this is insufficient for our purposes, we require full convergence.

Proposition 4.13
.

There exists
c,C>0
such that for every
T\in(0,\infty]
and every
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,

\exp(-C-c\|f\|_{H^{-1/2+\kappa_{0}^{\prime}}_{z}}^{2})\leq\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)\leq\exp(C+c\|f\|_{H^{-1/2+\kappa_{0}^{\prime}}_{z}}^{2}).

(4.63)

Proof.

Let us assume
f=0
as the case of
f
non-zero follows easily from this case by additionally using Lemma
4.4
and duality. Let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small. Then by Propositions
4.9
and
4.10
, together with the definition of
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{\infty}
in (
4.62
), we have that there exists
C_{\delta}>0
and
p_{0}\in[1,\infty)
such that for every
u\in\mathbb{L}^{0}
,

\mathbb{F}^{0,f}_{T}(u)\geq-C_{\delta}\mathbb{E}[\|\Xi^{0}\|^{p_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}}]+(1-\delta)\mathbb{E}\left[\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell^{0}_{T}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right].

(4.64)

In particular, by Proposition
4.6
and positivity of the terms on the righthand side, there exists
C>0
such that

-\log\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)\geq-C.

(4.65)

Let us now consider the reverse inequality. We may simply choose
u
such that
\ell^{0}_{T}(u)=0
. It is then not hard to show, by similar arguments (except we use Proposition
4.6
again instead of positivity of terms) that, up to a redefinition of
C
,

-\log\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)\leq C.

(4.66)

∎

4.4
Convergence of
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)
and proof of Theorem
3.14

Let us not turn our attention to the convergence of
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{T}(f)
. By Proposition
4.9
, we have reduced it to showing that the infima of the renormalized boundary cost functions converge. The renormalized cost functions have themselves a natural pointwise limit (c.f. the second statement in Proposition
4.10
) and therefore we will show that the infima at
T<\infty
converge to the infima of the latter (at
T=\infty
). We will do so by using the framework of
\Gamma
-convergence. In order to facilitate this, at the cost of certain technicalities, we will instead work with the space of joint laws of
(\Xi,u)
since it has better compactness properties.

Let us consider the following space of joint laws

\mathcal{X}^{0}:=\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}):\exists u\in\mathbb{L}^{0},\,\mu={\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\Xi^{0}_{\infty}(X),u)\text{ and }\mathbb{E}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}]<\infty\}.

(4.67)

Let us emphasize that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}
is endowed with the strong topology,
\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}
is the space
\mathcal{L}^{0}
endowed with the weak topology, and the laws are considered on the product space (equipped with the product topology). We will view
\mathcal{X}^{0}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{0}
, where

\mathcal{Y}^{0}:=\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}):\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}<\infty\}.

(4.68)

We endow
\mathcal{X}^{0}
with the following notion of convergence. A sequence
(\mu_{n})\subset\mathcal{X}^{0}
converges to a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{Y}^{0}
, written as
\mu_{n}\rightarrow\mu
, if:

(i)

The measures converge weakly
\mu_{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
in
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}
.

(ii)

The second moments are uniformly bounded:
\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{n}}\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}<\infty
.

We let
\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{0}
denote the sequential closure of
\mathcal{X}^{0}
in the space
\mathcal{Y}^{0}
.

Let us now embed the renormalized variational problem in this space of laws. For every
T\in(0,\infty]
, define the functions
{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}^{0,f}_{T}:\mathcal{Y}^{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Big[F^{0,f}_{T}(\Xi^{0}_{T}(X^{0}),u)\Big],\qquad(\Xi^{0}(X^{0}),u)\sim\mu.

(4.69)

As above, we set
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,0}_{T}=:\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0}_{T}
.
It is easy to see that for every
T\in(0,\infty]
,

\inf_{u\in\mathbb{L}^{0}}\mathbb{F}^{0,f}_{T}(u)=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{X}^{0}}{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu).

(4.70)

In order to prove Theorem
3.14
, we will show that
\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{\infty}(f)
is nontrivial for every
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and that the infima of
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}
converge to the infima of
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}
. We aim to show that the infima are all located on the
same
sequentially compact set as
T\rightarrow\infty
.

We will begin by showing that the infimum can be taken over the closure for every
T\in(0,\infty]
.
This will be contained in two technical lemmas whose proof we will postpone to Section
4.5
.
The first lemma deals with the case
T<\infty
and mostly concerns regularity properties of the renormalized cost function under the notion of sequential convergence.
The main difficulty with these types of regularity under approximation results is that our setup, which was formulated to treat the case
T\rightarrow\infty
, means that a priori any element in
\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
can be approximated with measures in
\mathcal{X}^{0}
, but only with uniform control in
\mathcal{L}^{0}
. This is problematic given the
\mathcal{H}
-norm term in the cost function. In the case
T<\infty
, however, the variational problem is not singular – we recall that it is supported on
\mathbb{H}^{0}
(almost sure regularity in
\mathcal{H}
) and not just
\mathbb{L}^{0}
(almost sure regularity in
\mathcal{L}^{0}
).

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
T>0
. For every
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
and every
K\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, there exists
\mu_{K}\in\mathcal{X}^{0}
such that
\mu_{K}\rightarrow\mu
and

|\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu)-\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu_{K})|<1/K.

(4.71)

We now turn to the second technical lemma, which addresses the case
T=\infty
. In this case, we cannot use any regularity boost to
u
since the remainder term
\ell^{0}_{\infty}
is no longer a mapping on
\mathcal{H}
. We will therefore have to do a more delicate analysis of the regularizing properties of
\ell^{0}_{\infty}
– see Section
4.5
, particularly Lemma
4.20
.

Lemma 4.15
.

For every
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
and every
K\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, there exists
\mu_{K}\in\mathcal{X}^{0}
such that
\mu_{K}\rightarrow\mu
and

|\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu)-\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu_{K})|<1/K.

(4.72)

and

\lim_{T\to\infty}{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu_{K})={\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu_{K})

(4.73)

As a direct corollary of Lemmas
4.14
and
4.15
, we the minimization can be taken over the closure at both
T<\infty
and
T=\infty
.

Corollary 4.16
.

For every
T\in(0,\infty]
, we have that

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{X}^{0}}{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu)=\inf_{\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu).

(4.74)

Let us now show that the infima of these functions concentrate on the same sequentially compact set, i.e. that they form an equicoercive family. For every
\mathrm{C}^{0}>0
, define the set
\mathcal{K}^{0}=\mathcal{K}^{0}(\mathrm{C}^{0})\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
defined by

\mathcal{K}^{0}:=\{\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}:\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}]\leq\mathrm{C}^{0}\}.

(4.75)

We claim that this
is sequentially compact in
\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
. In order to see this, let
(\mu_{n})\subset\mathcal{K}^{0}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}_{+}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}
denote the Banach space

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}_{+}:=C_{t}L^{4}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa_{0}^{\prime}/2}_{z}\times C_{t}L^{8}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa_{0}^{\prime}/2}_{z}\times C_{t}L^{1}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa_{0}^{\prime}/2}_{z}

(4.76)

Note that the balls
B_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}_{+}}(0,L)\times B_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}(0,L)
are compact in
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}_{w}^{0}
for every
L>0
by standard compact embedding theorems for Besov spaces (see
[
BCD11
]
) and the Banach-Alaouglu theorem. Hence by Markov’s inequality, the family of measures
(\mu_{n})
are tight and therefore sequentially compact by Prokhorov’s theorem
[
Bog98
, Theorem 8.6.7]
. Let
\mu
be the subsequential limit in the weak topology. Clearly
\mu\in\mathcal{K}^{0}
since
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}]\leq\mathrm{C}^{0}
by Fatou’s lemma.

Lemma 4.17
.

There exists
\mathrm{C}^{0}>0
such that for every
T\in(0,\infty]
,

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{X}^{0}}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu)=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{K}^{0}}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu),

(4.77)

where
\mathcal{K}^{0}=\mathcal{K}^{0}(\mathrm{C}^{0})
is as above.

Proof.

Note that by Sobolev embedding and Proposition
4.6
, there exists
C>0
such that for every
T\in(0,\infty]
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}]\leq C+\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}].

(4.78)

Moreover, by Proposition
4.10
, particularly (
4.64
), there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]\leq 2\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu)+C^{\prime}.

(4.79)

Hence we may deduce the existence of
\mathrm{C}^{0}
and therefore the compact set
\mathcal{K}^{0}
.
∎

We now establish that the family of functions
(\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T})

\Gamma
-converge to
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}
. This consists of proving two conditions: i) a Fatou-type inequality (
4.80
), and ii) existence of a recovery sequence (
4.81
).

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
. For every sequence
(\mu_{n})\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
such that
\mu_{n}\rightarrow\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
and every sequence
(T_{n}):T_{n}\rightarrow\infty
,

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu)\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T_{n}}(\mu_{n}).

(4.80)

Furthermore, for every sequence
(T_{n}):T_{n}\rightarrow\infty
there exists a sequence
(\mu_{n})\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}:\mu_{n}\rightarrow\mu
such that

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu)\geq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T_{n}}(\mu_{n}).

(4.81)

Thus
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}

\Gamma
-converges to
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

We will treat the case
f=0
since the general case follows with minor modifications. Fix
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
Let us first show the existence of a recovery sequence (
4.81
). By Lemma
4.15
there exists a sequence
(\mu_{K})\subset\mathcal{X}^{0}
such that
\mu_{K}\rightarrow\mu
and
\lim_{T\to\infty}|\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0}_{T}(\mu_{K})-\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0}_{\infty}(\mu_{K})|=0
for every
K\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\lim_{K\to\infty}|\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0}_{\infty}(\mu_{K})-\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0}_{\infty}(\mu)|=0
. In particular, given any sequence
(T_{n}):T_{n}\rightarrow\infty
, we can construct a subsequence of the
(\mu_{K})
satisfying (
4.81
).

We now turn to the Fatou-type inequality (
4.80
). Let
