(\mu_{n})\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
be any sequence such that
\mu_{n}\rightarrow\mu
and let us also fix
(T_{n}):T_{n}\rightarrow\infty
. Since
\mu_{n}\rightarrow\mu
, we have that
{\rm Law}_{\mu_{n}}(u)\rightarrow{\rm Law}_{\mu}(u)
weakly on
\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}
. Furthermore, the marginals on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}
are exactly the same for every
n
– in particular Proposition
4.6
applies. We need to address convergence of the remainder terms
\ell^{0}_{T}
– c.f. the conditions for pointwise convergence of the boundary residual potential in Proposition
4.10
. We may assume that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{T_{n}}^{0}(\mu_{n})<\infty
, otherwise the statement is trivial.
Without loss of generality we may pass to a subsequence realizing the liminf (which we do not relabel). Thus we have that
\sup_{n}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0}_{T_{n}}(\mu_{n})<\infty
, which implies that

\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{n}}\left[\|\overline{H}Z^{0}_{T_{n}}(u)\|^{4}_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}+\|\ell^{0}_{T_{n}}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\right]<\infty.

(4.82)

Since we already have
{\rm Law}_{\mu_{n}}(\ell^{0}_{T}(u))\rightarrow{\rm Law}_{\mu}(\ell^{0}_{\infty}(u))
weakly in
\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}
, by compactness we now have
{\rm Law}_{\mu_{n}}(\ell^{0}_{T}(u))\rightarrow{\rm Law}_{\mu}(\ell^{0}_{\infty}(u))
weakly in
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{w}
.

By Skorokhod’s theorem
[
Jak98
]
, up to a subsequence (not relabelled), there exists a probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
and random variables
\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{T_{n}},\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{\infty},\tilde{u}_{n},\tilde{u},\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{T_{n}},\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{\infty}
such that we have

\displaystyle{\rm Law}_{\mu_{n}}(\Xi^{0}_{T_{n}}(X^{0}),u,\ell^{0}_{T_{n}}(u))

\displaystyle={\rm Law}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{T_{n}},\tilde{u}_{n},\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{T_{n}}),

(4.83)

\displaystyle{\rm Law}_{\mu}(\Xi^{0}_{\infty}(X^{0}),u,\ell^{0}_{\infty}(u))

\displaystyle={\rm Law}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{\infty},\tilde{u},\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{\infty}),

(4.84)

and additionally such that
\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{T_{n}}\rightarrow\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{\infty}
almost surely in
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}
,
\tilde{u}_{n}\rightarrow\tilde{u}
almost surely in
\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}
, and
\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{T_{n}}\rightarrow\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{\infty}
almost surely in
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{w}
. Furthermore, we have the following equalities almost surely.

(\tilde{u}_{n})_{t}:=-4J_{t}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0,3}_{T_{n}})_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq T_{n}}+(\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{T_{n}})_{t},\quad\tilde{u}_{t}:=-4J_{t}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0,3}_{\infty})_{t}+(\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{\infty})_{t}.

(4.85)

Observe that we have
{\rm Law}_{\mu_{n}}(u)={\rm Law}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\tilde{u}_{n})
and
{\rm Law}_{\mu}(u)={\rm Law}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\tilde{u})
and that
\tilde{u}_{n}\rightarrow\tilde{u}
almost surely in
\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}
.

Writing
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
to denote expectation with respect to
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
, we have that

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0}_{T_{n}}(\mu_{n})=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Phi^{0}_{T_{n}}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{T_{n}},\tilde{u}_{n})+\|\overline{H}Z^{0}_{T}(\tilde{u}_{n})\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{T_{n}}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\right].

(4.86)

By Proposition
4.1
and Fatou’s lemma, we have that

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi^{0}_{\infty}(\tilde{\Xi}_{\infty}^{0},\tilde{u})]\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi^{0}_{T_{n}}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{T_{n}},\tilde{u}_{n})].

(4.87)

Furthermore, by lower semicontinuity of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}
with respect to convergence in
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{w}
and Fatou’s lemma,

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\|\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{\infty}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}]\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[\|\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{T_{n}}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}].

(4.88)

It remains to treat the
L^{4}
term, the effective boundary potential. It is slightly awkward to consider it as a map of the
\tilde{u}_{n}
directly, so let us instead use the equalities in law (
4.85
) and view it as a mapping
(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{T_{n}},\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{T_{n}})\mapsto\|\overline{H}Z^{0}_{T_{n}}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{T_{n}},\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{T_{n}})\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}
instead. Observing that, by Sobolev embedding,

\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(\tilde{u}_{n})\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{T}J_{t}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0,3}_{T_{n}})_{t}dt\right\|_{L^{4}_{z}}+\|Z^{0}_{T}(\tilde{\ell}^{0}_{T_{n}})\|_{H^{1/2}_{z}}

(4.89)

we conclude with help of Lemma
4.4
that the aforementioned map is lower semicontinuous on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{H}^{0}_{w}
. Thus by Fatou’s lemma, we have that

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(\tilde{u})\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}]\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(\tilde{u}_{n})\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}].

(4.90)

Combining (
4.87
), (
4.88
), (
4.90
) yields (
4.80
).
∎

We will now prove our main theorem of the section.

Proof of Theorem
3.14
.

By Lemma
4.18
, we have that the functions
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}

\Gamma
-converge to
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}
. By Lemma
4.17
and the
11
11

11

Strictly speaking, we have not specified a topology but only convergence of sequences. However, one can check that the proof of the fundamental theorem of
\Gamma
-convergence follows in our setting.
fundamental theorem of
\Gamma
-convergence
[
DM93
]
, we have that the minimizers converge. The theorem follows by the identity (
4.70
) and Corollary
4.16
.

Let us now turn our attention to items (i)-(iv). The fact that
\nu^{0}=\text{Law}_{\mu_{\infty}}(W_{\infty}^{0}+Z_{\infty}^{0}(u))
and
{\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(W^{0}_{\infty})=\mu^{0}
, where
\mu_{\infty}
is a minimizer of
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0}_{\infty}
,
follows by the same arguments as in
[
BG23
, Lemma 11 and Theorem 12]
. Item (iii) is implied by (iv), so let us establish that.
Note that equicoercivity it follows that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\infty}}[\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}(u)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}]<\infty
and that there exists
C>0
such that

\|Z_{\infty}^{0}(u)\|_{H^{1/2-\delta}_{z}}\leq C(\|\mathbb{W}^{0,3}_{\infty}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2-\delta}_{z}}+\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}).

(4.91)

Item (iv) follows.

∎

4.5
Proof of approximation lemmas

We now turn to the proofs of Lemmas
4.14
and
4.15
, thereby closing the proof of Theorem
3.14
. As a preliminary, we will need a technical result concerning approximation of measures in
\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
with measures of bounded support and sufficient moment properties.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
T\in(0,\infty]
. Let
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}_{z}}^{4}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}]<\infty
. Then there exist sequences
(\mu_{L})_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
and
(\mu_{L,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\subset\mathcal{X}^{0}
such that:

(i)

\mu_{L}\rightharpoonup\mu
on
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}
as
L\rightarrow\infty
and
\mu_{L,n}\rightharpoonup\mu_{L}
on
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathcal{L}_{w}^{0}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

(ii)

For every
L\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have that
\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}\leq L

\mu_{L}
-almost surely. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have that
\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}\leq L

\mu_{L,n}
-almost surely.

(iii)

We have the convergence properties:

\displaystyle\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L}}[\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}],

(4.92)

\displaystyle\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L}}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}].

(4.93)

Proof.

The proof is a slight modification of the proof of
[
BG20
, Lemma 14]
, we omit the details.
∎

We will also need the following regularizations.

(i)

Space regularization.
Let
\eta^{0}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and let
(\eta^{0}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
denote the corresponding family of (spatial) mollifiers. Given
u\in\mathcal{L}^{0}
, Denote by
{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}:\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{0}
the linear map defined for every
u\in\mathcal{L}^{0}
by

{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}(u):=u\ast_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\eta^{0}_{\varepsilon},

(4.94)

where
*_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}
denotes spatial convolution on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. This map is continuous as a map from
\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}
and from
\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}_{w}
.

(ii)

Space-time regularization.
Let
\zeta^{0}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
and let
(\zeta^{0}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be the corresponding family of (space-time) mollifiers. Denote by
{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}:\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{0}
the linear map defined for every
u\in\mathcal{L}^{0}
by

{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}(u)_{t}:=e^{-\varepsilon t}u\ast_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\zeta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot),\qquad\forall t\geq 0.

(4.95)

This map is continuous as a map from
\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}
.

We now turn to the proof of the approximation lemma when
T<\infty
.

Proof of Lemma
4.14
.

Let
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
be such that
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu)<\infty
. Recall that by (
4.4
) for
T<\infty
, we have that

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu):=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\langle f,W_{T}^{0}+Z_{T}^{0}(u)\rangle_{L^{2}_{z}}+\mathcal{V}^{0}_{T}(W_{T}^{0},Z_{T}^{0}(u))+\frac{1}{2}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\right].

(4.96)

First, observe that by duality and Lemma
4.4

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\langle f,W_{T}^{0}+Z_{T}^{0}(u)\rangle_{L^{2}_{z}}\right]\geq-\|f\|_{H^{-1/2}_{z}}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}.

(4.97)

Therefore, by Cauchy’s inequality and since
\mathcal{V}_{T}^{0}(W_{T},Z_{T}^{0}(u))
is bounded from below, we deduce that
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu)<\infty
implies
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}]<\infty
. Next, we may write
\mathcal{V}_{T}^{0}(W_{T}^{0},Z_{T}^{0}(u))=\Phi^{0}_{T}+\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}
. For every
\varepsilon,\delta>0
sufficiently small, by Hölder’s inequality, there exists
C>0
such that

|\Phi^{0}_{T}|\leq N_{T}\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{3},

(4.98)

where
N_{T}
is an explicit positive random variable consisting of
L^{p}
norms of the cutoff Wick powers (we are using that when
T<\infty
this is smooth).

We now want to define a sufficient approximation of
\mu
in
\mathcal{X}
such that we have convergence of
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}
along this sequence. We note that approximating sequences defined by the closure are insufficient to ensure this since it only gives us information about the
\mathcal{L}^{0}
-norm of
u
. Hence we opt to use Lemma
4.19
. Let
(\mu_{L})_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
and
(\mu_{L,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\subset\mathcal{X}^{0}
be the sequences constructed in Lemma
4.19
.

We will regularize these two sequences and use that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}]<\infty
to obtain the existence of two further sequences:

(a)

(\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon})\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
such that

\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon})=\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu^{\varepsilon}),\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu^{\varepsilon})=\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu).

(4.99)

(b)

(\mu_{L,n}^{\varepsilon,\delta})\subset\mathcal{X}^{0}
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu_{L,n}^{\varepsilon,\delta})=\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon,\delta}),\quad\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon,\delta})=\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon}).

(4.100)

The desired sequence can then be constructed as a subsequence of
(\mu^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{L,n})
by taking appropriate diagonal subsequences of the above limits.Let us now construct said sequences. We begin by defining the regularizations:

\displaystyle\mu^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=({\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon})_{*}\mu,

\displaystyle\mu^{\varepsilon}_{L}

\displaystyle:=({\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon})_{*}\mu_{L},

(4.101)

\displaystyle\mu^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{L}

\displaystyle:=({\rm reg}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\delta})_{*}\mu^{\varepsilon}_{L},

\displaystyle\mu^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{L,n}

\displaystyle:=({\rm reg}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\delta})_{*}({\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon})_{*}\mu_{L,n}.

(4.102)

Let us first establish (
4.99
). By the continuity properties of
{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}
, we have that
\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\varepsilon}
as
L\rightarrow\infty
on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{H}^{0}
. By Skorokhod embedding we may write
\mu^{\varepsilon}_{L}={\rm Law}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0},\tilde{u}_{L}^{\varepsilon})
and
\mu^{\varepsilon}={\rm Law}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0},\tilde{u}^{\varepsilon})
on some probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
such that
\tilde{u}_{L}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\tilde{u}^{\varepsilon}
almost surely in
\mathcal{H}^{0}
. Writing

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon})=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[F^{0,f}_{T}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0},\tilde{u}_{L}^{\varepsilon})],\quad\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu^{\varepsilon})=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[F^{0,f}_{T}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0},\tilde{u}^{\varepsilon})],

(4.103)

the almost sure convergence of
(\tilde{\Xi},\tilde{u}^{\varepsilon}_{L})\rightarrow(\tilde{\Xi},\tilde{u}^{\varepsilon})
ensures that
F_{T}^{0,f}(\tilde{\Xi},\tilde{u}_{L})\rightarrow F^{0,f}_{T}(\tilde{\Xi},\tilde{u})
almost surely (thanks to the bounds established at the beginning of the proof).

We now wish to pass the limit inside the expectation. Recall that by construction, the sequence
(\mu_{L})
satisfies

\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L}}[\|Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}}^{4}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}}^{4}],\quad\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L}}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}].

(4.104)

By the continuity properties of
{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}
, we have that

\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon}}[\|Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}}^{4}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\varepsilon}}[\|Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}}^{4}],\quad\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon}}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\varepsilon}}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}].

(4.105)

Therefore, in order to conclude convergence of the cost functions, it is sufficient to show that the remainder terms in (
4.96
) are uniformly integrable. To do this, we show that they admit
1+\varepsilon
moments, provided
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small. Indeed, by Young’s inequalities applied to (
4.97
) and (
4.98
), and using hypercontractivity, there exists
c_{T}>0
such that

\sup_{L}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[|\langle f,W_{T}^{0}+Z_{T}^{0}(\tilde{u}^{\varepsilon}_{L})\rangle_{L^{2}_{z}}|^{1+\varepsilon}]\leq c_{T}+\sup_{L}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\|\tilde{u}^{\varepsilon}_{L}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}]<\infty,

(4.106)

and

\sup_{L}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[|\Phi^{0}_{T}|^{1+\varepsilon}]\leq c_{T}+\sup_{L}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}]<\infty.

(4.107)

Thus, by Vitali’s convergence theorem, we have that

\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu^{\varepsilon}_{L})=\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\infty}^{0,f}(\mu^{\varepsilon}).

(4.108)

For the removal of
\varepsilon
, let us first observe that since
\|{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}
and that
{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}u\rightarrow u
in
\mathcal{H}^{0}
, by dominated convergence we have that

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\varepsilon}}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}].

(4.109)

For the convergence of the
L^{4}
norm, note that
Z_{T}^{0}({\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}u)=\eta^{0}_{\varepsilon}\ast_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}Z_{T}^{0}(u)
. Since
\|\eta^{0}_{\varepsilon}\ast_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)-\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}}\rightarrow 0
, and in particular
\|\eta^{0}_{\varepsilon}\ast_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}}\leq 2\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}}
. Hence by dominated convergence we have that

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\varepsilon}}[\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}].

(4.110)

Then by arguing verbatim as above (i.e. Skorokhod embedding and then a uniform integrability argument), we obtain

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu^{\varepsilon})=\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu).

(4.111)

Then (
4.108
) and (
4.111
) establish (
4.99
).

We now turn to (
4.100
). Let us fix
L
and
\varepsilon
. Note that

\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L,n}}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}]<\infty\Rightarrow\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L,n}^{\varepsilon}}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}]<\infty,

(4.112)

and also that
\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leq L

\mu_{L,n}^{\varepsilon}
-almost surely. Furthermore, by continuity properties of
{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}
, we have that
\mu_{L,n}^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\mu_{L}^{\varepsilon}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{H}^{0}_{w}
. We will need to upgrade this to convergence in the norm topology on
\mathcal{H}^{0}
, which is done by the space-time regularization map. Indeed, by properties of
{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\delta}
, we have that
\mu_{L,n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\rightharpoonup\mu_{L,n}^{\varepsilon}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{H}^{0}
. A similar Skorokhod embedding argument as above – with a simplification due to the almost sure boundedness, thereby removing the need for uniform integrability – then yields (
4.100
). We omit the details.
∎

We now turn to the case
T=\infty
. Here, the variational problem is singular and so we do not have the same regularizing properties (from
\mathcal{L}^{0}
to
\mathcal{H}^{0}
). thus we will have to study the regularizing properties of the remainder map. The key properties we require are contained in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.20
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, define the boundary remainder map
{\rm rem}^{0}_{\varepsilon}:\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{0}
for every
(\Xi^{0},u)\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}
by

{\rm rem}^{0}_{\varepsilon}(\Xi^{0},u)_{t}:=-4J_{t}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}\Xi^{0,3}_{t}+{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}\Big(4J_{t}^{0}\overline{H}^{*}\Xi^{0,3}_{t}+\ell^{0}_{\infty}(u)_{t}\Big),\quad\forall t\geq 0.

(4.113)

This a continuous linear map
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{0}
and also as a map from
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{0}_{w}
. It satisfies

\ell^{0}_{\infty}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}(\Xi^{0},u))={\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}\left(\ell^{0}_{\infty}(\Xi^{0},u)\right).

(4.114)

Furthermore, the map satisfies the following properties: there exists
C>0
and for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that:

(i)

\|{\rm rem}^{0}_{\varepsilon}(\Xi^{0},u)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}\leq C(\|\Xi^{0}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}})
.

(ii)

for
T\in(0,\infty]
,
\|\overline{H}Z^{0}_{T}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\varepsilon}(\Xi^{0},u))\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq C(\|\Xi^{0}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}}+\|\overline{H}Z^{0}_{T}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})})
.

(iii)

\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}^{0}(\Xi^{0},u))\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leq C_{\varepsilon}(\|\Xi^{0}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}})
.

(iv)

\|{\rm rem}^{0}_{\varepsilon}(\Xi^{0},u)-u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}\to 0
if
u\in\mathcal{L}^{0}
and
\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\varepsilon}(\Xi^{0},u))-\ell_{\infty}^{0}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\to 0
if

\ell_{\infty}^{0}(u)\in\mathcal{H}^{0}
.

Proof.

Properties (i) and (ii) are trivial by using linearity of
{\rm reg}_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}
. Property (iii) follows from (
4.114
) and the definition of
{\rm reg_{\mathbb{T}^{2},\varepsilon}}
. Property (iv) can be easily checked from the definitions.
∎

In the case that
\Xi^{0}=\Xi^{0}(X^{0})
, we will omit it from notation in
{\rm rem}
. We now prove our main approximation theorem when
T=\infty
.

Proof of Lemma
4.15
.

For every
\kappa>0
, let us define
\mu^{\kappa}:=({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa})_{*}\mu
.
First we show the limit
\lim_{\kappa\to 0}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu^{\kappa})=\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu)
.
Note that we can represent the prelimit as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu^{\kappa})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Big[\langle f,Z^{0}_{\infty}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}u)\rangle_{L^{2}_{z}}+\Phi^{0,f}_{\infty}(\Xi^{0}_{\infty}(X^{0}),{\rm rem}_{\kappa}^{0}u)

(4.115)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\|\overline{H}Z_{\infty}^{0}({\rm rem}_{\kappa}^{0}u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}({\rm rem}_{\kappa}u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\Big].

(4.116)

Observe that
\Phi_{\infty}^{0,f}(\Xi^{0}_{\infty},{\rm rem}_{\kappa}\,u)\to\Phi_{\infty}^{0,f}(\Xi^{0}_{\infty},u)
since it is a sum of multilinear bounded functional on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{H}^{0}
(where we view
\Phi_{\infty}^{0}
as a function of
\ell^{0}_{\infty}
by a change of variables.)
From this we can see that the random variables under the expectation converge almost surely as
\kappa\to 0
to

\langle f,Z^{0}_{\infty}(u)\rangle_{L^{2}_{z}}+\Phi^{0,f}_{\infty}(\Xi^{0}_{\infty},u)+\|\overline{H}Z_{\infty}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}.

(4.117)

By properties (ii),(iii), and (iv) of Lemma
4.20
we may thus apply dominated convergence and conclude that
\lim_{\kappa\to 0}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu^{\kappa})=\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu)
.

Now let
(\mu_{L})_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
and
(\mu_{L,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\subset\mathcal{X}^{0}
be the sequence of measures constructed in Lemma
4.19
. Let us define
\mu^{\kappa}_{L}:=({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa})_{*}\mu_{L}
and
\mu^{\kappa}_{L,n}:=({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa})_{*}\mu_{L,n}
.
We will prove that

\lim_{L\to\infty}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu_{L}^{\kappa})=\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu^{\kappa})
– by similar arguments one can also show
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu_{L,n}^{\kappa})=\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu^{\kappa}_{L})
.
To the former claim, note that for every
\kappa>0
there exists
C_{\kappa}>0
such that

\|\overline{H}Z_{\infty}^{0}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\|\ell_{\infty}^{0}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\\
\leq C_{\kappa}\left(\|\overline{H}Z_{\infty}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}+\|\Xi^{0}_{\infty}\|^{4}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}}\right).

(4.118)

Recall also that

\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L}}\left[\|\overline{H}Z_{\infty}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\|\overline{H}Z_{\infty}^{0}(u)\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}^{2}\right].

(4.119)

By Skorohod’s theorem we can find a probability space
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
, whose expectation we denote by
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
, and random variables
\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{L},\tilde{u}_{L}
such that
{\rm Law}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{L},\tilde{u}_{L})=\mu_{L}

and
\lim_{L\to\infty}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{L},\tilde{u}_{L})=(\tilde{\Xi}^{0},\tilde{u})
almost surely in
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}\times\mathcal{L}^{0}
. Thus we have the representation

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu^{\kappa}_{L})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\langle f,Z^{0}_{\infty}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}\tilde{u}_{L})\rangle_{L^{2}_{z}}+\Phi^{0}_{\infty}(\tilde{\Xi^{0}_{L}},{\rm rem}_{\kappa}^{0}\tilde{u}_{L})

(4.120)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\|\overline{H}Z_{\infty}^{0}({\rm rem}_{\kappa}^{0}\tilde{u}_{L})\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}\tilde{u}_{L})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\Bigg],

(4.121)

Since
\|\overline{H}Z_{\infty}^{0}({\rm rem}_{\kappa}^{0}\tilde{u}_{L})\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell^{0}_{\infty}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}\tilde{u}_{L})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}
is positive and converges and expectation it also converges in
L^{1}
, and so we may use it as a dominating function (recall that dominated convergence holds also if the dominating function is not fixed but converges in
L^{1}
).
Invoking Lemma
4.11
and (
4.118
) we can thus apply dominated convergence to obtain
\lim_{L\to\infty}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu_{L}^{\kappa})=\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu^{\kappa})
.

Finally it remains to prove

\lim_{T\to\infty}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu_{L}^{\kappa})=\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{\infty}(\mu^{\kappa}_{L})
. Again we may represent

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{0,f}_{T}(\mu^{\kappa}_{L})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\langle f,Z^{0}_{T}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}\tilde{u}_{L})\rangle_{L^{2}_{z}}+\Phi^{0,f}_{T}(\tilde{\Xi_{L}^{0}},{\rm rem}_{\kappa}^{0}\tilde{u}_{L})

(4.122)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\|\overline{H}Z_{T}^{0}({\rm rem}_{\kappa}^{0}\tilde{u}_{L})\|_{L^{4}([-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell^{0}_{T}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}\tilde{u}_{L})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\Bigg],

(4.123)

Now
Z_{T}^{0}({\rm rem}_{\kappa}^{0}\tilde{u}_{L}),\ell^{0}_{T}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}\tilde{u}_{L})
converge to
Z_{\infty}^{0}({\rm rem}_{\kappa}^{0}\tilde{u}_{L}),\ell^{0}_{\infty}({\rm rem}^{0}_{\kappa}\tilde{u}_{L})
in
L^{4},\mathcal{H}
, respectively, almost surely in
\mu_{L}
and the corresponding norms are bounded by
\|\tilde{u}_{L}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{0}}\leq L
, by the properties of
{\rm rem^{0}}
and
Z_{T}^{0}
(and harmonic extension).
From the definition of
\Phi^{0,f}
and the bounds from Lemma
4.11
it is not hard to see that
\lim_{T\to\infty}\Phi_{T}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{L},\tilde{u}_{L})=\Phi_{\infty}(\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{L},\tilde{u}_{L})
and
\Phi_{T}(\tilde{\Xi}_{L}^{0},\tilde{u}_{L})\leq C(\|\tilde{\Xi}^{0}_{L}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{0}}^{p_{0}}+\|\overline{H}Z^{0}_{T}(\tilde{u}_{L})\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+1)
. We can also estimate the linear term similarly. Thus we get the claim by dominated convergence and Vitali’s convergence theorem.
∎

5
Algebraic renormalization of bulk amplitudes

The next four sections will be devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.18
. Without loss of generality, we will consider the case of
\sigma=\emptyset
, i.e. the amplitudes on
M
. In this section, our goal is to identify a
renormalized variational representation
of the bulk amplitudes which is amenable to study in the limit
T\rightarrow\infty
. At the end of this section, we will give a brief discussion of how the next sections play a role in the proof.

5.1
Boué-Dupuis representation of bulk amplitudes

We first recall the definition of cutoff amplitudes.
Let
T>0
. Recall from Definition
3.16
that for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, the cutoff amplitude is given by

\mathcal{A}_{T}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}):=\mathcal{A}^{\rm free}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\mathcal{E}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\mathcal{Z}_{T}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}),

(5.1)

where
\mathcal{A}^{\rm free}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
is the free amplitude defined in (
3.44
),
\mathcal{E}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
is defined in (
3.53
), and
\mathcal{Z}_{T}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
is the unnormalized Laplace transform of
f
under
\nu(\cdot\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
. In studying the limit
T\rightarrow\infty
, the free amplitude is irrelevant. Thus we will consider the twisted amplitude

\mathsf{A}_{T}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}):=\mathcal{E}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\mathcal{Z}_{T}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}).

(5.2)

To simplify further, we will consider the case
\mathsf{A}_{T}(f\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
with
f\equiv 0
since the case
f\neq 0
follows by straightforward modifications. We will henceforth just write
\mathsf{A}_{T}:=\mathsf{A}_{T}(0\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
since the boundary fields
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}
are considered fixed unless stated otherwise.

We now turn to establishing a variational representation for
\mathsf{A}_{T}
. We will again use the Boué-Dupuis formula as our starting point, just as in the preceding section and
[
BG20
]
, so we will

Let us fix
\kappa_{0}>0
and consider the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, where the sample space is
\Omega=C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{C}^{-3/2-\kappa_{0}}(M))
endowed with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra and under which the canonical coordinate process
X
under
\mathbb{P}
is an
L^{2}(M)
-Brownian motion. By abuse of notation, we will assume that
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is
\mathbb{P}
-complete and the filtration
(\mathcal{F})_{t\geq 0}
generated by
X
is augmented by
\mathbb{P}
-null sets.

Remark 5.1
.

Let us stress that we will assume that the process
X
is independent of the process
X^{0}
considered in Section
4
.

The process
X
admits a Fourier decomposition. Recall the basis
\mathcal{I}_{M}=\{\mathsf{f}_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
of Laplace eigenfunctions on
M
. Almost surely we have that

X_{t}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{n}B_{t}^{n},

(5.3)

where
\{B_{t}^{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
is a set of i.i.d. complex standard Brownian motions modulo the conjugacy constraint
B^{-n}=\overline{B^{n}}
.

We will use the above representation to embed the GFF in this Wiener space. The regularization we will use imposes a cutoff in the
z
-direction and leaves the
\tau
-direction unregularized. Let
(\rho_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be as in the boundary theory and define the multiplier
J_{t}:\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}
for every
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by

J_{t}(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi^{2}(n_{1}/2L)^{2}+4\pi^{2}(n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2})+m^{2}}}\sqrt{\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{2}((n_{2},n_{3}))}.

(5.4)

Here, by multiplier we are implicitly using that this acts on functions which can be periodized (such as those with value
0
at the ends of
M
).

Let us now define the stochastic process
(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by

W_{t}(\tau,z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{n}(\tau,z)\int_{0}^{t}J_{s}(n)dB_{s}^{n}.

(5.5)

For every
t>0
, a covariance computation yields that the above series converges almost surely in
\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa_{0}}(M)
and as
t\rightarrow\infty
we have that
W_{t}\rightarrow W_{\infty}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa_{0}},\mathbb{P})
. We have that
{\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(W_{\infty})=\mu
and so
W_{\infty}
is the GFF. Let us also note that for every
t\in(0,\infty]
, we have that the process in the
\tau
-variable has the following regularity:

\tau\mapsto W_{t}(\tau,\cdot)\in C^{1/2-\kappa_{0}}_{\tau}\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa_{0}}_{z}.

(5.6)

Finally, let us note that when
t<\infty
,
W_{t}\in C^{1/2-\alpha}(M)
and in particular is a well-defined function.

The space of drifts in the variational problem consists of progressively measurable processes that are
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. in

\mathcal{H}:=L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}.

(5.7)

We denote this space of processes by
\mathbb{H}
. For every
u\in\mathcal{H}
, consider the following map
u\mapsto Z_{t}(u)
,

Z_{t}(u):=\int_{0}^{t}J_{s}(u)ds,

(5.8)

where
J_{s}(u)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}}J_{s}(n)\hat{u}_{s}(n)\mathsf{f}_{n}
. Note that here we are abusing notation and identifying
u
with its measure-zero modification on
M
that makes it periodic.

The following is proposition is a direct consequence of the Boué-Dupuis formula
[
BD98
,
Ü14
]
.

Proposition 5.2
.

For every
T>0
,

-\log\mathsf{A}_{T}=\inf_{u\in\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}_{T}(W_{T},Z_{T}(u))+\frac{1}{2}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right],

(5.9)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{T}(W_{T},Z_{T}(u))

\displaystyle:=\int_{M}4\llbracket W_{T}^{3}\rrbracket Z_{T}(u)+6\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket Z_{T}(u)^{2}+12\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z_{T}(u)

(5.10)

\displaystyle\qquad+6\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket+4W_{T}Z_{T}(u)^{3}+12W_{T}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z_{T}(u)^{2}

(5.11)

\displaystyle\qquad+12W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket Z_{T}(u)+4W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{3}\rrbracket+Z_{T}(u)^{4}

(5.12)

\displaystyle\qquad-2\gamma_{T}W_{T}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}-2\gamma_{T}W_{T}Z_{T}(u)-\gamma_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket

(5.13)

\displaystyle\qquad-2\gamma_{T}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z_{T}(u)-\gamma_{T}Z_{T}(u)^{2}\,dx-(\delta_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})-\Delta\delta_{T}^{0})

(5.14)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{M}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket-\llbracket\overline{H}(\varphi_{-})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket-\llbracket\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket dx

(5.15)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{M\Delta\{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\}}\llbracket\overline{H}(\varphi_{-})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket+\llbracket\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket dx.

(5.16)

Remark 5.3
.

We recall that the Wick ordering of the terms
H(\varphi_{-},\varphi)_{T}
involve the covariance
C^{B}_{T}
, whereas the Wick ordering of the terms
\overline{H}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}
involve the covariance
\overline{C}^{B}_{T}
.

5.2
Setup for the renormalization

The variational problem in Proposition
5.2
contains divergences and this is seen by a power-counting heuristic. On the one hand, the regularity of Wick powers of the GFF is well-known. On the other hand, the drift terms satisfy the following regularity estimate whose proof is similar to
[
BG20
, Lemma 2]
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For every
u\in\mathcal{H}=L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
, we have that

\sup_{T}\|Z_{T}(u)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}^{2}.

(5.17)

Below, we will analyze
\mathcal{V}_{T}
term-by-term to isolate and renormalize divergences, leading us to a renormalized variational problem that we can handle. We will first identify
bulk stochastic terms
, which produce divergences even in the case of smooth or zero boundary conditions
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}
. In addition, we will use the regularity estimates developed in Section
4
to identify
boundary-bulk stochastic terms
, which produce divergences because of the rough regularity of the boundary fields (recall the notion of admissible boundary condition). As in
[
BG20
]
, we will use a combination of a paracontrolled ansatz on the drift, which induces a divergence in the drift entropy term, to twist these divergences into a form that we can renormalize by using the available mass and energy renormalization counter-terms. The goal is then to be left with remainder terms that be analytically estimated with the coercive terms, consisting of
\int_{M}Z_{T}(u)^{4}\,dx
and the residual drift entropy term left after the paracontrolled ansatz. See the end of the section, where we are more precise on the latter point. Let us emphasize that the required drift ansatz is more complicated than in the previous section on the boundary theory due to the need for mass renormalization in the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
model. Furthermore, additional boundary-dependent terms are required in the drift ansatz, which produces extra difficulties and subtleties as compared with the periodic case
[
BG20
]
.

Remark 5.5
.

Let us emphasize that analyzing the boundary-bulk stochastic terms is a significant point of departure from
[
BG20
]
. We will show that many of these terms admit a finite fractional moment – measured in the appropriate norm – uniformly in
T
. This will use the additional second moment requirement on the drift term
Z^{0}
in the definition of admissible boundary conditions. As such, these norms will be bounded almost surely in the boundary data, and we will show that this is sufficient to obtain the convergence of the bulk amplitudes (again, almost surely in the boundary data). As a consequence, the quantitative bounds we obtain on the bulk amplitudes will depend in a non-trivial way on these norms – in particular, we will obtain an exponential upper bound in terms of linear combinations of fractional moments of these norms.

Remark 5.6
.

On a technical level, in order to isolate divergences, we will use paraproduct and resonant product decompositions as done in
[
BG20
]
. It will therefore be convenient to do a periodization trick in
some
integrands, where we replace the integral over
M
by the integral over the torus
\mathcal{M}
, obtained from
M
by identifying
\partial^{-}M
and
\partial^{+}M
. Let us observe that
Z_{T}(v)
and the Wick powers of
W_{T}
are periodic thanks to the boundary values of
\{\mathsf{f}_{n}\}
. For the harmonic extension terms, there is a discontinuity at the image of
\partial^{-}M
and
\partial^{+}M
under the identification – thus we make a measure
0
modification implicitly. As we shall see in later sections, this will only cause problems when we try to estimate these terms in strictly positive regularity function spaces.

Let us now turn to isolating the divergent integrals. By the power-counting heuristic mentioned above, the singular terms in
\mathcal{V}_{T}(W_{T},Z_{T}(u))
are given by the following terms, which are arranged in terms of joint homogenity in
Z_{T}(u)
and
H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
.

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle:=\int_{M}4\llbracket W^{3}_{T}\rrbracket Z_{T}(u)\,dx,

(5.18)

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{M}12\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z_{T}(u)\,dx+\int_{\mathcal{M}}6\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket Z^{2}_{T}(u)\,dx,

(5.19)

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle:=\int_{M}12W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket Z_{T}(u)dx+4\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket Z_{T}(u)\,dx.

(5.20)

Let us stress here that we have used the periodization trick on the second term in
S_{2}
, since we will use paraproduct and resonant product decompositions.

We now turn to the counter-terms at our disposal. First, let us discuss mass renormalization. Recall that the renormalization constant
\gamma_{T}
is defined in terms of the covariance of the periodic GFF. However, the covariance of the stochastic objects above are expressible in terms of the Dirichlet GFF and therefore we will consider the latter and estimate the error term coming from the replacement. Let us define, for every
x\in M
,

\gamma_{T}^{M}(x):=-3\cdot 4^{2}\int_{M}C_{T}^{M}(x,y)^{3}

(5.21)

The mass renormalization counter-terms are contained in the term

\displaystyle U_{1}:=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}}-2\gamma_{T}^{M}W_{T}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}-2\gamma_{T}^{M}W_{T}Z_{T}(u)-\gamma_{T}^{M}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket

(5.22)

\displaystyle\qquad-2\gamma_{T}^{M}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z_{T}(u)-\gamma_{T}^{M}Z_{T}(u)^{2}dx.

(5.23)

For the energy renormalization, we will build up the required counter-term in an ad-hoc way as in
[
BG20
]
, and show that the difference between this and the term
\delta_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})-\delta_{0}^{T}
is finite. Thus, let us set

U_{2}=-\delta_{T}^{M},

(5.24)

where
\delta_{T}^{M}
is defined in (
7.147
)-(
7.155
). Note that, just as for
\delta_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
, the dependence of
\delta_{T}^{M}
on the boundary data is only through whether
\varphi_{-}
and
\varphi_{+}
have nontrivial
W^{0}
-components, and thus we omit it from notation.

The other terms in
\mathcal{V}_{T}
, minus the term involving
Z_{T}(u)^{4}
, are treated as remainder terms. This is because they do not contain divergences and can be estimated in terms of the coercive terms (which are discussed below) using analytic techniques
12
12

12

This is slightly inaccurate. The term
W_{T}Z_{T}^{3}
requires use of the stochastic term
\mathbb{W}_{T}^{1\circ[3]}
. We stress that this term does not require additional renormalization to define. Thus the argument to estimate this term is mostly analytic after a double paraproduct decomposition. See the treatment of the analogous term in
[
BG20
]
.
. We now list them.

\displaystyle R_{1}=

\displaystyle\int_{M}12W_{T}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z^{2}_{T}+6\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket Z^{2}_{T}\,dx,

(5.25)

\displaystyle R_{2}=

\displaystyle\int_{M}4W_{T}Z^{3}_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+4H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z^{3}_{T}\,dx,

(5.26)

\displaystyle R_{3}=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket{\mathrm{d}}x-\int\llbracket\bar{H}(\varphi_{-})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket{\mathrm{d}}x-\int\llbracket\bar{H}(\varphi_{+})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket{\mathrm{d}}x

(5.27)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{M\Delta\{[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\}}\llbracket\overline{H}(\varphi_{-})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket+\llbracket\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})_{T}^{4}\rrbracket dx.

(5.28)

\displaystyle R_{4}=

\displaystyle-\int_{M}(\gamma_{T}-\gamma_{T}^{M})\Big(2W_{T}Z_{T}+\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket+2H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z_{T}+Z_{T}^{2}\Big)

(5.29)

\displaystyle-(\delta_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})-\Delta\delta_{T}^{0}-\delta_{T}^{M}).

(5.30)

Note that we will define additional remainder terms below that occur when handling the singular terms
S_{1}-S_{3}
.

The above definitions now allow us to rewrite the Boué-Dupuis formula as in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.7
.

For every
u\in\mathbb{H}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}_{T}(W_{T},Z_{T}(u))+\frac{1}{2}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}S_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}U_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}R_{i}+\int_{M}Z_{T}(u)^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right].

(5.31)

5.2.1
Paracontrolled ansatz and coercive terms

We now define our paracontrolled ansatz. We begin by introducing the stochastic objects that will appear in our analysis. First, we set

\mathbb{W}_{T}^{2}:=12\llbracket W_{t}^{2}\rrbracket,\,\mathbb{W}_{T}^{3}=4\llbracket W_{T}^{3}\rrbracket,\,\mathbb{W}_{T}^{[3]}=\int_{0}^{T}J_{t}^{2}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{3}dt.

(5.32)

Let us define

\displaystyle G_{T}=

\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}-J^{2}_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})-J^{2}_{t}\left(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}H\left(\varphi_{-}{,\varphi_{+}}\right)_{T}\right){\mathrm{d}}t,

(5.33)

where the paraproduct
\succ
is on
\mathcal{M}
, i.e. we are implicitly using the periodicity of the objects above. Let us emphasize that
H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}
is
not
dependent on
t
.

The term
Z_{t}^{\flat}
above is defined as follows. Let
(\theta_{t})
be a family of smooth maps
\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
t\mapsto\theta_{t}
is continuously differentiable and the following is satisfied. There exists
c>0
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

\theta_{t}(\xi)\sigma_{s}(\xi)=0,\quad\forall t\leq s,

(5.34)

and for
t>T_{0}
for some
T_{0}
sufficiently large,

\theta_{t}(\xi)=1,\qquad|\xi|\leq ct

(5.35)

See
[
BG20
, Section 4]
for a construction. For
T>0
fixed and larger than
T_{0}
, let us define
u\mapsto Z_{t}^{\flat}(u)
to be the process on
[0,T]
defined by:

Z_{T;t}^{\flat}(u):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\theta_{t})\ast_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}Z_{T}(u),\qquad\forall t\leq T,

(5.36)

where above we have used the Fourier (inverse) transform and spatial convolution on the periodic direction
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
By the first property of
\theta_{t}
, we have that
Z_{T;t}^{\flat}(u)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\theta_{t})\ast_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}Z_{T^{\prime}}(u)
for any
T^{\prime}\geq t
. Hence we simply write
Z_{t}^{\flat}(u)
in the sequel. By Mikhlin multiplier theorem,

\|Z_{t}^{\flat}(u)\|_{L^{4}}\leq\|Z_{T}(u)\|_{L^{4}},

(5.37)

provided
T>T_{0}
. We will assume throughout that
T>T_{0}
implicitly.

Remark 5.8
.

The relevance of the
\flat
is exactly to control terms involving
t<T
and higher than quadratic homogeneity in the drift using analytic estimates. This is because, in our coercive terms, the quartic term is
\|Z_{T}(u)\|_{L^{4}}
and it is not clear how this term controls homogeneity for
t<T
. We refer to
[
BG20
]
for more discussion.

We define the full drift ansatz.

Definition 5.9
.

For every
T>0
and every
u\in\mathbb{H}
, let us write
\ell_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(u)=\ell_{T}(u)\in\mathbb{H}
to denote the drift defined by

\displaystyle u_{t}-\ell_{T}(u)_{t}

\displaystyle=-J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{3}_{t}-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})

(5.38)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-12J_{t}(W_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket)-4J_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{3}\rrbracket,

(5.39)

when
t\leq T
, and
\ell_{T}(u)=0
for
t>T
.

We now turn to the coercive terms.

Definition 5.10
.

For every
T>0
and every
u\in\mathbb{H}
, define

\mathcal{C}_{T}(u):=\|Z_{T}(u)\|_{L^{4}_{x}}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell_{T}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

(5.40)

5.3
Step-by-step renormalization

We now proceed to do a step-by-step renormalization of the singular terms
S_{1}-S_{3}
. Below, we will often write
\equiv
to mean equal up to mean zero terms (with respect to
\mathbb{P}
). Also, we will drop the dependency of the integral on the spatial domain when clear from context. We remind the reader that only the second term in
S_{2}
is periodized, and otherwise the integration domain is
M
.

5.3.1
Renormalization of
S_{1}

The singular term
S_{1}
is controlled by an ansatz on the drift and a component of the energy renormalization
U_{2}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let

u_{t}=-J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{3}+w_{t}

and define

\delta^{1,M}_{T}:=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{3}_{t})^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\right].

(5.41)

Then

S_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}t-\delta^{1,M}_{T}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\|w\|^{2}_{L^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}t.

(5.42)

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Itô’s formula. Note that the stochastic integral
\int_{0}^{T}u_{t}\cdot d\mathbb{W}^{3}_{t}
is mean zero when
T<\infty
since
\mathbb{E}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]<\infty
.

∎

5.3.2
Renormalization of
S_{2}

We now turn our attention to the term
S_{2}
. The ansatz (
5.11
) propagates in and thus we will have to analyze its effect on
S_{2}
. It is convenient to introduce the
intermediate ansatz

Z_{T}(u)=-\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T}+K_{T},

(5.43)

where
K_{T}=Z_{T}(w)
. Then we have that

\displaystyle S_{2}=

\displaystyle-12\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\mathbb{W}_{T}^{[3]}{\mathrm{d}}x+12\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x

(5.44)

\displaystyle+6\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket(\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T})^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x-12\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T}K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+6\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket K^{2}_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x.

(5.45)

Let us define

\delta^{2,M}_{T}:=-6\mathbb{E}\left[\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket(\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T})^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x\right].

(5.46)

Using Wick’s theorem to cancel the first term and
\delta^{2,M}_{T}
to cancel the third, we obtain

S_{2}-\delta^{2,M}_{T}\equiv\tilde{S}_{2},

(5.47)

where

\tilde{S}_{2}:=12\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x-12\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T}K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+6\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket K^{2}_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x.

(5.48)

These divergences will be treated using the counterterm
U_{1}
containing the mass renormalization.

Let us begin by further isolating the divergences using a paraproduct decomposition, which we can use thanks to the periodization trick.

Lemma 5.12
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{S}_{2}&=-12\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\circ\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T}K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\qquad\qquad+12\left(\int(\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket\succ Z^{\flat}_{T})K_{T}+\int(\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x\right)+R_{5},\end{split}

(5.49)

where

\displaystyle R_{5}:=

\displaystyle-12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\prec\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+6\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\prec K_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x-6{\rm Com}_{1}(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket,K_{T})

(5.50)

\displaystyle+12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ(Z_{T}-Z^{\flat}_{T}))K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x,

(5.51)

and

{\rm Com}_{1}(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket,K_{T})=\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ K_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x-\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\circ K_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x.

(5.52)

Proof.

The proof is by paraproduct decomposition and reorganization of terms. We first decompose

\displaystyle 12\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T}K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\circ\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\prec\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x.

The last term is contained in
R_{5}
. Furthermore

6\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket K^{2}_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x=12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ K_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+6\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\prec K_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x-6{\rm Com}_{1}(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket,K_{T}).

The last two terms are contained in
R_{5}
. Finally,

-12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ K_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x=12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ Z_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x

and

12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ Z_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x=12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ Z^{\flat}_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+12\int(\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\succ(Z_{T}-Z^{\flat}_{T}))K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x.

The last term is contained in
R_{5}
as well.
∎

We postpone treatment of the resonant product term in (
5.49
) (see
\Upsilon_{1}
below), and instead analyze the terms in the parentheses by using a second drift ansatz.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let

w_{t}=-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})+v_{s}

(5.53)

where
h_{s}
is defined by this relation. Then

\displaystyle 12\left(\int(\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket\succ Z^{\flat}_{T})K_{T}+\int\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x\right)+\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\|w_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}x

(5.54)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{b}_{t}+J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t+\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\|v_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}x+R_{6}

(5.55)

where

R_{6}:=12\int^{T}_{0}\int(\llbracket W_{t}^{2}\rrbracket\succ\dot{Z}^{\flat}_{t})K_{t}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t.

Proof.

Applying Ito’s formula on the products
(\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket\succ Z^{\flat}_{T})K_{T}
and
\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket K_{T}
, we get

\displaystyle 12\left(\int(\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket\succ Z^{\flat}_{T})K_{T}+\int(\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 12\left(\int_{0}^{T}\int(\llbracket W_{t}^{2}\rrbracket\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})J_{t}w_{t}{\mathrm{d}}t+\int^{T}_{0}\int(\llbracket W_{t}^{2}\rrbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})J_{t}w_{t}{\mathrm{d}}x\right)+{\rm mart}+R_{6},

(5.56)

where

{\rm mart}:=\sum_{i\leq j-2}\int_{0}^{T}\int K_{t}\Delta_{i}Z_{t}^{\flat}d(\Delta_{j}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2})+\int_{0}^{T}\int K_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}d\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}.

(5.57)

The first stochastic integral is a martingale thanks to finite
L^{4}
norm to the fourth power after expectation. The second stochastic integral is a martingale and thus mean zero because
\mathbb{E}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]~<~\infty
. Hence,
{\rm mart}\equiv 0
.

Now from the definition of
v
we get the statement by completing the
square.
∎

We further expand

\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})+J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t+\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle+2\int^{T}_{0}\int J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})\,J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t.

All in all we have proven that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle[S_{1}+S_{2}]-\delta^{1,M}_{T}-\delta^{2,M}_{T}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt

(5.58)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\right]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\right]

(5.59)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})\,J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\right]

(5.60)

\displaystyle-12\mathbb{E}\left[\int\llbracket W^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\circ\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T}K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x\right]

(5.61)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}R_{5}+\mathbb{E}R_{6}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt.

(5.62)

The first term on the r.h.s needs renormalization, and we put it together with a term from
U_{1}
to see the cancellation. But first we rewrite this as follows:

\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t=R_{7}+\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\circ J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t})(Z^{\flat}_{t})^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t,

(5.63)

where

R_{7}:=\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t-\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\circ J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t})(Z^{\flat}_{t})^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t.

(5.64)

We now treat the remaining term. Note that

\gamma_{T}^{M}(x):=-\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\cdot J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}(x)dt\right].

(5.65)

For every
T>0
, define

\Upsilon_{1,T}:=-\int^{T}_{0}\int(\frac{1}{2}J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\circ J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}+\dot{\gamma}_{t}^{M})(Z^{\flat}_{t})^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t-\int(\mathbb{W}_{T}^{2}\circ\mathbb{W}_{T}^{[3]}+2\gamma_{T}^{M}W_{T})K_{T}.

(5.66)

This is the bulk stochastic term

We now analyze the boundary-dependent remaining terms. Using the independence of the boundary conditions and
\mathbb{P}
, we have that

\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\right]\\
=3\cdot 4^{2}\int\int C_{T}^{M}(x,y)^{3}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(x)H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y.

(5.67)

We will want to understand how the righthand side behaves as a random variable when
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}\sim\nu^{0}
. To this end, we introduce the following boundary-bulk stochastic term. For every
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}\in\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa_{0}}_{z}
, define

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{2,T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}):=

\displaystyle\frac{12^{2}}{3}\int\int C_{T}^{M}(x,y)^{3}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(x)H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y

(5.68)

\displaystyle-\int\gamma^{M}_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket{\mathrm{d}}x-\delta_{T}^{3,M},

(5.69)

where

\delta_{T}^{3,M}:=3\cdot 4^{2}\int\int C_{T}^{M}(x,y)^{3}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}\left[H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(x)H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(y)\right]{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y,

(5.70)

where we recall that
\tilde{\mu}^{0}
is the boundary GFF coming from the domain Markov property.

We are now left with one boundary-bulk term, which we will treat as a remainder term.

R_{8}:=\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t}))(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\,H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t-\int 2\gamma^{M}_{T}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x.

(5.71)

5.4
Renormalization of
S_{3}

Using the intermediate ansatz, we have

\displaystyle S_{3}=

\displaystyle 12\int W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+4\int\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x

(5.72)

\displaystyle-12\int W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x-4\int\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\equiv 12\int W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+4\int\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket K_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x.

(5.73)

We will now renormalize these remaining singular terms by using the full drift ansatz to twist these divergences into a form that can be tamed using the the (remaining) counter-terms in the mass renormalization
U_{1}
.

Let
L_{T}=Z_{T}(v)
. Then by (
5.53
),
K_{T}=L_{T}+G_{T}
, from which we obtain

S_{3}=12\int W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket L_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+4\int\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket L_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+R_{9},

(5.74)

where

R_{9}:=12\int W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket G_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+4\int\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket G_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x.

(5.75)

We will now do the further drift ansatz. Recall the definition of
\ell_{T}(u)
. Notice that

v_{t}=12J_{t}W_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket+4J_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket+\ell_{T}(u)_{t}.

(5.76)

By Itô’s formula and completing the square, we therefore have the following lemma.

Lemma 5.14
.

For every
T>0
and every
u\in\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle 12\mathbb{E}\int W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket L_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+4\mathbb{E}\int\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket L_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\int^{T}_{0}\|v_{t}\|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\int^{T}_{0}\int(12J_{t}(W_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket)+4J_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\|\ell_{T}(u)_{t}\|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t.

The first two terms on the righthand side are singular, but they do not depend on the drift. We will therefore define a suitably renormalized version of them (using the remaining terms in
U_{2}
) as new boundary-bulk stochastic objects. We first expand and use that
\mathbb{E}[W_{T}]=0
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\int^{T}_{0}\int(12J_{t}(W_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket)+4J_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}x

(5.77)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{12^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(W_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{4^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}x.

(5.78)

Let us define

\displaystyle\delta^{4,M}_{T}

\displaystyle:=\frac{12^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}\,\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}\,\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}))^{2}_{T}\rrbracket)^{2}dxdt\right],

(5.79)

\displaystyle\delta^{5,M}_{T}

\displaystyle:=\frac{4^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int J_{t}(\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket)^{2}dxdt\right],

(5.80)

where we stress we that the expectations are once again taken with respect to the boundary GFF coming from the domain Markov property.
We now define the two boundary-bulk stochastic terms,

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{3,T}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{12^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(W_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}x+\delta^{4,M}_{T},

(5.81)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{4,T}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{4^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket)^{2}+\delta^{5,M}_{T}.

(5.82)

5.5
Renormalized variational problem and strategy of proof

The following proposition summarizes the outcome of the computations above.

Proposition 5.15
.

For every
u\in\mathbb{H}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}_{T}(W,u)+\frac{1}{2}\|u\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{9}R_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\Upsilon_{i,T}+\mathcal{C}_{T}(u)\right]

(5.83)

As we shall see in the subsequent sections, the function on the righthand side does not contain further divergences and thus we have arrived at the renormalized cost function and renormalized variational problem, as formalized below. As preparation, we can now define the boundary-bulk enhancement. Let
\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
consist of the Wick powers of the harmonic extenstion of
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}
, together with
\Upsilon_{2},\Upsilon_{3}
, and
\Upsilon_{4}
.

Definition 5.16
.

Let
T>0
. The
renormalized cost function
is the map given, for every
u\in\mathbb{H}
, by

\mathbb{F}_{T}^{\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})}(u):=\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}_{T}(u)+\mathcal{C}_{T}(u)\right],

(5.84)

where

\Phi_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(u)=\sum_{i=1}^{9}R_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\Upsilon_{i,T}.

(5.85)

The
renormalized variational problem
is given by

\inf_{u\in\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{F}_{T}^{\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})}(u)

(5.86)

Remark 5.17
.

The renormalized cost function can be trivially adapted to include the term
f
. In this case, we will denote the renormalized cost function
\mathbb{F}_{T}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})}
and the corresponding potential term is denoted
\Phi^{f;\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}_{T}
.

Let us now describe briefly the organization of Sections
6
-
8
and thus the proof of Theorem
3.18
. The actual convergence argument for Theorem
3.18
is completed in Section
8
via the theory of
\Gamma
-convergence. This splits into two parts: establishing
\Gamma
-convergence and establishing equicoercivity of the renormalized cost functions. The
\Gamma
-convergence proof is similar to the one in the boundary case, although the treatment of the remainder term at
T=\infty
is more subtle. For the equicoercivity, we need to establish uniform bounds on the terms
R_{i}
and
\Upsilon_{i}
in terms of the coercive terms
\mathcal{C}_{T}(u)
and constants. For the remainder terms, this follows by analytic arguments in Section
7
. The
\Upsilon_{i}
terms are analyzed in Section
6
, where we also establish Theorem
3.20
. In the case of the boundary-bulk terms, as mentioned in Remark
5.6
, we obtain that these terms are bounded in expectation in the bulk almost surely in the realization of the boundary data.

6
Stochastic estimates

In this section, we will estimate the stochastic terms
\Upsilon_{i,T}
for
i=1,\dots,4
. In the case of the bulk terms
i=1
, we will define the
bulk enhancement
of
W
and the desired estimates will follow from estimates on this process. In the case of the boundary-bulk terms
i=2,3,4
, we will estimate them when
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}
are i.i.d. distributed according to an admissible boundary condition, although we will mainly apply this when their law is given by
\nu^{0}_{T}
. We will in fact prove convergence of these terms to a limiting enhanced boundary field. At the end, we will upgrade these bounds to convergence and use this, together with stochastic estimates developed in Section
4
, to prove Theorem
3.20
.

6.1
Bulk enhancement and
\Upsilon_{1,T}

The
bulk enhancement
of
W
at scale
T
is the vector of stochastic processes
\Xi_{T}(W)
defined by

\Xi_{T}(W):=\left((W_{t})_{t\leq T},(\llbracket W_{t}^{2}\rrbracket)_{t\leq T},(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{[3]})_{t\leq T},(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{1\diamond[3]})_{t\leq T},(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2\diamond 2})_{t\leq T},(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2\diamond[3]})_{t\leq T}\right).

(6.1)

Above, the first three stochastic processes on the righthand side have already been defined. The remaining ones are defined by

\mathbb{W}_{T}^{1\diamond[3]}:=W_{T}\circ\mathbb{W}_{T}^{[3]},\,\mathbb{W}_{T}^{2\diamond 2}:=J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\circ J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}+2\dot{\gamma}_{t}^{M},\,\mathbb{W}_{T}^{2\diamond[3]}:=\mathbb{W}_{T}^{2}\circ\mathbb{W}_{T}^{[3]}+2\gamma_{T}^{M}W_{T}.

(6.2)

This vector naturally lives on a Banach space

\mathfrak{S}:=\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa}_{x}\times\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}_{x}\times\mathcal{C}^{1/2-\kappa}_{x}\times\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa}_{x}\times L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa}_{x}\times\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa}_{x},

(6.3)

where we endow
\mathfrak{S}
with the sum of the norms of the individual spaces in the product.

The following proposition gives uniform in
T
moment bounds on the components of
\Xi_{T}(W)
and resulting convergence to a
limiting bulk enhancement
of
W
in probability. Its proof follows by straightforward albeit tedious modifications of
[
BG20
, Section 8]
into real space and uses the kernel estimates on Dirichlet Green functions found in Appendix
B
. We omit the details.

Proposition 6.1
.

For every
p\in[1,\infty)
, there exists
C>0
such that for every
T>0
,

\mathbb{E}[\|W_{T}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa}_{x}}^{p}+\|\llbracket W_{T}^{2}\rrbracket\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}_{x}}^{p}+\|\mathbb{W}_{T}^{[3]}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2-\kappa}_{x}}^{p}]\leq C

(6.4)

and

\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbb{W}_{T}^{1\diamond[3]}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa}_{x}}^{p}]+\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbb{W}_{T}^{2\diamond 2}\|_{L^{1}_{t}\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa}_{x}}^{p}]+\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbb{W}_{T}^{2\diamond[3]}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1/2-\kappa}_{x}}^{p}]\leq C.

(6.5)

Furthermore, there exist random variables

W_{\infty},\,\llbracket W_{\infty}^{2}\rrbracket,\,\mathbb{W}_{\infty}^{[3]},\,\mathbb{W}_{\infty}^{1\diamond[3]},\,\mathbb{W}_{\infty}^{2\diamond 2},\,\mathbb{W}_{\infty}^{2\diamond[3]}

(6.6)

and limiting bulk enhancement

\Xi_{\infty}(W):=\left((W_{t})_{t\leq\infty},(\llbracket W_{t}^{2}\rrbracket)_{t\leq\infty},(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{[3]})_{t\leq\infty},(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{1\diamond[3]})_{t\leq\infty},(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2\diamond 2})_{t\leq\infty},(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2\diamond[3]})_{t\leq\infty}\right)

(6.7)

such that, for every
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[\|\Xi_{T}(W)-\Xi_{\infty}(W)\|_{\mathfrak{S}}^{p}]=0.

(6.8)

As a direct consequence of the preceding lemma together with duality and standard estimates, we obtain the following bound on
\Upsilon_{1,T}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For every
\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists
C=C(\delta)>0
such that for every
T>0
,

\mathbb{E}[|\Upsilon_{1,T}|]\leq C_{\delta}+\delta\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}_{T}(u)].

(6.9)

6.2
Stochastic estimates on
\Upsilon_{2,T}

We prove an estimate on the (almost) first moment of
\Upsilon_{2,T}
when
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}
are i.i.d. according to an admissible law on boundary conditions.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathbb{P}^{0}
be a law on admissible boundary conditions
\varphi^{0}:=(W^{0},Z^{0})
. Then for every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists
C>0
such that, for every
T>0
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{0}}[|\Upsilon_{2,T}(W^{0}_{-}+Z^{0}_{-},W^{0}_{+}+Z^{0}_{+})|^{\alpha}]\leq C+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{0}}[\|Z^{0}_{+}\|_{H^{1/2-\kappa}_{z}}^{2}+\|Z^{0}_{-}\|_{H^{1/2-\kappa}_{z}}^{2}].

(6.10)

Furthermore, the pushforward under the regularized boundary measure
(\Upsilon_{2,T})^{*}(\nu^{0}_{T}\otimes\nu^{0}_{T})
converges in law to a random variable

(\Upsilon_{2,\infty})^{*}(\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0})
.

Lemma
6.3
will follow from Lemmas
6.4
and
6.5
. We split
\Upsilon_{2,T}
into two terms, one which depends purely on
W^{0}_{-}
and
W^{0}_{+}
, and one which has dependency on the regular parts
Z^{0}_{-},Z^{0}_{+}
, and the latter lemmas handle these parts separately. To this end, let us write

\Upsilon_{2,T}(W_{-}^{0}+Z^{0}_{-},W^{0}_{+}+Z^{0}_{+})=:\Upsilon_{2,T}^{a}(W^{0}_{-},W^{0}_{+})+\Upsilon^{b}_{2,T}(W^{0}_{-},Z^{0}_{-},W^{0}_{+},Z^{0}_{+}),

(6.11)

where

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{2,T}^{a}(W^{0}_{-},W^{0}_{+})

\displaystyle:=\frac{12^{2}}{3}\int\int C^{M}_{T}(x,y)^{3}H(W^{0}_{-},W^{0}_{+})_{T}(x)H(W^{0}_{-},W^{0}_{+})_{T}(y)dxdy

(6.12)

\displaystyle\quad-\int\gamma_{T}^{M}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket dx-\delta^{3,M}_{T}.

(6.13)

The first lemma handles
\Upsilon^{a}_{2,T}
, for which we can use Gaussian computations. We estimate its variance. Recall that
\mu^{0}
is the marginal of
W^{0}
under
\mathbb{P}^{0}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

There exists
C>0
such that, for every
T>0
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{0}\otimes\mu^{0}}[|\Upsilon_{2,T}^{a}|^{2}]\leq C.

(6.14)

Proof.

Since the covariance of
\mu^{0}
is equal to the covariance of
\tilde{\mu}^{0}
up to an infinitely smoothing term, it is sufficient to do this estimate where the expectation is taken with respect to
\tilde{\mu}^{0}
. Recall the definitions of the covariances
C^{M}_{T}
and
C^{B}_{T}
. Let us write
\Upsilon^{a}_{2,T}=I_{1}+I_{2}
, where

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{12^{2}}{3}\int\int C_{T}^{M}(x,y)^{3}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(x)H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(y)dxdy-\int\gamma^{M}_{T}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x

(6.15)

\displaystyle=\frac{12^{2}}{3}\int\int C^{M}_{T}(x,y)^{3}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(x)\left(H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(y)-H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}(x)\right),

(6.16)

and

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle:=\int\gamma_{T}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}[H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}]dx-\delta^{3,M}_{T}

(6.17)

\displaystyle=\frac{12^{2}}{3}\int\int C^{M}_{T}(x,y)^{3}\left(C^{B}_{T}(x,x)-C^{B}_{T}(x,y)\right)=-\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}[I_{1}].

(6.18)

Hence,

\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}[|\Upsilon_{2,T}^{a}|^{2}]=\mathrm{Var}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}(I_{1}).

(6.19)

We now compute the second moment of
I_{1}
. By expanding and using Wick’s theorem,

\displaystyle\frac{3^{2}}{12^{4}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}[I_{1}^{2}]

(6.20)

\displaystyle=\int\int\int\int C^{M}_{T}(x_{1},y_{1})^{3}C^{M}_{T}(x_{2},y_{2})^{3}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}\Bigg[H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(x_{1})H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(x_{2})

(6.21)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Big(H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(y_{1})-H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(x_{1})\Big)\Big(H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(y_{2})-H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(x_{2})\Big)\Bigg]

(6.22)

\displaystyle=\int\int\int\int C^{M}_{T}(x_{1},y_{1})^{3}C^{M}_{T}(x_{2},y_{2})^{3}\Bigg\{\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{1})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{1})\right)\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{2},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{2},x_{2})\right)

(6.23)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\left(C^{B}_{T}(y_{1},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{2},y_{1})+C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\right)

(6.24)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\right)\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{2},y_{1})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\right)\Bigg\}.

(6.25)

However, note that

\displaystyle\frac{3^{2}}{12^{4}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}[I_{1}]^{2}=\int\int\int\int

\displaystyle C^{M}_{T}(x_{1},y_{1})^{3}C^{M}_{T}(x_{2},y_{2})^{3}

(6.26)

\displaystyle\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{1})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{1})\right)\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{2},x_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{2},y_{2})\right).

(6.27)

Hence, by symmetry considerations

\displaystyle\frac{3^{2}}{12^{4}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mu}^{0}\otimes\tilde{\mu}^{0}}[|\Upsilon_{2,T}^{a}|^{2}]

(6.28)

\displaystyle=\int\int\int\int C^{M}_{T}(x_{1},y_{1})^{3}C^{M}_{T}(x_{2},y_{2})^{3}\Bigg\{\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\right)\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{2},y_{1})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\right)

(6.29)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\left(C^{B}_{T}(y_{1},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{2},y_{1})+C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\right)\Bigg\}

(6.30)

\displaystyle=\int\int\int\int C^{M}_{T}(x_{1},y_{1})^{3}C^{M}_{T}(x_{2},y_{2})^{3}\Bigg\{\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\right)\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{2},y_{1})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})\right)

(6.31)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\qquad+\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})\right)\left(C^{B}_{T}(y_{1},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})\right)\Bigg\}

(6.32)

\displaystyle=2\int

\displaystyle\int\int\int C^{M}_{T}(x_{1},y_{1})^{3}C^{M}_{T}(x_{2},y_{2})^{3}\Bigg\{\left(C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})\right)\left(C^{B}_{T}(y_{1},y_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})\right)\Bigg\}.

(6.33)

We denote the righthand side of the above as
J
. In order to estimate this, we will need the estimate on the gradient of the Poisson kernel
C^{B}_{T}
, see Lemma
C.2
. Let us write
\ell(x_{i},y_{j})
to denote the straight line connecting
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
. Note that for all
\xi\in\ell(x_{i},y_{j})
,
d(\xi,B)\geq\min(d(x_{i},B),d(y_{j},B))
. Therefore, by the mean-value theorem and the gradient estimate, for every
\varepsilon,\delta>0
sufficiently small,

\displaystyle|C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\sup_{\xi\in\ell(x_{2},y_{2})}|\nabla_{e_{2}}C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},\xi)||x_{2}-y_{2}|\right)^{\varepsilon}

(6.34)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{|x_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}}\right)

(6.35)

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{\varepsilon}}{{\rm min}(d(x_{2},\partial M)^{(2-\delta)\varepsilon},d(y_{2},\partial M)^{(2-\delta)\varepsilon})\cdot d(x_{1},\partial M)^{\delta\varepsilon}}

(6.36)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{|x_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}}\right).

(6.37)

In particular, by redefining
\delta
, we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle|C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},x_{2})-C^{B}_{T}(x_{1},y_{2})|

(6.38)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{\delta}}{{\rm min}(d(x_{2},\partial M)^{\delta},d(y_{2},\partial M)^{\delta})\cdot d(x_{1},\partial M)^{\delta}}\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{|x_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}}\right).

(6.39)

Hence, we may bound

\displaystyle|J|

(6.40)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int\int\int\int\frac{\max(d(x_{2},\partial M)^{-\delta},d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-\delta})\max(d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-\delta},d(y_{1},\partial M)^{-\delta})}{|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{3-\delta}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3-\delta}}

(6.41)

\displaystyle\times d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-\delta}d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-\delta}

(6.42)

\displaystyle\times\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{|x_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{|y_{2}-x_{1}|^{1-\varepsilon}}\right)

(6.43)

\displaystyle=:J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4},

(6.44)

where the
J_{i}
are defined according to expanding the brackets in the last line above (see below). Note that, by symmetry,
J_{2}=J_{3}
(these correspond to the cross terms in the expansion above).

We will first estimate
J_{1}
. Note that, also by symmetry,

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle:=C\int\int\int\int\frac{\max(d(x_{2},\partial M)^{-\delta},d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-\delta})\max(d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-\delta},d(y_{1},\partial M)^{-\delta})}{|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{3-\delta}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3-\delta}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta}|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-\delta}}

(6.45)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\ \times d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-\delta}d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-\delta}

(6.46)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\int\int\int\int\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{3-\delta}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3-\delta}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta}|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-\delta}}\right)

(6.47)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Big(d(x_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}+d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}

(6.48)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+d(x_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(y_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}+d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(y_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}\Big)

(6.49)

\displaystyle=2C^{\prime}\int\int\int\int\left(\frac{d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(x_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}+d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}}{|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{3-\delta}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3-\delta}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta}|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-\delta}}\right)

(6.50)

\displaystyle=:J_{1}(a)+J_{1}(b).

(6.51)

We estimate
J_{1}(a)
first. Integrating out
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
respectively, we obtain

\displaystyle J_{1}(a)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}\int\int\int\frac{d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(x_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}}{|x_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-2\delta}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3-\delta}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta}}

(6.52)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime}\int\int\frac{d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(x_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2-4\delta}}\leq C_{4}

(6.53)

where the last inequality is by Young’s convolution inequality.
We now estimate
J_{1}(b)
. Integrating out
y_{1}
and then
x_{2}
, and then using Young’s convolution inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle J_{1}(b)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}\int\int\int\frac{d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}}{|x_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-2\delta}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3-\delta}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta}}

(6.54)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime}\int\int\frac{d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-2\delta}d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-2\delta}}{|x_{1}-y_{2}|^{2-4\delta}}\leq C_{4}.

(6.55)

Let us now turn to
J_{2}
(which in turn bounds
J_{3}
by symmetry). As by the above manipulations (with an extra straightforward Cauchy-Schwarz step, c.f. the estimate of
J_{4}
below), we obtain

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\int\int\int\int\frac{d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-4\delta}+d(x_{2},\partial M)^{-4\delta}+d(y_{1},\partial M)^{-4\delta}+d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-4\delta}}{|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{3-\delta}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3-\delta}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta}|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{1-\delta}}

(6.56)

\displaystyle=:J_{2}(a)+J_{2}(b)+J_{2}(c)+J_{2}(d).

(6.57)

We will only estimate
J_{2}(a)
, the other terms follow by similar considerations. Integrating out
y_{2}
and then
y_{1}
, and then using Young’s convolution inequality,

\displaystyle J_{2}(a)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}\int\int\int\frac{d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-4\delta}}{|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{3-\delta}|x_{2}-y_{1}|^{1-2\delta}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta}}

(6.58)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime}\int\int\frac{d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-4\delta}}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2-4\delta}}\leq C_{4}.

(6.59)

Finally, let us estimate
J_{4}
.

\displaystyle J_{4}

\displaystyle:=C\int\int\int\int\frac{\max(d(x_{2},\partial M)^{-\delta},d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-\delta})\max(d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-\delta},d(y_{1},\partial M)^{-\delta})}{|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{3-\delta}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3-\delta}|x_{1}-y_{2}|^{2-2\delta}}

(6.60)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times d(x_{1},\partial M)^{-\delta}d(y_{2},\partial M)^{-\delta}

(6.61)
