\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|^{p(1-\delta)}_{L^{\infty}_{\tau}B^{-1/2-\kappa}_{p,p,z}}

(7.173)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\int_{\Sigma_{1}}\frac{\tau^{-(1/2+\kappa+s)p(1-\delta)}}{|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|^{1+sp}}\left(\int_{\tau}^{\tau^{\prime}}\|\partial_{\tau^{\prime\prime}}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(\tau^{\prime\prime},\cdot)\|_{B^{s}_{p,p,z}}d\tau^{\prime\prime}\right)^{p\delta}d\tau d\tau^{\prime}.

(7.174)

The condition
p<2
is necessary here to ensure that the singularity at
\tau=0
is integrable.

Let us now estimate the derivative term. Recall that
H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})=\overline{H}(\varphi_{-})+\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})+S(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
. Upon identication of
-L
with
L
, and the shift of
+L
, in the regime
\tau<0
we have that,

\|\partial_{\tau}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(\tau,\cdot)\|_{B^{s}_{p,p,z}}\leq\|\partial_{\tau}\overline{H}(\varphi_{-})(\tau,\cdot)\|_{B^{s}_{p,p,z}}+\|\partial_{\tau}\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})(\tau,\cdot)\|_{B^{s}_{p,p,z}}.

(7.175)

By the explicit expression for
\overline{H}
, we have that

\partial_{\tau}\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})=\mathcal{N}_{0}\overline{H}(\varphi_{+}),

(7.176)

where we recall
\mathcal{N}_{0}=-\sqrt{-\Delta_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}+m^{2}}
. On the set
\tau^{\prime\prime}\in(\tau,\tau^{\prime})
, we may estimate

\|\mathcal{N}_{0}\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})(\tau^{\prime\prime},\cdot)\|_{B^{s}_{p,p,z}}\leq c_{3}\|\mathcal{N}_{0}\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau}B^{-3/2-\kappa}_{p,p,z}}\tau^{-3/2-\kappa-s}.

(7.177)

Thus,

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\tau}^{\tau^{\prime}}\|\partial_{\tau^{\prime\prime}}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(\tau^{\prime\prime},\cdot)\|_{B^{s}_{p,p,z}}d\tau^{\prime\prime}\right)^{p\delta}

(7.178)

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}(\|\mathcal{N}_{0}\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau}B^{-3/2-\kappa}_{p,p,z}}^{p\delta}+\|\mathcal{N}_{0}\overline{H}(\varphi_{+})\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau}B^{-3/2-\kappa}_{p,p,z}}^{p\delta})\tau^{-(3/2+\kappa+s)p\delta}|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|^{p\delta}.

(7.179)

Thus the bound on
I_{1}
reduces to estimating

\int_{\Sigma_{1}}\frac{\tau^{-(1/2+\kappa+s)p(1-\delta)}\tau^{-(3/2+\kappa+s)p\delta}}{|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|^{1+(s-\delta)p}}d\tau d\tau.

(7.180)

By first fixing
p<2
, then
\delta
and
s
sufficiently small such that
s<\delta
, we find that the above integrals are integrable near
\tau=0
and thus we obtain the desired bound.

By symmetry considerations, to finish the lemma it remains to estimate the integral
I
on the set
\Sigma_{2}:=\{\tau<0<\tau^{\prime}:\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in\Sigma\}
— we call this
I_{2}
. By similar estimates as above, we have that

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma_{2}}\frac{\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(\tau,\cdot)-H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(\tau^{\prime},\cdot)\|^{p}_{B^{s}_{p,p,z}}}{|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|^{1+sp}}d\tau d\tau^{\prime}

(7.181)

\displaystyle\,\leq c_{5}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}_{\tau}B^{-1/2-\kappa}_{p,p,z}}\int_{\Sigma_{2}}\frac{\tau^{-(1/2+\kappa+s)p}}{(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)^{1+sp}}d\tau d\tau^{\prime}.

(7.182)

Integrating first in
\tau^{\prime}
and then in
\tau
, we have that the integral on the righthand side above is finite provided
p<2
and
s,\kappa>0
is sufficiently small.

∎

We now turn to the proof of
R_{8}
.

Proof of
7.13
.

The first integral of
R_{8}
is given by

I_{1}:=\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t}))(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\,H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

(7.183)

We will begin by putting this in a more tractable form, by moving the
J_{t}
on to
\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}
. Let us define
I_{1}^{\prime}
by the decomposition

I_{1}=\int_{0}^{T}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\,H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}))dxdt+I_{1}^{\prime},

(7.184)

We first estimate
I_{1}^{\prime}
. By the commmutator lemma applied to
J_{t}
and regularizing properties of
J_{t}
, both found in Appendix
B
, we have that

\|J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})-J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t}\|_{H^{1/4-3\kappa}}\leq\frac{C}{\langle t\rangle^{1/2+\kappa}}\|\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}}\|Z^{\flat}_{t}\|_{H^{1/4-\kappa}}.

(7.185)

Hence

|I_{1}^{\prime}|\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle^{1/2+\kappa}}\|\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}}\|Z^{\flat}_{t}\|_{H^{1/4-\kappa}}\|J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\,H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})\|_{H^{-1/4+3\kappa}}dt.

(7.186)

In order to estimate the integral on the righthand side, observe that

\|J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\,H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})\|_{H^{-1/4+3\kappa}}\\
\leq\frac{C}{\langle t\rangle^{7/4-3\kappa}}\|\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\,H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{\langle t\rangle^{7/4-3\kappa}}\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B^{s}_{p,p}}.

(7.187)

Above, the first inequality follows by the regularizing properties of
J_{t}
, and the second inequality follows by Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding (where
s>0
is sufficiently small so that
p>2
is sufficiently close to 2).
Adding this back into the estimate of
I_{1}^{\prime}
and using properties of the
\flat
, we obtain that

|I_{1}^{\prime}|\leq C\|\langle t\rangle^{-9/4+2\kappa}\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}_{x}}\|_{L^{1}_{t}}\cdot\|Z_{T}\|_{H^{1/4-\kappa}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B^{s}_{p,p}},

(7.188)

which can then be estimated by Young’s inequality.

Now, let us do a paraproduct decomposition and write

\int_{0}^{T}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\,H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})dxdt\\
=\int_{0}^{T}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})dxdt+I_{1}^{\prime\prime}.

(7.189)

By using commutator estimates as above, taking
p<2
(for Lemma

7.14
to apply), we have

\|J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})-J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B_{p,p}^{\kappa}}\\
\leq\frac{C}{\langle t\rangle^{1/2+\kappa}}\|\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B_{p,p}^{3\kappa}}.

(7.190)

On the other hand, by para and resonant product estimates,

\displaystyle\|J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\preccurlyeq H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})\|_{B_{p,p}^{\kappa}}\leq C\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle^{3/2+\kappa}}\|\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}}{\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B_{p,p}^{2\kappa}}}.

(7.191)

Writing
q
as the Hölder conjugate of
p
,

\displaystyle|I_{1}^{\prime\prime}|

\displaystyle:=\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})(J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\,H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}))dxdt\right|

(7.192)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle^{1/2+\kappa}}\|J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t}\|_{B^{-2\kappa}_{q,q}}(\|\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}}+\langle t\rangle^{-1}\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}){\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B_{p,p}^{3\kappa}}}.

(7.193)

Provided
p>2
is sufficiently close to 2, then
q
can be taken sufficiently close to
2
as well (we need
q<4
). By properties of
\flat
and a paraproduct estimate, we obtain that

\displaystyle|I_{1}^{\prime\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\|Z_{T}\|_{L^{4}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B^{3\kappa}_{p,p}}

(7.194)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times(\|\langle t\rangle^{-3/2-\kappa}\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}_{x}}^{2}\|_{L^{1}_{t}}+\|\langle t\rangle^{-5/2-\kappa}\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}_{x}}\|_{L^{1}_{t}}).

(7.195)

This can then be estimated using Young’s inequality.

To summarize, we have written

I_{1}=\int_{0}^{T}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})dxdt+I_{1}^{\prime}+I_{1}^{\prime\prime},

(7.196)

and have obtained estimates on
I_{1}^{\prime}
and
I_{1}^{\prime\prime}
. We now turn to estimating the final form of the integrand together with the remaining term in
R_{8}
, which is the renormalization counter term. To this end, we define

I_{2}:=\int_{0}^{T}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})dx-\int 2\gamma_{T}^{M}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}Z_{T}dx.

(7.197)

By Itô’s formula on the product
\gamma_{T}^{M}Z_{T}
and reorganizing terms, we may write

I_{2}=I_{3}+I_{2}^{\prime},

(7.198)

where

I_{3}:=\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})-(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\circ J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t})Z^{\flat}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}dxdt,

(7.199)

and where

\displaystyle I_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=I_{2}^{\prime}(a)+I_{2}^{\prime}(b),

(7.200)

\displaystyle I_{2}^{\prime}(a)

\displaystyle:=\int^{T}_{0}\int(J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\circ J_{t}\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}+2\dot{\gamma}^{M}_{t})Z^{\flat}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}dxdt,

(7.201)

\displaystyle I_{2}^{\prime}(b)

\displaystyle:=\int^{T}_{0}\int 2\gamma_{t}^{M}\dot{Z}^{\flat}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}dxdt+\int 2\gamma_{T}^{M}(Z_{T}-Z^{\flat}_{T})H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}dx.

(7.202)

We first estimate
I_{2}^{\prime}(b)
. Note that
\gamma_{T}^{M}(\tau,\cdot)=\gamma_{T}^{M}(\tau)
is constant for every
\tau
. By splitting into
\tau
-slices, we have that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int

\displaystyle\gamma_{T}^{M}(Z_{T}-Z^{\flat}_{T})H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}dx\Bigg|

(7.203)

\displaystyle\leq C\int\frac{|\gamma_{T}^{M}(\tau)|}{T^{1/4-\kappa}}\|Z_{T}(\tau,\cdot)-Z_{T}^{\flat}(\tau,\cdot)\|_{H^{1/2-\kappa}_{z}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})(\tau,\cdot)_{T}\|_{H^{-1/4}_{z}}d\tau

(7.204)

\displaystyle\leq T^{-1/4+\kappa}\|\gamma_{T}^{M}\|_{L^{\infty}}(\|Z_{T}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{1/2-\kappa}_{z}}+\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|^{2}_{L_{\tau}^{2}H^{-1/4}_{z}}),

(7.205)

where the first line is by duality and Bernstein’s inequality (using spectral support considerations of
Z_{T}-Z_{T}^{\flat}
in the
z
-variable), and the second line is by the Cauchy-Schwarz and Young inequalities. Using that
\|\gamma_{T}^{M}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}=\|\gamma_{T}^{M}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau}}=O(\log T)
, the desired estimate on this term follows.

For the other integral in
I_{2}^{\prime}(b)
, we will estimate it up to a mean-zero term. By Wick’s theorem it is sufficient to estimate the integral with
\dot{Z}_{t}^{\flat}
replaced by
\dot{K}_{t}^{\flat}
. Recall the
\flat
interpolation estimate from
[
BG20
]
: for every sufficiently regular function,

\|\dot{f}_{t}^{\flat}\|_{B^{s}_{p,p}}\leq\frac{C}{\langle t\rangle^{1+s-s^{\prime}}}\|f_{T}\|_{B^{s^{\prime}}_{p,p}}.

(7.206)

Then, by splitting into
(\tau,z)
variables and using duality,

\int_{0}^{T}\|\gamma_{t}^{M}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\dot{K}_{t}^{\flat}\|_{H^{\kappa}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{-\kappa}_{z}}dt\\
\leq C^{\prime}\int_{0}^{T}\|\gamma_{t}^{M}\|_{L^{\infty}}\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle^{2}}\|K_{T}\|_{H^{1-\kappa}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{-\kappa}_{z}}dt,

(7.207)

which can then be estimated by using that
\|\gamma_{t}^{M}\|_{L^{\infty}}=O(\log t)
, Young’s inequality, and the estimates on
K_{T}
from Lemma
7.4
.

We now turn to
I_{2}^{\prime}(a)
. Recall that

\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2\diamond 2}:=J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\circ J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}+2\dot{\gamma}_{t}^{M}.

(7.208)

Thus, by duality and fractional Leibniz, followed by Young’s inequality,

|I_{2}^{\prime}(a)|\leq C\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2\diamond 2}\|_{L_{t}^{1}\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa}}\|Z^{\flat}_{t}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}B_{5/2,5/2}^{\kappa}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B_{5/3,5/3}^{\kappa}},

(7.209)

from which the desired estimate follows.

Finally, we estimate
I_{3}
. We will slightly adapt the quadrilinear estimate in
[
BG20
, Proposition 11]
. First, observe that for every
f,g,h
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int(f_{t}\succ g_{t})(f_{t}\succ h_{t})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int(f_{t}\circ(f_{t}\succ h_{t}))g)dxdt+{\rm com}_{1}

(7.210)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int(f_{t}\circ f_{t})g_{t}h_{t}+{\rm com}_{2}+{\rm com}_{1},

(7.211)

where

\displaystyle{\rm com}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{T}\int(f_{t}\succ g_{t})(f_{t}\succ h_{t})-(f_{t}\circ(f_{t}\succ h_{t}))g_{t}dxdt,

(7.212)

\displaystyle{\rm com}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{T}\int\Big((f_{t}\circ(f_{t}\succ h_{t}))-(f_{t}\circ f_{t})h_{t}\Big)g_{t}dxdt.

(7.213)

We will set
f_{t}=J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}
,
g_{t}=Z_{t}^{\flat}
and
h_{t}=H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}
.

Let us first estimate
{\rm com}_{1}
. Let
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
. Then by the trilinear estimate
[
BG20
, Proposition 10]
and paraproduct estimate,

\displaystyle|{\rm com}_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa}}\|Z_{t}^{\flat}\|_{B^{2\kappa}_{p,p}}\|J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\succ H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B^{-\kappa}_{q,q}}dt

(7.214)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\int_{0}^{T}\|J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa}}^{2}\|Z_{t}^{\flat}\|_{B^{2\kappa}_{p,p}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{L^{q}}dt.

(7.215)

Thus, taking
q<2
sufficiently close to
2
such that there exists
\kappa^{\prime}>0
such that
H^{1/2-\kappa^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow B^{2\kappa}_{p,p}
, we have that (using properties of
\flat
)

|{\rm com}_{1}|\leq C^{\prime\prime}\|Z_{T}\|_{H^{1/2-\kappa^{\prime}}}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{L^{q}}\int_{0}^{T}\|J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa}}^{2}dt,

(7.216)

which can then be put in the desired form by standard arguments using the regularizing properties of
J_{t}
in Appendix
B
.

We now turn to
{\rm com}_{2}
. For the second term, by the trilinear estimate
[
BG20
, Proposition 8]
and paraproduct estimates,

|{\rm com}_{2}|\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-\kappa}}^{2}\|H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}\|_{B^{3\kappa}_{q,q}}\|Z_{t}^{\flat}\|_{L^{p}}dt.

(7.217)

The desired inequality follows by similar arguments as above provided
q<2
is chosen sufficiently close to
2
such that
p\leq 4
.

∎

7.7
Estimate of
R_{9}

Recall that

R_{9}=12\int W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket G_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x+4\int\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket G_{T}{\mathrm{d}}x=:R_{9}(a)+R_{9}(b),

(7.218)

where

G_{T}=\int^{T}_{0}-J^{2}_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})-J^{2}_{t}\left(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}H\left(\varphi_{-}{,\varphi_{+}}\right)_{T}\right){\mathrm{d}}t.

(7.219)

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrarily small and
p=4/3(1+\varepsilon)
and let
q=4(1+\varepsilon)/(3\varepsilon)
.
For every
\delta>0
, there exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|R_{9}|]

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\|d(x,\partial M)^{1/2+2\kappa}\llbracket H^{3}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\rrbracket\|^{p}_{L_{\tau}^{\infty}H_{z}^{-1+\kappa}}

(7.220)

\displaystyle\quad+\|d(x,\partial M)^{1/2-\kappa}\llbracket H^{2}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\rrbracket\|^{2}_{L^{4/3}_{\tau}L^{\infty}_{z}}\Big)

(7.221)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[C\|\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\|^{q}_{L^{\infty}_{t}\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}}+\delta\|Z_{T}\|^{4}_{L^{4}}\right].

(7.222)

Proof.

Observe that, by Wick’s theorem,

\displaystyle R_{9}(a)

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{R}_{9}(a):=-12\int W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket\int_{0}^{T}J^{2}_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x,

(7.223)

\displaystyle R_{9}(b)

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{R}_{9}(b):=-4\int\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}_{T}\rrbracket\int_{0}^{T}J^{2}_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x.

(7.224)

We estimate
\tilde{R}_{9}(a)
first. We will split into
(\tau,z)
variables. By duality and Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle|\tilde{R}_{9}(a)|

\displaystyle\leq\int\|W_{T}(\tau,\cdot)\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T}^{2}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)\|_{H_{z}^{-1+\kappa}}\left\|\int_{0}^{T}J^{2}_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})(\tau,\cdot)dt\right\|_{H^{1-\kappa}_{z}}d\tau

(7.225)

\displaystyle\leq\|W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\|_{L^{4/3}_{\tau}H^{-1+\kappa}_{z}}\left\|\int_{0}^{T}J^{2}_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{\flat}_{t})dt\right\|_{L^{4}_{\tau}H^{1-\kappa}_{z}}.

(7.226)

In order to estimate the second term on the RHS, note that

\left\|\int_{0}^{T}J^{2}_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{b}_{t})dt\right\|_{L^{4}_{\tau}H^{1-\kappa}_{z}}\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{T}J^{2}_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}\succ Z^{b}_{t})dt\right\|_{B^{1-\kappa}_{4,1}}\leq C\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa/2}}\|Z_{T}\|_{L^{4}}.

(7.227)

We now turn our attention to the remaining term. Let
q<4
and
p>4/3
be Hölder conjugate. By Young’s inequality and the above estimate, we thus must estimate

I_{0}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}):=\mathbb{E}\left[\|W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\|_{L^{4/3}_{\tau}H^{-1+\kappa}_{z}}^{p}\right].

(7.228)

We will in fact estimate
I_{0}:=\mathbb{E}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{0}[I_{0}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})]
.
By hypercontractivity and Minkowski’s integral inequality, we may bound this by

\displaystyle I_{0}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\left[\|W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\|_{L^{4/3}_{\tau}H^{-1+\kappa}_{z}}^{2}\right]^{p/2}

(7.229)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\|W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\|_{H^{-1+\kappa}_{z}}^{2}\right]^{1/2}\right\|^{p}_{L^{4/3}_{\tau}}.

(7.230)

Now to bound the expectation, observe that
(m^{2}-\Delta_{per})^{-(1+2\kappa)}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
has a kernel bounded by
|z-z^{\prime}|^{-4\kappa}
.
Thus for fixed
\tau
we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|W_{T}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}\rrbracket\|_{H^{-1+\kappa}_{z}}^{2}\right]

(7.231)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int\int\frac{1}{|z-z^{\prime}|^{4\kappa}}C^{M}(x,x^{\prime})|\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}(\tau,z)\rrbracket\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}(\tau,z^{\prime})\rrbracket|\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}

(7.232)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int\int\frac{1}{|z-z^{\prime}|^{4\kappa}}\frac{d(\tau,\partial M)^{1-6\kappa}}{|z-z^{\prime}|^{2-6\kappa}}\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\|\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}(\tau,\cdot)\rrbracket\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}^{2}

(7.233)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|d(\tau,\partial M)^{1/2-3\kappa}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}_{T}(\tau,\cdot)\rrbracket\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{z}}

(7.234)

Now taking this to the power
2/3
and integrating in
\tau
we obtain the desired bound.

We now turn to
\tilde{R}_{9}(b)
. As above, it is sufficient to estimate, with
p
as above,

I:=\int\|\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)\|_{H^{-1+\kappa}_{z}}^{p}d\tau.

(7.235)

Hence we may interpolate with the distance to the boundary to obtain

|I|\leq C\|d(\tau,\partial M)^{\frac{1+4\kappa}{2}}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}\rrbracket(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau}H^{-1+\kappa}_{z}}^{p}\int d(\tau,\partial M)^{-\frac{p(1+4\kappa)}{2}}d\tau.

(7.236)

The integral in
\tau
on the righthand side is finite provided
p
is sufficiently close to
4/3
. Furthermore, the first term on the RHS may be controlled by using Proposition
4.1
.

∎

8
Convergence of bulk amplitudes

In this section, we show that the variational representation of the bulk amplitudes converge to a limiting variational representation using the theory of
\Gamma
-convergence. As a consequence, we prove Theorem
3.18
. Our proof of convergence follows the
\Gamma
-convergence argument of Section
4
(and as a result, of
[
BG20
]
) with the crucial difference coming in the handling of the remainder map.

8.1
Relaxation of the variational problem

We begin by relaxing the variational problem on an appropriate space of joint laws that has sufficient compacity properties to establish
\Gamma
-convergence. Recall that
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
. Define

\mathcal{L}:=L^{2}_{t}W^{-1/2-\delta,3}_{x}.

(8.1)

This choice is motivated by the fact that the mapping
u\to Z(u)
is a compact map
\mathcal{L}\mapsto L^{\infty}_{t}L^{4}_{x}
.

Recall the limiting bulk enhancement
\Xi_{\infty}(W)
defined in Proposition
6.1
. We consider the following spaces of joint laws:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle:=\{\mu\in P(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{S}}\times\mathcal{L}):\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}]<\infty\},

(8.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle:=\{\mu\in\mathcal{Y}:{\rm Law}_{\mu}(\Xi)={\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\Xi_{\infty}(W))\}.

(8.3)

We endow
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{X}
with the following notion of sequential convergence. We say that a sequence
(\mu_{n})
converges to
\mu
in
\mathcal{Y}
if

(i)

\mu_{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{S}}\times\mathcal{L}_{w}
,

(ii)

\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{n}}[\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}]<\infty
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
denote the sequential closure of
\mathcal{X}
in
\mathcal{Y}
under this notion of convergence. Let us stress that, since the marginal of the first components are fixed for any sequence in
\mathcal{X}
, the marginal of the first component is also fixed in
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
.

We now define our cost functions. For
T\in(0,\infty]
, define the functional on
\overline{\mathcal{X}}

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})}_{T}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\Phi^{f;\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}_{T}(u)+\mathcal{C}_{T}(u)\right],\qquad\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}},

(8.4)

where
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}
denotes the expectation with respect to the measure
\mu
,
\Phi_{T}^{f;\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}
is as in Definition
5.16
(see also Proposition
5.15
and Remark
5.17
), and
\mathcal{C}_{T}(u)
is as in (
5.40
).

Remark 8.1
.

We stress that the function
u\mapsto\Phi_{T}^{f;\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(u)+\mathcal{C}_{T}(u)
is deterministically well-defined for
u\in\mathcal{L}
. It does not require progressive measurability of
u
, which is not necessarily valid anymore. Furthermore, let us emphasize that we have implicitly extended this function to include the case
T=\infty
. Since
u\in\mathcal{L}
, the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}(u)
is straightforward. Furthermore, by the stochastic estimates in Section
6
the terms
\Upsilon^{i}_{T}
in
\Phi_{T}^{f;\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}
can be extended in a straightforward manner to include
T=\infty
. It remains to justify why we may define the
R_{i}
terms at
T=\infty
. This follows from viewing the
R_{i}
as (sums of) multilinear maps and deducing continuity of these maps from the estimates used to prove Proposition
7.1
. We omit further details.

The following proposition is an immediate consequence of the definition (
8.4
), Definition
5.16
, and Remarks
5.17
and
8.1
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\mu={\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\Xi,u)
for
v
progressively measurable with respect to
W
and almost surely in
\mathcal{L}
. Then for every
T\in(0,\infty]
,

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})}_{T}(\mu)=\mathbb{F}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})}_{T}(v).

(8.5)

8.2
Approximation properties of the functionals

We begin with some technical approximation properties of the renormalized cost functions involved in the variational problem, analogous to those in the case of the boundary theory.

The first technical property concerns approximation of the cost functions when
T<\infty
. Its proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
4.14
with minor adaptations required, and thus we omit it.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
T>0
. For every
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}
and every
K\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, there exists
\mu_{K}\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\mu_{K}\rightarrow\mu
and

|\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\varphi_{0},\varphi_{+}}_{T}(\mu)-\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\varphi_{0},\varphi_{+}}_{T}(\mu_{K})|<1/K.

(8.6)

We now turn to approximation properties of the cost function when
T=\infty
.

Lemma 8.4
.

The limiting renormalized cost function has the following approximation properties.

(i)

For every
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}
and every
K\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, there exists
\mu_{K}\in\mathcal{X}
such that

|\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\varphi_{0},\varphi_{+}}_{\infty}(\mu)-\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\varphi_{0},\varphi_{+}}_{\infty}(\mu_{K})|<1/K.

(8.7)

(ii)

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{X}
. If
\Xi^{\infty}(\varphi_{0}^{T},\varphi_{+}^{T})\to\Xi^{\infty}(\varphi_{0}^{\infty},\varphi_{+}^{\infty})
in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}
, then

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\varphi^{T}_{0},\varphi^{T}_{+}}_{T}(\mu)=\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\varphi_{0},\varphi_{+}}_{\infty}(\mu).

(8.8)

In order to prove Lemma
8.4
it is necessary to have a bound on
\mathbb{E}[\|\ell_{\infty}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(u)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}]
. Since the ansatz map is more complicated now, this will require some significant modifications as compared with the proof of Lemma
4.15
, which is the analogous result for the boundary theory. For convenience, let us write

\mathbb{U}_{t}:=J_{t}\mathbb{W}_{t}^{3}+J_{t}(\mathbb{W}^{2}_{t}H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}))+12J_{t}W_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{2}\rrbracket+4J_{t}\llbracket H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})^{3}\rrbracket

(8.9)

so that, we have

\ell(u)_{t}:=\ell^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}_{\infty}(u)_{t}=u_{t}+\mathbb{U}_{t}+J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\succ Z_{t}^{\flat}(u)).

(8.10)

We will now regularize
\ell(u)
. In order for our regularization to exist, we will need to modify the paracontrolled part of the ansatz. For
\eta>0
, define

\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2,\leq}:=P_{|k|\leq\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa/2}}^{\eta}},\,\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2,>}:=\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}-\mathbb{W}_{T}^{2,\leq},

(8.11)

where
P_{|k|\leq C}
denotes the projection on the fourier modes
\leq C
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times L\mathbb{T}
. Note that here we are implicitly viewing these objects as periodic distributions.
We will choose
\eta
so that, by Bernstein’s inequality, there exists
c>0
sufficiently small such that

\|\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2,>}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1-\kappa}}\leq c.

(8.12)

Define the regularized ansatz map

\tilde{\ell}(u):=-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2,\leq}\succ Z_{t}^{\flat}(u))+\ell^{\infty}(u),

(8.13)

corresponding to taking the full remainder of
u
and subtracting the low frequency part of the paracontrolled term. Furthermore, by using the definition and the triangle inequality, we have that there exists
C>0
such that

\|\tilde{\ell}(u)\|_{\mathcal{L}}\leq C\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}}+C.

(8.14)

We now define the regularized remainder map
{\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}u
as the implicit solution of the equation

{\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}\,u=-\mathbb{U}_{t}-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2,>}\succ Z_{t}^{\flat}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}u))+{\rm reg}_{\varepsilon;x}\Big(\tilde{\ell}(u)\Big),

(8.15)

where, for every
\tilde{u}\in\mathcal{L}
,
{\rm reg}_{\varepsilon;x}\tilde{u}
is the convolution of
\tilde{u}
(viewed as a periodic distribution) in space at scale
\varepsilon
– c.f. (
4.94
).
The fact that this equation has a solution follows immediately from standard properties of convolution, paraproduct estimates, and the Banach contraction mapping theorem in the space
\mathcal{L}
. We omit the details.

Remark 8.5
.

The use of a contraction mapping argument in the regularization justifies the need for the high frequency decomposition of
\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}
depending on the norm, and for
c
has to be chosen sufficiently small for this to work).

Lemma
8.4
follows from the following properties of the regularized remainder map by adapting the arguments used in the proof of Lemma
4.15
using Lemma
4.20
.

Lemma 8.6
.

There exists
C>0
such that for every
\varepsilon>0
, the following statements hold.

(i)

For every
u\in\mathcal{L}
,

\|{\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}(u)\|_{\mathcal{L}}\leq C\left(\|\Xi_{\infty}(W)\|_{\mathfrak{S}}+\|\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}}\right).

(8.16)

(ii)

For every
T\in(0,\infty]
and
u\in\mathcal{L}
,

\|Z_{T}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}(u))\|_{L^{4}}\leq\left(\|\Xi_{\infty}(W)\|_{\mathfrak{S}}+\|\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}}+\|Z_{T}(u)\|_{L^{4}}\right).

(8.17)

(iii)

For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
c_{\varepsilon}>0
such that, for every
u\in\mathcal{L}
,

\|\ell^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}_{\infty}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}(u))\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq c_{\varepsilon}\left((1+\|\Xi_{\infty}(W)\|_{\mathfrak{S}}+\|\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}})^{4}+\|Z_{\infty}(u)\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}\right).

(8.18)

Furthemore, the map
u\to\ell_{\infty}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon})
is a continuous from
\mathcal{L}_{w}\to\mathcal{H}

Proof.

Property (i) and (ii) are straightforward consequences of the definition of
{\rm rem}
and
{\rm reg}
. Let us turn to property (iii). Note that

\displaystyle\ell^{\infty}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}u)

\displaystyle=\ell^{\infty}\left(-\mathbb{U}_{t}-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2,>}\succ Z_{t}^{\flat}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}u))+{\rm reg}_{\varepsilon;x}\Big(\tilde{\ell}(u)\Big)\right)

(8.19)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{U}_{t}-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2,>}\succ Z_{t}^{\flat}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}u))+{\rm reg}_{\varepsilon;x}\Big(\tilde{\ell}(u)\Big)

(8.20)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{U}_{t}+J_{t}\left(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2}\succ Z_{t}^{\flat}\left(-\mathbb{U}_{t}-J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2,>}\succ Z_{t}^{\flat}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}u))+{\rm reg}_{\varepsilon;x}\Big(\tilde{\ell}(u)\Big)\right)\right)

(8.21)

\displaystyle=J_{t}(\mathbb{W}_{t}^{2,\leq}\succ Z_{t}^{\flat}({\rm rem}_{\varepsilon}u))+{\rm reg}_{\varepsilon;x}(\tilde{\ell}(u)).

(8.22)

Then the desired result follows by standard estimates.
∎

8.3
Convergence and the proof of Theorem
3.18

We now turn to the proof of Theorem
3.18
. The key inputs are the uniform equicoercivity and
\Gamma
-convergence of the bulk cost functions (which represent the amplitudes). Using the uniform bounds on the remainder terms together with the approximation lemmas above, the following two lemmas follow by identical arguments as in the proofs of Lemmas
4.17
and
4.18
in the boundary case – we omit the details.

Lemma 8.7

(Equicoercivity)
.

Let
(\Xi^{\partial}_{T})_{T>0}
converge to
\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}
. There exists a sequentially compact set
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}(\sup_{0<T\leq\infty}\|\Xi^{\partial}_{T}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}
such that, for every
T\in(0,\infty]
,

\inf_{\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\Xi_{T}^{\partial}}_{T}(\mu)=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{K}}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{T}}_{T}(\mu).

(8.23)

Lemma 8.8

(
\Gamma
-convergence)
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}
.

(i)

(Fatou) For every
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}
, for every sequence
\mu_{n}\rightarrow\mu
in
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
, every sequence
(T_{n})\rightarrow\infty
, and every sequence
\Xi^{\partial}_{T_{n}}\rightarrow\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}
,

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}}_{\infty}(\mu)\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{T_{n}}}_{T_{n}}(\mu_{n}).

(8.24)

(ii)

(Recovery sequence) For every
\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}
, for every sequence
(T_{n})\rightarrow\infty
, and every sequence
\Xi^{\partial}_{T_{n}}\rightarrow\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}
, there exists a sequence
\mu_{n}\rightarrow\mu
in
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
such that

\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}}_{\infty}(\mu)\geq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{T_{n}}}_{T_{n}}(\mu_{n}).

(8.25)

Proof of Theorem
3.18
.

Part I follows from Propositions
5.15
and
8.2
. Part III follows from the bounds established on the
\Upsilon^{i}
in Section
6
and the
R_{i}
in Section
7
(see Proposition
7.1
), and the extension of these estimates to
T=\infty
(see Remark
8.1
).

It remains to establish Part II, the convergence of the amplitudes. As a consequence of the
\Gamma
-convergence of Lemma
8.8
, the equicoercivity of Lemma
8.7
, and the fundamental theorem of
\Gamma
-convergence
[
DM93
]
, we have that for every
f
and every sequence
\Xi^{\partial}_{T}\rightarrow\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}
,

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\min_{\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}}{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{T}}_{T}(\mu)=\min_{\mu\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}}{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}^{f;\Xi^{\partial}_{T}}_{\infty}(\mu).

(8.26)

Consequently, if
\Xi^{\partial}_{T}\to\Xi^{\partial}_{\infty}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{T}^{\sigma}(f,\Xi_{T}^{\partial})=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\sigma}(f,\Xi_{\infty}^{\partial}),\qquad\forall f\in C^{\infty}(M),

(8.27)

as required.
∎

9
Smallfield reduction

In this section, we prove Lemma
3.23
which in turn fills the remaining unproven claim used in the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Lemma
3.23
.

For every
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}
i.i.d. distributed according to
\nu^{0}
, let us define

\displaystyle E_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})

\displaystyle:=E_{\nu^{0}_{T}}\Big[\mathcal{A}_{T}^{-}(f\mid\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})\mathcal{A}^{+}_{T}(f\mid\varphi^{0},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})

(9.1)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times e^{c\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\varphi^{0})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}}^{\alpha}+c\|\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi^{0},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}^{\prime}}^{\alpha}}\Big].

(9.2)

Recall that we wish to establish that
\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0}
-almost surely, there exists
C>0
such that

\sup_{T}E_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\leq C

(9.3)

Let
T>0
be fixed. By Definition
3.16
and the Markov property for
\mu
, Proposition
2.3
— see the calculations in the proof of Proposition
3.17
— we have that
E_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
is equal to

\mathcal{A}^{\rm free}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})\mathcal{E}_{T}(\varphi_{-}\ast\rho_{T},\varphi_{+}\ast\rho_{T})\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\exp\left(\langle f,\varphi\rangle-U_{T}^{\alpha}(\varphi_{T}\mid\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\right)\right].

(9.4)

Above, the potential
U_{T}^{\alpha}(\varphi,\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})
is equal by definition to the quantity

V_{T}(\varphi_{T}\mid\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})+c\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},(\varphi_{T})_{0})\|^{\alpha}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}}+c\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}((\varphi_{T})_{0},\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+})\|^{\alpha}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}},

(9.5)

where
(\varphi_{T})_{0}=\varphi_{T}(0,\cdot)
is the restriction of
\varphi_{T}
to
\tau=0
.

Since the constant may depend on
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}
and also
f
, we can ignore the contribution of the free amplitude and the term
\mathcal{E}_{T}(\varphi_{-}\ast\rho_{T},\varphi_{+}\ast\rho_{T})
. It is sufficient to analyze the expectation term, which we denote by
E_{2,T}
.

The term
E_{2,T}
can be analyzed similarly as the bulk amplitudes in Sections
5
-
8
. Applying the Boué-Dupuis formula and using the same notation as before, the desired estimate on
E_{2,T}
amounts to establishing that there exists
C>0
such that for every
T>0
,

\inf_{v\in\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{F}_{2,T}(v)\geq-C,

(9.6)

where the cost function
\mathbb{F}_{2,T}(v)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{2,T}(v)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathcal{V}_{T}(W_{T},Z_{T}(v))+c\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},(W_{T}+Z_{T}(v)+H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})_{0})\|^{\alpha}

(9.7)

\displaystyle\qquad+c\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{+},(W_{T}+Z_{T}(v)+H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})_{0}\|^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\|v\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\Bigg].

(9.8)

We note that, since we are only interested in bounds, a
\Gamma
-convergence argument to deduce full convergence is not necessary.

The potential term
\mathcal{V}_{T}(W_{T},Z_{T}(v))
is treated identically as in Section
5
and it remains to modify the arguments to estimate the second and third terms. By symmetry, we will estimate the second term, i.e. the one with
\varphi_{-}
. First of all, note that
(W_{T})_{0}
exists by the Markov property and its law is given by boundary Gaussian with covariance given by the Dirichlet-to-Neumann kernel mollified at scale
T
, i.e. the pushforward of
\tilde{\mu}^{0}
under the regularization map
\varphi^{0}\mapsto(\varphi^{0})_{T}
. For the term
Z_{T}(v)
, applying the ansatz established in Section
5
, by the intermediate ansatz (
5.43
) we have that
Z_{T}(v)=-\mathbb{W}_{t}^{[3]}+K_{T}
. The first component can be restricted to the
\tau=0
slice with regularity in
\mathcal{C}^{1/2-}_{z}
, and by trace theorem we have that the restriction of the
H^{1-}
component belongs to
H^{1/2-}
. Finally, the harmonic extension term is smooth away from
\partial M
and hence smooth on
B
. Thus,

(Z_{T}(v)+H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})_{0}\in H^{1/2-}(B).

(9.9)

Combining the above, we have that
(W_{T}+Z_{T}(v)+H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})_{T})_{0}
is an admissible law on boundary conditions under the measure
\mathbb{P}
arising from the Boué-Dupuis formula. By abuse of notation, we will therefore write this term as
W_{T}^{0}+Z_{T}^{0}
.

Remark 9.1
.

Although in the definition of admissible law,
W_{T}^{0}
is distributed according to the boundary Gaussian field
\mu^{0}
, we may replace
\mu^{0}
by the boundary Gaussian with Dirichlet-to-Neumann covariance
\tilde{\mu}^{0}
with straightforward modifications. One way to see this is to use the Boué-Dupuis representation of
(W_{T})_{0}
in terms of a stochastic series. By direct calculation, one can then show that
(W_{T})_{0}
is equal almost surely to a random variable distributed according to
\mu^{0}
plus a smoothing term of Sobolev regularity greater than
\frac{1}{2}
.

Recall that
\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},W_{T}^{0}+Z_{T}^{0})
consists of the Wick powers of harmonic extensions the individual boundary fields as well as the random variables
\{\Upsilon_{2,T},\Upsilon_{3,T},\Upsilon_{4,T},\Upsilon_{5,T}\}
defined in Section
5
. We emphasize from the outset that the term
\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-}
is treated as deterministic in this lemma. For the Wick powers, we may directly apply Proposition
4.3
. For the random vairables
\Upsilon_{i,T}
, we have shown in Section
6
that there exists
\alpha>0
such that, for every
\delta>0
, there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
— depending on
\varphi_{-}
— such that

\mathbb{E}[|\Upsilon_{i,T}|^{\alpha}]\leq c_{1}+\delta\mathbb{E}[\|Z^{0}_{T}\|_{H^{1/2-}_{z}}^{2}]\leq c_{2}+\delta\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\|Z_{T}(v)\|_{L^{4}_{x}}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(v)\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right]\right),

(9.10)

where
\ell^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}_{T}(v)
is the residual drift entropy induced by the full drift ansatz, see Definition
5.9
.
Thus we have established that, with
\alpha
chosen as above, for every
\delta>0
there exists
C>0
depending on
\varphi_{-}
, such that

\mathbb{E}[\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\rho_{T}\ast\varphi_{-},(W_{T}+Z_{T}(v)+H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}))_{0})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}}^{\alpha}]\leq C+\delta\left(\mathbb{E}\|Z_{T}(v)\|^{4}_{L^{4}}+\frac{1}{2}\|\ell_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(v)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\right).

(9.11)

Adding these estimates to the estimates developed in Sections
6
and
7
establishes (
9.6
).

∎

10
The
\varphi^{4}_{3}
Hamiltonian

In this section we prove Theorem
1.5
. Namely, we will construct the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
Hamiltonian and prove some fundamental spectral properties such as discreteness of spectrum.

10.1
Tightness of the ground state of regularized
\varphi^{4}_{3}
Hamiltonians

We begin by proving a technical result that will be crucial to our strategy to construct the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
Hamiltonian. For every
T\geq 0
, we will introduce a cutoff Hamiltonian with discrete spectrum and which has a simple lowest eigenvalue whose associated normalized eigenvector is positive almost surely. These are called ground states for the cutoff Hamiltonian. We will then prove that these ground states form a tight sequence.

Let
T\in[0,\infty)
. Recall the cutoff boundary measure
\nu^{0}_{T}
. Define the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{T}:=L^{2}(\nu^{0}_{T})
.
Let
(\mathbf{P}_{\tau}^{T})_{\tau\geq 0}
be the semigroup acting on bounded functions
f:\mathcal{H}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\mathbf{P}_{\tau}^{T}f(\varphi):=\int f(\varphi^{\prime})\mathcal{A}_{\tau}^{T}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\nu^{0}_{T}(d\varphi^{\prime}),\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{T},\qquad\forall\tau>0,

(10.1)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}^{T}
is the regularized
\varphi^{4}_{3}
amplitude on
[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
defined in Definition
3.16
. Furthermore, let
\mathbf{P}^{T}_{0}={\rm Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{T}}
.

The following proposition follows from the classical theory of Schrödinger operators with semibounded potentials. We refer to
[
RS78
, Chapter 11-12]
for further detail (see also the proof of discrete spectrum in Section
10.3
).

Proposition 10.1
.

Let
T\in[0,\infty)
. There exists a self-adjoint linear map
\mathbf{H}_{T}:D(\mathbf{H}_{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{T}
, where
D(\mathbf{H}_{T})\subset\mathcal{H}_{T}
is a dense linear subspace, such that
\mathbf{H}_{T}
is the infinitesimal generator of
(\mathbf{P}^{T}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
. The linear map
\mathbf{H}_{T}
has discrete spectrum
\sigma(\mathbf{H}_{T})\subset[E_{0}^{T},\infty)
where
E_{0}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}
is the lowest eigenvalue which is simple and its associated normalized eigenvector
e_{0}^{T}
can be chosen such that
e_{0}^{T}>0

\nu^{0}_{T}
-almost surely.

Remark 10.2
.

Our regularized
\varphi^{4}_{3}
Hamiltonian
\mathbf{H}_{T}
differs from the Hamiltonian considered in
[
Gli68
]
, where a spectral cutoff is used directly in (the quadratic form of) the Hamiltonian.

The bounds on the amplitude derived in Theorem
3.18
imply that the lowest eigenvalues are uniformly bounded in
T\geq 0
, as formalized in the proposition below. The proof follows from a simplification
15
15

15

Here, the simplification comes from the fact we already know the spectrum is discrete, so no spectral measure decomposition is required.
of the arguments used below to prove the corresponding result when
T=\infty
, see Proposition
10.19
.

Proposition 10.3
.

There exists
C>0
such that

-C\leq\inf_{T}E_{0}^{T}\leq\sup_{T}E_{0}^{T}\leq C.

(10.2)

We now turn to "tightness" for the
ground states

e_{0}^{T}
. We begin by showing that their
L^{q}(\nu^{0}_{T})
norms are uniformly bounded for some
q>2
.

Lemma 10.4
.

There exists
q>2
and
c_{0}>0
such that

\sup_{T}\int(e^{T}_{0})^{q}d\nu^{0}_{T}\leq c_{0}.

(10.3)

Proof.

Recall that by definition of
e_{0}^{T}
, for every
\tau>0
, almost surely in
\varphi
we have

e_{0}^{T}(\varphi)=e^{E^{T}_{0}\tau}\int\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\tau}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})e_{0}^{T}(\varphi^{\prime})\nu_{T}^{0}(d\varphi^{\prime}).

(10.4)

By Proposition
10.3
, the term
e^{E_{0}^{T}\tau}
is uniformly bounded in
T
(provided
\tau
is fixed).

Let us now consider the integral term. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and gluing property (with cutoff) of Proposition
3.17
, there exists
c_{1}>0
such that for every
T>0
,

\left(\int\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\tau}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})e^{T}_{0}(\varphi^{\prime})\nu_{T}^{0}(d\varphi^{\prime})\right)^{2}\leq c_{1}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{2\tau}(\varphi,\varphi)

(10.5)

We deduce that

e_{0}^{T}(\varphi)^{2}\leq c_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2\tau}^{T}(\varphi,\varphi).

(10.6)

Furthmore, from the bounds on the amplitudes in Theorem
3.18
, we deduce the existence of
c_{3},p>0
such that

e_{0}^{T}(\varphi)\leq c_{2}\exp(\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi,\varphi)\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}([0,2\tau]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{p}).

(10.7)

Let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small. By (
10.6
) and (
10.7
), and the bound on the amplitude, there exists
\alpha>0
and
c_{3}>0
such that, uniformly in
T
,

\int e_{0}^{T}(\varphi)^{2+\delta}\nu^{0}_{T}(d\varphi)\leq c_{3}\int\mathcal{A}^{T}_{2\tau}(\varphi,\varphi)\exp(\|\Xi^{\partial}_{T}(\varphi,\varphi)\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}([0,2\tau]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{\alpha})\nu^{0}_{T}(d\varphi).

(10.8)

The right-hand side of (
10.8
) can then be estimated by a straightforward adaptation of the reasoning in Section
9
to prove Lemma
3.23
.

∎

Remark 10.5
.

In the proof of Lemma
10.4
we use that
e_{0}^{T}
is an eigenvector heavily. However, we only use that it is associated to the lowest eigenvlaue
E_{0}^{T}
when we assert that
e^{E_{0}^{T}\tau}
is bounded uniformly in
T
. For higher order eigenvalues, the bound may in principle depend on
T
. We will instead apply this same argument to the eigenvectors of
\mathbf{H}

after
we have proved that its spectrum is discrete.

Let us also point out that the bounds above, particularly (
10.8
), can be used to estimate the amplitude in a higher order norm.

Corollary 10.6
.

There exists
q>2
and
c_{q}>0
such that

\sup_{T\in[0,\infty)}\|\mathcal{A}_{\tau}^{T}\|_{L^{q}(\nu^{0}_{T};L^{2}(\nu^{0}_{T}))}\leq c_{q}.

(10.9)

In order to upgrade Lemma
10.4
to a true tightness result on the eigenvectors, we will express
e_{0}^{T}
in terms of the simultaneous coupling of the boundary fields
(\nu^{0}_{T})_{T\geq 0}
and
\nu^{0}=\nu^{0}_{\infty}
.

Recall that
\nu^{0}_{T}={\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{0}}(W^{0}_{T}+Z^{0}_{T})
and
\nu^{0}={\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{0}}(W^{0}_{\infty}+Z^{0}_{\infty})
, where
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
is the probability space and
(W^{0}+Z^{0})
is the process constructed in Theorem
3.14
and Section
4
. We implicitly redefine our Hilbert spaces and operators as acting on this coupling space. Thus have
{\rm Law}_{\nu^{0}_{T}}(e_{0}^{T})={\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{0}}(e_{0}^{T}(W^{0}_{T}+Z^{0}_{T}))
.

We now turn to tightness of the ground states.

Proposition 10.7
.

There exists a random variable
e_{0}
defined on the coupling space that is a measurable function of
W^{0}_{\infty}+Z^{0}_{\infty}
, i.e.
e_{0}=e_{0}(W^{0}_{\infty}+Z^{0}_{\infty})
, such that
\|e_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{0})}=1
and, up to a subsequence,

e_{0}^{T}(W_{T}^{0}+Z^{0}_{T})\rightarrow e_{0}(W^{0}_{\infty}+Z^{0}_{\infty}),

(10.10)

where the convergence can be taken as either
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-almost sure or in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{0})
. Furthermore,
e_{0}>0
almost surely with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{0}
and for
q>2
sufficiently close to
2
, there exists
C>0
such that

\|e_{0}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{P}^{0})}\leq C.

(10.11)

Proof.

For convenience, let us write
\tilde{e}_{0}^{T}:=e_{0}^{T}(W_{T}^{0}+Z_{T}^{0})
. Since
\|\tilde{e}_{0}^{T}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{0})}=1
for every
T
, the sequence
(\tilde{e}_{0}^{T})_{T>0}
is compact in the weak
-*
topology of
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{0})
. Let us write
\tilde{e}_{0}
to denote a limit point. We wish to show that
\|\tilde{e}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{0})}=1
, from which we can deduce that, for the associated subsequence,
\|\tilde{e}_{0}^{T}-\tilde{e}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{0})}\rightarrow 0
(i.e. weak
-*
plus convergence of norms implies strong convergence). Upon taking another subsequence, we obtain the almost sure convergence. Let us also observe that
\tilde{e}_{0}=e_{0}(W_{\infty}^{0}+Z_{\infty}^{0})
, for some
e_{0}
, in terms of measurability. In order to see this, note that each
e_{0}^{T}
is measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
(W^{0}+Z^{0})_{t\geq T}
, and hence the intersection of these
\sigma
-algebras. We thus define the
e_{0}
of the proposition to be equal to
\tilde{e}_{0}
.

Consider now a subsequence
(\tilde{e}^{T_{n}}_{0})_{n}
with limit point
\tilde{e}_{0}
in the weak
-*
topology. Recall the
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{0})}
-norms are equal to
1
along the whole subsequence. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality the sequence as random variables are tight and thus converge in law up to a further subsequence — not relabelled — to a random variable
\hat{e}_{0}
, perhaps defined on a different probability space, whose law coincides with the law of
\tilde{e}_{0}
under
\mathbb{P}^{0}
. By Skorokhod embedding, we may assume without loss of generality that
\tilde{e}_{0}^{T_{n}}\rightarrow\hat{e}_{0}
almost surely and thus, thanks to the uniform
L^{p}
boundedness of
(\tilde{e}_{0}^{T_{n}})
of Lemma
10.4
for some
p>2
and Vitali’s convergence theorem, we have that
\tilde{e}_{0}^{T_{n}}\rightarrow\hat{e}_{0}
in
L^{2}
. In particular, we have that the original limiting random variable we consider,
\tilde{e}_{0}
, satisfies
\|\tilde{e}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{0})}=1
.

Let us now turn to the prove that
\tilde{e}_{0}>0
almost surely. Recall that for every
T<\infty
,

e_{0}^{T}(W^{0}_{T}+Z^{0}_{T})=e^{E_{0}^{T}\tau}\int\mathcal{A}_{\tau}^{T}(W^{0}_{T}+Z^{0}_{T},\tilde{W}^{0}_{T}+\tilde{Z}^{0}_{T})e_{0}^{T}(\tilde{W}^{0}_{T}+\tilde{Z}^{0}_{T})d\mathbb{P}^{0}(\tilde{W}^{0}+\tilde{Z}^{0}).

(10.12)

Up to another subsequence, we have that
(\tilde{e}_{0}^{T_{n}})
converges almost surely to
\tilde{e}_{0}
. Taking limits on both sides of above, by Fatou’s lemma, the convergence of the amplitudes in Theorem
3.18
, and Proposition
10.3
, we have that there exists
C>0
such that

e_{0}(W^{0}_{\infty}+Z^{0}_{\infty})\geq e^{-C\tau}\int\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{\tau}(W^{0}_{\infty}+Z^{0}_{\infty},\tilde{W}^{0}_{\infty}+\tilde{Z}^{0}_{\infty})e_{0}(\tilde{W}^{0}_{\infty}+\tilde{Z}^{0}_{\infty})d\mathbb{P}(\tilde{W}^{0}+\tilde{Z}^{0})>0.

(10.13)

Thus
e_{0}((W^{0}+Z^{0})_{\infty})>0
almost surely.

Finally, we prove the
L^{q}
bound. This follows immediately by Fatou’s lemma together with the uniform bound on
L^{q}
norms of the approximations in Lemma
10.4
.
∎

10.2
Construction of the Hamiltonian

In this subsection, we will construct the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
Hamiltonian on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
as an unbounded linear map
\mathbf{H}
acting on a dense domain
D(\mathbf{H})\subset\mathcal{H}:=L^{2}(\nu^{0})
. To begin with, let us define the semigroup
(\mathbf{P}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
acting on
\mathcal{H}
such that
\mathbf{P}_{0}={\rm Id}
and for every
\tau>0
and for every bounded function
f:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbf{P}_{\tau}f(\varphi):=\int f(\varphi^{\prime})\mathcal{A}_{\tau}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\nu^{0}(d\varphi^{\prime}),\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{H}.

(10.14)

Above,
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}
is an abuse of notation to refer to the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
amplitude on the cylinder
[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
. We will construct
\mathbf{H}
as the generator of
(\mathbf{P}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
, i.e.
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}=\exp(-\tau\mathbf{H})
for every
\tau\geq 0
.

Proposition 10.8
.

There exists a self-adjoint linear map
\mathbf{H}
acting on a dense domain
D(\mathbf{H})\subset\mathcal{H}
such that

\mathbf{H}f:=-\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\tau}(\mathbf{P}_{\tau}-{\rm Id}_{\mathcal{H}})f,\quad\forall f\in D(\mathbf{H}),

(10.15)

where the convergence is in norm.

Definition 10.9
.

The
\varphi^{4}_{3}
Hamiltonian is the linear map
\mathbf{H}
.

In order to prove Proposition
10.8
, we will use the classical Hille-Yosida theorem. We will therefore need to show strong continuity of
(\mathbf{P}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
at
\tau=0
and an exponential bound (in
\tau
) on their operator norms.

We will use the regularized semigroups considered in the preceding subsection in order to prove strong continuity of the semigroup
(\mathbf{P}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
. Recall that by Proposition
10.1
, the ground state
e_{0}^{T}
of
\mathbf{H}_{T}
is strictly positive almost surely. Therefore, we may conjugate the semigroup
(\mathbf{P}^{T}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
by
(e_{0}^{T})^{-1}
. This gives rise to a so-called
ground state-transformed semigroup
that has been considered in the context of one-dimensional Schrödinger operators, see
[
LHB20
]
and references therein. Let
\overline{\nu}_{T}^{0}(d\varphi):=e_{0}^{T}(\varphi)^{2}\nu_{T}^{0}(d\varphi)
and denote
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}:=L^{2}(\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T})
. For every
\tau>0
, define the map
\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{T}_{\tau}
acting on bounded functions
f:\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{T}_{\tau}(f)(\varphi)=e^{-E_{0}^{T}\tau}\int\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\tau}^{T}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})}{e^{T}_{0}(\varphi)e^{T}_{0}(\varphi^{\prime})}f(\varphi^{\prime})\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}(d\varphi^{\prime}),\qquad\forall\varphi\in\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}.

(10.16)

Furthermore, let
\overline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}:={\rm Id}_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}}
.

The semigroup
(\overline{\mathbf{P}}_{\tau}^{T})_{\tau\geq 0}
has a generator that is a positive linear map acting on
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}
. One way to see this is that, for every
\tau\geq 0
,
\overline{\mathbf{P}}_{\tau}^{T}
is obtained from
\mathbf{P}_{t}^{T}
by conjugating with the multiplication map by
(e_{0}^{T})^{-1}
and shifting by
e^{-E_{0}^{T}\tau}
. Thus,

\overline{\mathbf{P}}_{\tau}=(e_{0}^{T})^{-1}e^{-\tau(\mathbf{H}_{T}+E_{0}^{T})}(e_{0}^{T})^{-1}.

(10.17)

From this and the fact that
e_{0}^{T}
is strictly positive almost everywhere, we have that the generator of
(\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{T}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
exists and is equal to
(e_{0}^{T})^{-1}(\mathbf{H}_{T}+E_{0}^{T})(e_{0}^{T})^{-1}
. We deduce that its spectrum satisfies
\sigma(\overline{\mathbf{H}}_{T})\subset[0,\infty)
. Thus, for every
\tau>0
we have that the operator norm satisfies
\|\overline{\mathbf{P}}_{\tau}^{T}\|_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}}\leq 1
.

In the sequel, we will exploit that
(\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{T}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
can be associated to a Markov process. This is formalized in the lemma below, which is a consequence of
[
LHB20
, Section 3.10.2]
.

Lemma 10.10
.

For every
T\in[0,\infty)
,
(\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{T}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
is the transition semigroup of a Markov process
\tilde{X}_{T}
with continuous sample paths and stationary measure
\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}
.

Remark 10.11
.

By an abuse of notation, we will assume that
\tilde{X}_{T}
is defined on some probability space labelled
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Given
\varphi\in H^{-1/2-\kappa}_{z}
, we will write
\mathbb{P}_{\varphi}
to denote the process
\tilde{X}_{T}
conditioned on
\tilde{X}_{T}(0,\cdot)=\varphi(\cdot)
. Finally, we will further abuse notation and write
\tilde{X}_{T}
to denote the two-sided process obtained, conditionally on
\tilde{X}_{T}(0,\cdot)
, by concatenating two independent copies.

Below, we will consider bridge measures associated to the two-sided process
\tilde{X}_{T}
by singularly conditioning the process on the two endpoints. We will only need the case of
[-L,L]
for an arbitrary
L>0
. Let
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}
be sampled independently according to
\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}
. Almost surely, we may define a stochastic process
X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}\in L^{2}([-L,L],H^{-1/2-\kappa}_{z})
whose law is characterized
16
16

16

We will abuse notation and write the probability space as
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
.
by the following Laplace transform. For every
f\in L^{2}_{\tau}H^{1/2+\kappa}_{z}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\langle f,X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\tau}H^{-1/2-\kappa}_{z}}\right)\right]\\
:=\frac{1}{Z_{T}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\exp(-\langle f,\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}_{\tau}(H_{z}^{-1/2-\varepsilon})}-V_{T}(\varphi+H(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})))].

(10.18)

Remark 10.12
.

By approximation, we may allow for the term
\langle f,X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}\rangle
to be replaced by
f(X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}})
for continuous functions
f
of linear growth.

The following lemma asserts that the processes defined above are bridge processes derived from
\tilde{X}_{T}
. It is a consequence of the DLR property of the measures on the righthand side of (
10.18
). See
[
LHB20
, Equation (4.1.17) and Proposition 4.1]
.

Lemma 10.13
.

For every
T\in[0,\infty)
and almost surely for every
\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}\in H^{-1/2-\kappa}_{z}
,

{\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\tilde{X}_{T}|_{[-L,L]}\mid\tilde{X}_{T}(-L,\cdot)=\varphi_{-},\tilde{X}_{T}(L,\cdot)=\varphi_{+})={\rm Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}})

(10.19)

Let us consider the set
{\rm Lip}_{b}:=\{f:H^{-1/2-\kappa}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:f\text{ is Lipschitz and bounded}\}\subset\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}
. We have that
{\rm Lip}_{b}
is dense linear subspace of
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}
for every
T>0
. The following lemma contains the key technical estimate that we will need in order to prove strong continuity of the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
semigroup.

Lemma 10.14
.

For every
f\in{\rm Lip}_{b}
, we have that

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0}\sup_{T}\|\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{T}_{\tau}f-f\|_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}}=0.

(10.20)

Proof.

Let
\tau,T>0
. Recall the definition of
\overline{\mathbf{P}}_{t}^{T}
and its associated Markov process defined by Lemma
10.10
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and the tower property of conditional expectation, we have that

\int(\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{T}_{\tau}f-f)^{2}d\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f(\tilde{X}_{T}(\tau))-f(\tilde{X}_{T}(0))\right)^{2}\right].

(10.21)

Hence, by conditioning on the values of the process at
-L
and
L
, and using Lemma
10.13
, we have that

\eqref{eq: overline p bound 1}\leq I_{1}+I_{2},

(10.22)

where

\displaystyle I_{1}=\int\int\mathbbm{1}_{\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}}\leq K}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f(X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(\tau))-f(X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(0))\right)^{2}\right]d\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}d\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T},

(10.23)

\displaystyle I_{2}=\int\int\mathbbm{1}_{\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}}>K}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f(X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(\tau))-f(X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}(0))\right)^{2}\right]d\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}d\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}.

(10.24)

To estimate
I_{1}
, first note that by the fact that
f
is Lipschitz continuous there exists
c_{1}>0
such that, for every
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

I_{1}\leq c_{1}\tau^{2\alpha}\int\int\mathbbm{1}_{\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}}\leq K}\mathbb{E}\left[\|X^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}_{T}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{-1/2-\kappa}_{z}}^{2}\right]d\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}d\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}.

(10.25)

We claim that, provided
\alpha<1/2
, there exists
c_{2}(K)>0
such that

\int\int\mathbbm{1}_{\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}}\leq K}\mathbb{E}\left[\|X^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}_{T}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{-1/2-\kappa}_{z}}^{2}\right]d\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}d\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}\leq c_{2}(K).

(10.26)

Hence, uniformly in
T
,

I_{1}\leq c_{1}c_{2}(K)\tau^{2\alpha}.

(10.27)

In order to establish the Hölder continuity estimate (
10.26
), we will apply the Boué-Dupuis formula to the right-hand side of (
10.18
) applied to

f(X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}})=\|X_{T}^{\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+}}\|_{C^{1/2-\kappa}_{\tau}H^{-1/2-\kappa}_{z}},

(10.28)

see Remark
10.12
. We may then repeat the analysis of the previous sections with the following observations to handle the norm term. Recall the intermediate ansatz on the drift. By 1d Sobolev embedding, for every
\alpha<1/2
, for every
\kappa>0
sufficiently small, there exists
\kappa^{\prime}>0
such that, for
c_{3}>0
,

\|Z_{T}(v)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{-1/2-\kappa}_{z}}\leq c_{3}\|Z_{T}(v)\|_{H^{1-\kappa^{\prime}}}.

(10.29)

This can then be estimated as usual.
Furthermore, we have that for every
p\in[1,\infty)
, there exists
c_{4}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbb{W}_{T}^{[3]}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2-\kappa}_{\tau}H^{-1/2-\kappa}_{z}}^{p}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbb{W}_{T}^{[3]}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2-\kappa}_{x}}^{p}]\leq c_{4}.

(10.30)

The rest of the argument follows as in Sections
5
-
8
(without the need for the
\Gamma
-convergence argument).

In order to estimate
I_{2}
, since
f
is bounded, it suffices to estimate
\mathbb{E}_{\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}\otimes\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}}[\mathbbm{1}_{\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}}>K}]
. By Markov’s inequality and changing the base measure, for every
\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists
c_{5}>0
such that

\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}\otimes\overline{\nu}^{0}_{T}\left(\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}}>K\right)\leq c_{5}K^{-\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{0}_{T}\otimes\nu^{0}_{T}}[\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}}^{\delta}e_{0}^{T}(\varphi_{-})^{2}e_{0}^{T}(\varphi_{+})^{2}].

(10.31)

By the bounds on the stochastic norms in Proposition
4.3
and the
(\Upsilon_{i})_{i\geq 2}
terms in Section
6
, for
\alpha^{\prime}>0
sufficiently small we have that

\sup_{T}\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{0}_{T}\otimes\nu^{0}_{T}}[\|\Xi_{T}^{\partial}(\varphi_{-},\varphi_{+})\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{B}}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}]<\infty.

(10.32)

Thus, by Hölder’s inequality, the above estimates, and the
L^{q}
uniform in
T
bounds on the ground states in Lemma
10.4
, there exists
c_{6}>0
such that, uniformly in
T
,

I_{2}\leq c_{6}K^{-\delta}.

(10.33)

The lemma now follows from the estimates on
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
by taking
\tau\rightarrow 0
and then
K\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

We now return to the problem of showing strong continuity of
(\mathbf{P}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
. Once again, we will work with the simultaneous coupling. Let us define
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}:=L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{0})
. We will isomorphically map the linear maps
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}^{T}
on to this space as follows. Let
T\in[0,\infty]
. Re-define, for every bounded
f:\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbf{P}_{\tau}^{T}f((W^{0}+Z^{0})_{T}):=\int f((W^{0}+Z^{0})_{T})\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\tau}\left((W^{0}+Z^{0})_{T},(\tilde{W}^{0}+\tilde{Z}^{0})_{T}\right)\,d\mathbb{P}^{0}(\tilde{W}^{0}+\tilde{Z}^{0}).

(10.34)

These isomorphisms preserve the corresponding operator norms. The results we obtain can then be translated back to the semigroup acting on
\mathcal{H}
by the isomorphism.

Remark 10.15
.

From now on, we only take the limit
T\rightarrow\infty
along the subsequence for which Proposition
10.7
is valid.

We begin with a preliminary estimate on the operator norm of
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}
.

Lemma 10.16
.

Let
\tau\geq 0
. For every
f\in{\rm Lip}_{b}
,

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}(e_{0}g)-\mathbf{P}_{\tau}^{T}(e_{0}^{T}g)\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}=0.

(10.35)

Proof.

The case
\tau=0
is a consequence of Proposition
10.7
. Let
\tau>0
. For compacity of notation, let us write
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=(W^{0}+Z^{0})
. Almost surely in
\mathbb{P}^{0}
, for
q>0
let us define the integral

I_{T}^{q}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}):=\int\left(e_{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime})f(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime})\mathcal{A}_{\tau}(\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime})-e_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{T}^{\prime})f(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{T}^{\prime})\mathcal{A}_{\tau}^{T}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{T},\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{T}^{\prime})\right)^{q}d\mathbb{P}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}).

(10.36)

Furthermore, write
I_{T}:=I_{T}^{1}
and write
\iota_{T}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime})
for the integrand in this case.
To prove the lemma, it is equivalent to show that

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int I_{T}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})^{2}d\mathbb{P}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})=0.

(10.37)

We will do this by Vitali’s convergence theorem.

First, we will show that the integrand when
q=1
,
\iota_{T}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime})\rightarrow 0
almost surely. The amplitudes in the integrand converge thanks to Theorem
3.18
, which is applicable since since the enhancement of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}_{T}
converges to the enhancement of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}
by Theorem
3.20
. Furthermore, since the limit
T\rightarrow\infty
is taken along the subsequence for which Proposition
10.7
holds, we have the almost sure convergence of
e_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}_{T})
to
e_{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime})
. Hence,
\iota_{T}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime})\rightarrow 0
since
f
is continuous and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}_{T}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}
.

We will now show that
I_{T}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\rightarrow 0
almost surely.
By Hölder’s inequality, the
L^{q}
bounds on
e_{0}^{T}
and
e_{0}
of Lemma
10.4
and Proposition
10.7
, and the gluing property of Theorem
1.2
, we have that, there exists
q_{0}>1
such that for every
q\in[1,q_{0}]
, there exists
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that, for every
T>0
,

I_{T}^{q}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\leq c_{1}\|f\|_{\infty}\max_{S\in\{T,\infty\}}\|\mathcal{A}_{\tau}^{S}(\boldsymbol{\varphi},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}^{q}\leq c_{2}\|f\|_{\infty}\max_{S\in\{T,\infty\}}\mathcal{A}^{S}_{2\tau}(\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\varphi})^{q}.

(10.38)

Hence, almost surely
I_{T}(\varphi)\rightarrow 0
by Vitali’s convergence theorem and the pointwise convergence of the
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}
integrand
\iota_{T}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime})
.

By applying (
10.38
) for
q=1
, we have that for every
p>2
, there exists
c_{3}>0
such that, for every
T>0
,

\|I_{T}\|_{L^{p}}\leq c_{3}\|f\|_{\infty}\max_{S\in\{T,\infty\}}\|\mathcal{A}^{S}_{2\tau}(\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\varphi})\|_{L^{p}(d\mathbb{P}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}))}.

(10.39)

Hence by Corollary
10.6
, taking
p
sufficiently close to
2
, we obtain that there exists
c_{4}>0
such that, for every
T>0
,

\|I_{T}\|_{L^{p}}\leq c_{4}\|f\|_{\infty}.

(10.40)

Thus, by Vitali’s convergence theorem, (
10.37
) holds.

∎

A simplification of the proof (redoing the estimates without the eigenvector) yields the convergence of
\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}^{T}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{T})}
to
\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}
. Thus we get the required exponential growth bound on the operator norms required to apply the Hille-Yosida theorem. We formalize this in the following proposition.

Proposition 10.17
.

There exists
C>0
such that

\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}\leq e^{C\tau}.

(10.41)

Proof.

By Lemma
10.16
and the fact that the isomorphisms of the semigroups on to
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}
preserves operator norms, we have that

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}^{T}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{T})}=\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}.

(10.42)

Hence, by the fact that
\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}^{T}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{T})}\leq e^{-E_{0}^{T}\tau}
for every
\tau\geq 0
, and the uniform bounds of Proposition
10.3
, there exists
C>0
such that

\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}\leq e^{C\tau}.

(10.43)

∎

We now prove strong continuity of
(\mathbf{P}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
.

Proposition 10.18
.

For every
f\in\mathcal{H}
,

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0}\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}f-f\|_{\mathcal{H}}=0.

(10.44)

Proof.

Recall the construction of
e_{0}
in Proposition
10.7
. Since
e_{0}>0
almost surely, we may define the measure
\overline{\nu}^{0}
by the density
\overline{\nu}^{0}(d\varphi):=e_{0}(\varphi)^{2}\nu^{0}(d\varphi)
and let
\overline{\mathcal{H}}:=L^{2}(\overline{\nu}^{0})
. Again, since
e_{0}>0
, the map
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}:\overline{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
,
f\mapsto e_{0}f
is an isometric bijection. Recall the definition of
{\rm Lip}_{b}
above. Since
{\rm Lip}_{b}\subset\overline{\mathcal{H}}
is dense, we have that the set

B:=\{e_{0}f:f\in{\rm Lip}_{b}\}\subset\mathcal{H}

(10.45)

is dense in
\mathcal{H}
. Thus it is sufficient to estimate

\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}(e_{0}g)-e_{0}g\|_{\mathcal{H}},

(10.46)

for
g\in{\rm Lip}_{b}
. By Lemma
10.16
,

\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}(e_{0}g)-e_{0}g\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\|\mathbf{P}^{T}_{\tau}(e_{0}^{T}g)-e_{0}^{T}g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{T}}.

(10.47)

Thus, since

\|\mathbf{P}_{\tau}^{T}(e_{0}^{T}g)-e_{0}^{T}g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{T}}=\|\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{T}_{\tau}(g)-g\|_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{T}},

(10.48)

the proposition follows from the uniform convergence to
0
on
{\rm Lip}_{b}
of the right-hand side as stated in Lemma
10.14
.
∎

We now prove Proposition
10.8
and thereby complete the construction of the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
Hamiltonian
\mathbf{H}
.

Proof of Proposition
10.8
.

The proposition follows by the Hille-Yosida theorem to the semigroup
(\mathbf{P}_{\tau})_{\tau\geq 0}
, which is applicable thanks to Propositions
10.17
and
10.18
.

∎

10.3
Spectral properties of
\mathbf{H}

We will now prove that the spectrum of
\mathbf{H}
, denoted by
\sigma(\mathbf{H})
, is discrete. Recall that by the Rayleigh-Ritz criterion,

\inf\sigma(\mathbf{H})=\frac{\langle\mathbf{H}\varphi,\varphi\rangle}{\|\varphi\|^{2}}.

(10.49)

By construction,
\sigma(\mathbf{H})\subset\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 10.19
.

The spectrum of
\mathbf{H}
is bounded from below, i.e.

\inf\sigma(\mathbf{H})>-\infty.

(10.50)

Proof.

Let
\tau\geq 1
. By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the gluing of amplitudes in Theorem
1.2
, for every
u\in\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{P}_{\tau}u,u\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|

\displaystyle\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\int\left(\int u(\varphi^{\prime})\mathcal{A}_{\tau}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\nu^{0}(d\varphi^{\prime})\right)^{2}\nu^{0}(d\varphi)\right)^{1/2}

(10.51)

\displaystyle=\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\int\int\int u(\varphi^{\prime})u(\varphi^{\prime\prime})\mathcal{A}_{\tau}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\mathcal{A}_{\tau}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime\prime})\nu^{0}(d\varphi^{\prime})\nu^{0}(d\varphi^{\prime\prime})\nu^{0}(d\varphi)\right)^{1/2}

(10.52)

\displaystyle=\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\int\int\mathcal{A}_{2\tau}(\varphi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime\prime})u(\varphi^{\prime})u(\varphi^{\prime\prime})\nu^{0}(d\varphi^{\prime})\nu^{0}(d\varphi^{\prime\prime})\right)^{1/2}.

(10.53)

Hence, again by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the gluing of amplitudes in Theorem
1.2
, and by the bounds on the periodic amplitudes (c.f. the main theorem of
[
BG20
]
), there exists
E_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

|\langle\mathbf{P}_{\tau}u,u\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left((\mathcal{A}_{4\tau}^{\rm per})^{1/2}\left(\int u(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}u(\varphi^{\prime\prime})^{2}\nu(d\varphi)\nu(d\varphi^{\prime})\right)^{1/2}\right)^{1/2}\leq e^{E_{0}t}\|u\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}.

(10.54)

In particular,
\|e^{-t(\mathbf{H}+E_{0})}\|_{\text{op}}\leq 1
. We claim that
(\mathbf{H}+E_{0})\geq 0
. Assume not. Let
P_{\mathbf{H}}
denote the projection-valued measure associated to
\mathbf{H}
coming from the spectral theorem. For every
a<0
let us consider
u_{a}\in\mathcal{H}
such that

\langle u_{a},P_{\mathbf{H}}((-\infty,a))u_{a}\rangle>0,

(10.55)

and write
\mu^{a}
to denote the finite Borel measure such that, for every Borel set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\langle u_{a},P_{\mathbf{H}}(\Omega)u_{a}\rangle=\int_{\Omega}\mu^{a}(d\lambda).

(10.56)

Note that such a non-zero projection exists by the assumption
\inf\sigma(\mathbf{H}+E_{0})=-\infty
.
By functional calculus,

\langle e^{-t(\mathbf{H}+E_{0})}u_{a},u_{a}\rangle=\int_{\lambda<-E_{0}}e^{-t(\lambda+E_{0})}\mu^{a}(d\lambda)+\int_{\lambda\geq-E_{0}}e^{-t(\lambda+E_{0})}\mu^{a}(d\lambda).

(10.57)

The second term is bounded uniformly in
t
. Let us analyze the first term for
a<-E_{0}
. Note that
(-\infty,a)
is of positive
\mu^{a}
-measure by construction. Thus, by monotone convergence

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\lambda<-E_{0}}e^{-t(\lambda+E_{0})}\mu^{a}(d\lambda)\geq\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\int_{(-\infty,a)}e^{-t(\lambda+E_{0})}\mu^{a}(d\lambda)=+\infty.

(10.58)

This gives a contradiction and hence
\mathbf{H}+E_{0}\geq 0
and hence
\inf\sigma(\mathbf{H})\geq-E_{0}
.
∎

Let us now prove that
\sigma(\mathbf{H})
is discrete. We follow the strategy of
[
GGV24
]
.

Proposition 10.20
.

The spectrum
\sigma(\mathbf{H})
is discrete and consists of eigenvalues of finite multiplicity with associated eigenvectors that form an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

For every
\tau>0
,
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}
is Hilbert-Schmidt. To see this, first note that
P_{\tau}
is an integral operator with kernel
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}
. It is sufficient to show that
\|\mathcal{A}_{\tau}\|_{L^{2}(\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0})}
is finite. By the gluing property, for every
\tau>0
, there exists
C(\tau)\geq 0
such that

\int\mathcal{A}_{\tau}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\mathcal{A}_{\tau}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\nu(d\varphi)\nu(d\varphi^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{2\tau}^{\rm per}\leq e^{C(\tau)}.

(10.59)

For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-E_{0},\infty)
, let us define the resolvent
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}:=(\mathbf{H}-\alpha)^{-1}
. By Proposition
10.19
,
\inf\sigma(\mathbf{H})>-\infty
, by functional calculus we have that
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. Since
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
is compact, we obtain
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
is compact.

Let
\alpha_{0}<-E_{0}
and consider the map

B(\lambda):=(\lambda-\alpha_{0})\mathbf{R}_{\alpha_{0}}.

(10.60)

Note that
B(\lambda)
is compact for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and
\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow\alpha_{0}}(\lambda-\alpha_{0})^{-1}(B(\lambda)-B(\alpha_{0}))=\mathbf{R}_{\alpha_{0}}
exists. Therefore, by the analytic Fredholm theorem,
({\rm Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-B(\lambda))^{-1}
exists outside of a discrete set of poles in
\mathbb{C}
. Now, by the resolvent identity, for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-E_{0},\infty)
,

\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{R}_{\alpha_{0}}+\mathbf{R}_{\alpha_{0}}\,(\alpha-\alpha_{0}){\rm Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}.

(10.61)

Hence, we obtain that the resolvent admits a meromorphic extension outside of a discrete set on
\mathbb{C}
(with poles of finite multiplicity). Thus
\sigma(\mathbf{H})
is discrete. Furthermore, since
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha_{0}}
is compact, it has an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors. Thus,
\mathbf{H}
admits an orthonormal basis eigenvectors.
∎

We now establish a Perron-Froebenius theorem on the lowest eigenvalue and associated ground state. By abuse of notation, let us write
E_{0}
for the lowest eigenvalue.

Proposition 10.21
.

The eigenvalue
E_{0}
is almost surely simple and its associated normalized eigenvector, which we denote by
e_{0}
, can be chosen so that
e_{0}>0
almost surely with respect to
\nu^{0}
.

Proof.

By
[
RS78
, Proposition XIII.44]
, it is sufficient to show that
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}
is positivity improving for every
\tau>0
. This means that for every
f\in\mathcal{H}
such that
f\geq 0
and
f
is not identically
0
almost surely, then we have that
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}f>0
almost surely. This follows from the fact that
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}
is positive
\nu^{0}\otimes\nu^{0}
-almost everywhere. ∎

Remark 10.22
.

Although we did not prove this, it is reasonable to expect that the random variable
e_{0}
constructed in Proposition
10.7
coincides with
e_{0}
above (hence the abuse of notation).

Finally, we end with uniform
L^{q}
bounds on the eigenvectors of
\mathbf{H}
. The proof follows from similar arguments as in Lemma
10.4
and thus we omit it.

Proposition 10.23
.

There exists
q>2
such that, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
c(q,i)>0
such that

\|e_{i}\|_{L^{q}(\nu^{0})}\leq c(q,i),

(10.62)

where
e_{i}
is a normalized eigenvector associated to eigenvalue
E_{i}
.

10.4
Proof of Theorem
1.5

We now combine the results established in this section in order to prove Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of theorem
1.5
.

The construction of the
\varphi^{4}_{3}
Hamiltonian is a consequence of Proposition
10.8
, see Definition
10.9
. The spectral properties and eigenvector bounds are a consequence of all of the results in Section
10.3
.

∎

Appendix A
Besov spaces, paraproducts, and resonant products

In this appendix, we recall some basic definitions and properties of Besov spaces and paradifferential calculus from
[
BCD11
, Sections 2.7 and 2.8]
.

A.1
Besov spaces

We define Besov spaces on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Let
B(x,r)
denote the ball centred at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
of radius
r>0
and let
A
denote the annulus
B(0,\frac{4}{3})\setminus B(0,\frac{3}{8})
. Let
\tilde{\Delta},\Delta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,1])
be radially symmetric and satisfy

•

\mathrm{supp}\tilde{\chi}\subset B(0,\frac{4}{3})
and
\mathrm{supp}\chi\subset A
;

•

\sum_{k\geq-1}\chi_{k}=1
, where
\chi_{-1}=\tilde{\chi}
and
\chi_{k}(\cdot)=\chi(2^{-k}\cdot)
for
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

Identify
\Delta_{k}
with its Fourier multiplier.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, we define the Besov spaces
B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to be the completion of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with respect to the norm

\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\Big\|\Big(2^{ks}\|\Delta_{k}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\Big)_{k\geq-1}\Big\|_{l^{q}}

(A.1)

where
l^{q}
is the usual space of
q
-summable sequences, interpreted as a supremum when
q=\infty
.

The following analytic estimates are standard.

Proposition A.1
.

The following estimates hold.

(i)

Duality.
Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\in[1,\infty]
such that
\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}=1
. Then

\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}fgdx\right|\leq\|f\|_{B^{-s}_{p_{1},q_{1}}}\|g\|_{B^{s}_{p_{2},q_{2}}},\qquad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

(A.2)

(ii)

Fractional Leibniz.
Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},q\in[1,\infty]
satisfy
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}=\frac{1}{p_{3}}+\frac{1}{p_{4}}
. Then there exists
C>0
such that

\|fg\|_{B^{s}_{p,q}}\leq C\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p_{1},q}}\|g\|_{L^{p_{2}}}+\|f\|_{L^{p_{3}}}\|g\|_{B^{s}_{p_{4},q}},\qquad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

(A.3)

(iii)

Interpolation.
Let
s,s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
s_{1}<s<s_{2}
,
p,p_{1},p_{2},q,q_{1},q_{2}\in[1,\infty]
and
\theta\in(0,1)
satisfy

s=\theta s_{1}+(1-\theta)s_{2},\quad\frac{1}{p}=\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}+\frac{1-\theta}{p_{2}},\quad\frac{1}{q}=\frac{\theta}{q_{1}}+\frac{1-\theta}{q_{2}}.

(A.4)

Then there exists
C>0
such that

\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,q}}\leq C\|f\|_{B^{s_{1}}_{p_{1},q_{1}}}^{\theta}\|f\|_{B^{s_{2}}_{p_{2},q_{2}}}^{1-\theta}.

(A.5)

(iv)

Bernstein inequalities. Let
R>0
and let
B_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(R)=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:|n|\leq R\}
. Let
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
s_{1}<s_{2}
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
. There exists
C>0
such that

\|f\|_{B^{s_{2}}_{p,q}}\leq CR^{s_{2}-s_{1}}\|f\|_{B^{s_{1}}_{p,q}},\qquad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

(A.6)

and

\|g\|_{B^{s_{1}}_{p,q}}\leq CR^{s_{1}-s_{2}}\|g\|_{B^{s_{2}}_{p,q}},

(A.7)

for
f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{F}f)\subset B_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(R)
and
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{F}g)\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus B_{f}(R)
.

A.2
Para and resonant products

We now introduce para and resonant products following
[
BCD11
, Section 2.8]
. Let
f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Define the paraproduct

f\succ g=\sum_{l<k-1}\Delta_{k}f\Delta_{l}g

(A.8)

and the resonant product

f\circ g=\sum_{|k-l|\leq 1}\Delta_{k}f\Delta_{l}g.

(A.9)

We also write
f\prec g
interchangeably for
g\succ f
. We thus have Bony’s decomposition

fg=f\prec g+f\circ g+f\succ g.

(A.10)

The following para and resonant product estimates are standard.

Proposition A.2
.

The following estimates hold.

(i)

Paraproduct estimate.
Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,p_{1},p_{2},q\in[1,\infty]
be such that
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}
. There exists
C>0
such that

\|f\succ g\|_{B^{s}_{p,q}}\leq C\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p_{1},q}}\|g\|_{L^{p_{2}}},\qquad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

(A.11)

(ii)

Resonant product estimate.
Let
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
s=s_{1}+s_{2}>0
. Let
p,p_{1},p_{2},q\in[1,\infty]
satisfy
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}
. There exists
C>0
such that

\|f\circ g\|_{B^{s}_{p,q}}\leq C\|f\|_{B^{s_{1}}_{p_{1},\infty}}\|g\|_{B^{s_{2}}_{p_{2},q}},\qquad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

(A.12)

Appendix B
Bulk covariance estimates

B.1
Interpolation with cutoff scale
T
